# Daily Summary: 2025-05-24

### MuseRAG: Idea Originality Scoring At Scale
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16232v1)
- **Authors**: Ali Sarosh Bangash, Krish Veera, Ishfat Abrar Islam, Raiyan Abdul Baten
- **Abstract**: An objective, face-valid way to assess the originality of creative ideas is to measure how rare each idea is within a population -- an approach long used in creativity research but difficult to automate at scale. Tabulating response frequencies via manual bucketing of idea rephrasings is labor-intensive, error-prone, and brittle under large corpora. We introduce a fully automated, psychometrically validated pipeline for frequency-based originality scoring. Our method, MuseRAG, combines large language models (LLMs) with an externally orchestrated retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework. Given a new idea, the system retrieves semantically similar prior idea buckets and zero-shot prompts the LLM to judge whether the new idea belongs to an existing bucket or forms a new one. The resulting buckets enable computation of frequency-based originality metrics. Across five datasets (N=1143, n_ideas=16294), MuseRAG matches human annotators in idea clustering structure and resolution (AMI = 0.59) and in participant-level scoring (r = 0.89) -- while exhibiting strong convergent and external validity. Our work enables intent-sensitive, human-aligned originality scoring at scale to aid creativity research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MUSERAG, a fully automated pipeline for frequency-based originality scoring of creative ideas.  MUSERAG leverages large language models (LLMs) in a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework. Given an idea, the system retrieves semantically similar existing ideas and prompts the LLM to determine if the new idea belongs to an existing bucket or warrants a new one. This process enables the computation of frequency-based originality metrics.  The authors demonstrate that MUSERAG achieves high agreement with human annotators in both idea clustering structure and participant-level scoring across several datasets. They also show convergent and external validity. The key contribution is automating a previously labor-intensive and subjective process, enabling originality scoring at scale.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (Score: 7.5):**  The primary novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques into a cohesive, automated pipeline validated for a specific, challenging task. LLMs have been used for various NLP tasks, including text clustering and generation. The RAG framework is also a known approach.  The *novelty* here stems from the *specific application* of LLMs and RAG to automating frequency-based originality scoring, a task traditionally reliant on human annotation. The paper overcomes significant challenges in automating this task, such as handling fat-tailed distributions of idea buckets and ensuring intent-sensitive clustering. While the components aren't entirely new, their orchestration and validation for this particular problem space are novel and valuable.

*   **Significance (Score: 8):** The significance is strong for several reasons:
    *   **Automation and Scalability:** Automating originality scoring addresses a major bottleneck in creativity research, allowing for larger-scale studies and more efficient analysis of creative outputs. This is a substantial step forward.
    *   **Psychometric Validation:** The rigorous psychometric validation (demonstrating construct, convergent, and external validity) strengthens the credibility of the automated approach. This is crucial for its adoption by researchers. Showing that MUSERAG *aligns* with human judgments is important as it increases confidence that the approach is reliable and capturing something meaningful.
    *   **Accessibility:** The authors made the codebase available, enhancing accessibility for other researchers.
    *   **Interdisciplinary Impact:** While primarily targeting creativity research, the methods and findings may have implications for other areas, such as innovation management, design thinking, and automated assessment of open-ended responses in education.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed and carefully implemented pipeline.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation using multiple datasets and metrics.
    *   Strong evidence of validity and reliability.
    *   Thorough analysis of human annotation characteristics.
    *   Open-source code.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The system's performance is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM.  Future LLM limitations or biases could impact the system's effectiveness and the generalizability of its assessment.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Sensitivity:** Although the authors addressed the prompt engineering issue, prompt length and phrasing can still impact the performance. Further work is needed to analyze the system's overall sensitivity.
    *   **Limited Domain:** The evaluation is restricted to text-based divergent thinking tasks. Generalization to other creative domains is uncertain.  The paper acknowledges this as a limitation.
    *  **Potential for Bias:** Although mentioned, there should be more information on how the model ensures fair demographical and cultural considerations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by:
    *   Providing a practical and validated tool for originality scoring.
    *   Encouraging more large-scale studies of creativity.
    *   Stimulating further research on automated creativity assessment methods.
    *   Serving as a benchmark for future systems.

**Justification:**

MUSERAG demonstrates a valuable advance by automating frequency-based originality scoring and aligning it to existing human annotators, enabling larger-scale creativity studies with more efficient analysis of creative outputs. Overall, its novel application of components, strong validations, and addressing a vital bottleneck allows it to make a solid contribution to the field, deserving the assigned score.

### LIFEBench: Evaluating Length Instruction Following in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16234v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Zhang, Zhenhong Zhou, Junfeng Fang, Rongwu Xu, Kun Wang, Yuanhe Zhang, Rui Wang, Ge Zhang, Xinfeng Li, Li Sun, Lingjuan Lyu, Yang Liu, Sen Su
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) can solve PhD-level reasoning problems over long context inputs, they still struggle with a seemingly simpler task: following explicit length instructions-e.g., write a 10,000-word novel. Additionally, models often generate far too short outputs, terminate prematurely, or even refuse the request. Existing benchmarks focus primarily on evaluating generations quality, but often overlook whether the generations meet length constraints. To this end, we introduce Length Instruction Following Evaluation Benchmark (LIFEBench) to comprehensively evaluate LLMs' ability to follow length instructions across diverse tasks and a wide range of specified lengths. LIFEBench consists of 10,800 instances across 4 task categories in both English and Chinese, covering length constraints ranging from 16 to 8192 words. We evaluate 26 widely-used LLMs and find that most models reasonably follow short-length instructions but deteriorate sharply beyond a certain threshold. Surprisingly, almost all models fail to reach the vendor-claimed maximum output lengths in practice, as further confirmed by our evaluations extending up to 32K words. Even long-context LLMs, despite their extended input-output windows, counterintuitively fail to improve length-instructions following. Notably, Reasoning LLMs outperform even specialized long-text generation models, achieving state-of-the-art length following. Overall, LIFEBench uncovers fundamental limitations in current LLMs' length instructions following ability, offering critical insights for future progress.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LIFEBENCH: Evaluating Length Instruction Following in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LIFEBENCH, a new benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to follow explicit length instructions in text generation tasks. The benchmark includes 10,800 instances across four diverse task categories (Question Answering, Summarization, Reasoning, and Creative Generation) in both English and Chinese. The study evaluates 26 widely-used LLMs and finds that most models struggle with following length instructions, particularly as the specified length increases. The benchmark reveals several insights, including that models often generate outputs that are too short, terminate prematurely, or even refuse to generate when instructed to produce long texts. Even long-context LLMs do not necessarily improve length-following, and reasoning LLMs tend to outperform specialized long-text generation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** LIFEBENCH fills a significant gap in the LLM evaluation landscape by providing a rigorous benchmark specifically focused on length instruction following, an often overlooked but critical aspect of LLM performance.

    *   **Diverse Tasks and Languages:** The benchmark's inclusion of diverse tasks and both English and Chinese instances increases its generalizability and relevance for real-world applications.

    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper offers valuable insights into the factors influencing LLMs' ability to follow length instructions, such as task type, language, and input length, which can inform future research and development efforts.

    *   **Identification of Limitations:** The study highlights fundamental limitations in current LLMs' ability to follow length instructions, offering actionable insights for future progress.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope of Analysis:** While the paper identifies limitations, it may lack specific solutions or mechanisms to overcome the observed shortcomings in LLMs' length instruction following capabilities.

    *   **Generalizability to Other Models:** Although the study evaluates 26 LLMs, it might not be fully representative of all existing models, especially newer or more specialized LLMs.

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Problem Generation:** The study uses GPT-4 to generate new reasoning problems, potentially introducing biases into the dataset, although the manual review may mitigate these issues.

    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Task Design:** The tasks themselves, while diverse, are based on existing benchmarks. The main novelty lies in the combination of the tasks and the focus on length following as the primary evaluation metric.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The primary novelty lies in the creation of a **comprehensive and targeted benchmark**, LIFEBENCH, designed specifically to assess LLMs' ability to follow explicit length instructions. This is a significant contribution, as existing benchmarks often focus on quality but overlook length constraints.
    *   The study **uncovers fundamental limitations** in current LLMs' length instruction following ability, including the fact that models cannot reliably approach their vendor-claimed maximum output length and that many models cannot accurately recognize how many words they have generated.
    *   The paper offers critical insights for future progress in LLM development by **identifying key factors** impacting length instruction fidelity, such as task type, language, and input length.
    *   The benchmark can serve as a valuable tool for the community to **develop and evaluate new techniques** for improving length-instruction following in LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence on the Field:**

    *   The benchmark has the potential to **shift the focus of LLM evaluation** toward a more comprehensive approach that considers not only quality but also adherence to constraints.
    *   The insights generated by this study can **inform the design and training of future LLMs**, leading to models that are better able to generate outputs of the desired length.
    *   The benchmark can serve as a **valuable tool for comparing and evaluating the effectiveness of different length-control techniques** in LLMs.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**. The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by introducing a comprehensive and rigorously analyzed benchmark for evaluating length instruction following in LLMs. The novelty lies primarily in the creation of the benchmark itself and in the insights it provides into the limitations of current models. While the study does not offer specific solutions to the observed problems, it sets the stage for future research and development efforts in this area. The comprehensive evaluation and valuable insights into factors affecting length instruction fidelity, such as task type, language, and input length, and its ability to serve as a valuable tool make it a significant contribution to the field.

### Align-GRAG: Reasoning-Guided Dual Alignment for Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16237v1)
- **Authors**: Derong Xu, Pengyue Jia, Xiaopeng Li, Yingyi Zhang, Maolin Wang, Qidong Liu, Xiangyu Zhao, Yichao Wang, Huifeng Guo, Ruiming Tang, Enhong Chen, Tong Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, but still struggle with issues like hallucinations and outdated information. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) addresses these issues by grounding LLM outputs in external knowledge with an Information Retrieval (IR) system. Building on this foundation, graph-based RAG systems go a step further by retrieving subgraphs, which preserve the relationships between knowledge entities and provide more comprehensive context. However, graph RAG faces two challenges: (1) Retrieving relevant information introduces irrelevant nodes (especially in dense graph databases, where retrieval usually extends to adjacent nodes), and leads to overly lengthy inputs that hinder efficiency; (2) The representation gap between graph and language during generation with LLMs limits the ability to fully leverage graph structures for enhanced understanding. To address these limitations, we propose Align-GRAG, a novel reasoning-guided dual alignment framework in post-retrieval phrase. It first formulates a subgraph by retrieving nodes and edges. Then an Aligner is proposed to jointly optimizes a graph encoder with LLM-summarized reasoning. It achieves dual alignment of graph node and representation by leveraging KL divergence loss and contrastive loss, facilitating efficient pruning of irrelevant knowledge and establishing a unified semantic space. The Generator integrates the aligned graph data with LLM to produce coherent and accurate answers. Experiments on GraphQA benchmark across three tasks (including common sense reasoning, scene graph understanding, and knowledge graph reasoning) validate the effectiveness of our method. The code will be available upon accepted.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Align-GRAG: Reasoning-Guided Dual Alignment for Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Align-GRAG, a novel framework designed to enhance graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GRAG) systems.  It addresses two key challenges in GRAG: (1) the inclusion of irrelevant nodes during subgraph retrieval, and (2) the representation gap between graph embeddings and the language models (LLMs) used for generation. Align-GRAG operates in a post-retrieval phase.  It leverages an LLM to summarize reasoning chains bridging the question and answer and then uses these summaries as a signal to align a graph encoder. This alignment is performed in two stages:  *Graph Node Alignment*, which prunes irrelevant nodes by ranking them based on the reasoning chain, and *Graph Representation Alignment*, which uses contrastive learning to create a unified semantic space between graph and language representations. The refined graph and query are then fed into an LLM to generate the final answer. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on the GraphQA benchmark, showing improved performance across several tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a compelling and novel approach to GRAG.  The core idea of using LLM-generated reasoning chains to guide the alignment of graph representations is innovative. The dual alignment strategy, focusing on both node relevance and representation space unification, is well-structured.  While existing works have attempted to integrate LLMs and graph data, Align-GRAG's use of LLM-derived *reasoning* as a critical training signal is a distinguishing feature.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the accuracy, efficiency, and contextual awareness of GRAG systems.  By pruning irrelevant nodes, the framework reduces computational overhead and focuses the LLM's attention on the most relevant information.  By aligning the graph and language representations, it allows the LLM to better leverage the structural information captured in the graph. The experimental results on the GraphQA benchmark support these claims.  GRAG is becoming increasingly important as more data is stored in graph formats and as LLMs struggle with knowledge-intensive tasks, so improvements in this space are valuable to the field.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing GRAG approaches.
*   **Novel Approach:** The use of LLM-derived reasoning chains for graph alignment is a valuable contribution.
*   **Dual Alignment Strategy:** The separation into node and representation alignment is well-defined and effective.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experiments on the GraphQA benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of the framework.
*   **Generalization Analysis:** The paper shows that Align-GRAG works with different GNNs and LLMs backbones.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The framework's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the reasoning chains generated by the LLM. If the reasoning is flawed, it could lead to incorrect node ranking and suboptimal alignment. The paper acknowledges this dependency, but more analysis on the impact of LLM hallucination on the generated reasoning chains would be beneficial.
*   **Limited LLM Scale Evaluation**: The paper mainly used Llama2-7B or 13B. While these models are great, it is not clear how this method would scale for extremely large models in terms of retrieval quality.
*   **Closed source implications:** Since the method relies on generating graph embeddings, it might not be directly implemented on close-source models like GPT-4.
*   **Experiment Dataset Limitations:** GraphQA is a well-established benchmark, but it would be beneficial to evaluate the framework on a broader range of datasets, particularly those that better represent real-world knowledge graphs, to fully assess its generalizability.
*   **Efficiency Trade-off:** The reliance on an initial LLM summarization step introduces a computational overhead. While the node pruning improves efficiency in later stages, the initial summarization cost should be considered. The experiments discuss the efficiency of node pruning, but do not fully account for the additional summarization step.

**Potential Influence:**

Align-GRAG has the potential to significantly influence the development of future GRAG systems.  The reasoning-guided alignment strategy offers a promising direction for improving the integration of LLMs and graph data. The techniques proposed in this paper could be adopted and extended by other researchers working in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and effective approach to address a significant challenge in GRAG, with strong experimental support. While the reliance on reasoning chain quality and the experiment's limitation hold the paper back from being a truly exceptional contribution, its innovation, potential impact, and solid results warrant a high score.

### DOVE: Efficient One-Step Diffusion Model for Real-World Video Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16239v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Chen, Zichen Zou, Kewei Zhang, Xiongfei Su, Xin Yuan, Yong Guo, Yulun Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated promising performance in real-world video super-resolution (VSR). However, the dozens of sampling steps they require, make inference extremely slow. Sampling acceleration techniques, particularly single-step, provide a potential solution. Nonetheless, achieving one step in VSR remains challenging, due to the high training overhead on video data and stringent fidelity demands. To tackle the above issues, we propose DOVE, an efficient one-step diffusion model for real-world VSR. DOVE is obtained by fine-tuning a pretrained video diffusion model (*i.e.*, CogVideoX). To effectively train DOVE, we introduce the latent-pixel training strategy. The strategy employs a two-stage scheme to gradually adapt the model to the video super-resolution task. Meanwhile, we design a video processing pipeline to construct a high-quality dataset tailored for VSR, termed HQ-VSR. Fine-tuning on this dataset further enhances the restoration capability of DOVE. Extensive experiments show that DOVE exhibits comparable or superior performance to multi-step diffusion-based VSR methods. It also offers outstanding inference efficiency, achieving up to a **28$\times$** speed-up over existing methods such as MGLD-VSR. Code is available at: https://github.com/zhengchen1999/DOVE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DOVE, a novel and efficient one-step diffusion model designed for real-world video super-resolution (VSR). It addresses the main limitation of existing diffusion-based VSR methods, which is their slow inference speed due to the need for multiple sampling steps. DOVE is built by fine-tuning a pretrained video diffusion model (CogVideoX) and incorporates a latent-pixel training strategy to effectively adapt the model for VSR. This training strategy involves two stages: adaptation in the latent space and refinement in the pixel space. The authors also construct a high-quality video dataset, HQ-VSR, tailored for VSR using a systematic video processing pipeline. Experimental results demonstrate that DOVE achieves comparable or superior performance compared to multi-step diffusion-based VSR methods while offering significant speedups (up to 28x).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of a one-step diffusion model to the video super-resolution task. While one-step diffusion models have been explored in image SR, their direct application to VSR is challenging due to the increased computational cost and fidelity requirements. The paper's latent-pixel training strategy is also a novel adaptation method for video. Constructing a dedicated high-quality training dataset for VSR is also a valuable contribution. The choice of regression loss over adversarial training is also a novel strategy to achieve good VSR performance, considering the issues that come with adversarial training.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its ability to drastically improve the inference efficiency of diffusion-based VSR without sacrificing performance. This makes diffusion models more practical for real-world applications where speed is crucial.  The HQ-VSR dataset will likely be a valuable resource for future research in VSR. The training strategy is well-motivated by addressing training stability and memory requirements. The performance compared to other state-of-the-art methods proves the model's significance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Significant Speedup:** The demonstrated 28x speedup compared to MGLD-VSR is a major achievement.
    * **Comparable/Superior Performance:** Maintaining competitive or better performance while being significantly faster is a strong selling point.
    * **Well-Designed Training Strategy:** The latent-pixel training approach is effective in adapting the pretrained model for VSR.
    * **High-Quality Dataset:** The HQ-VSR dataset addresses a gap in the availability of suitable training data for VSR.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its benefits.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Pretrained Model:** The method relies heavily on a strong pretrained video diffusion model (CogVideoX). It's less clear how DOVE would perform if starting from scratch or using a weaker pretrained model.
    * **Dataset Dependency:** The high-quality results are at least partially enabled by the specifically curated HQ-VSR dataset. Performance might degrade on other, less well-curated datasets.
    * **Limited Ablation Study:** While the ablation study explores aspects of the training strategy, it could benefit from additional analyses, for instance, varying the timestep t in Eq. (2). Although the explanation provided is reasonable, experimental evidence would reinforce the selection of 399 as the t value.
    * **Limited Scope of Comparison:** Though the evaluation involves numerous metrics and datasets, more recent single-step techniques from image SR could've been adapted and included to strengthen the comparison.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in VSR by demonstrating the feasibility of one-step diffusion models for this task. It may also encourage the development of more efficient training strategies and specialized datasets for VSR.

* **Justification for Score:**  While the paper builds upon existing work in diffusion models and SR, the specific combination of one-step inference, latent-pixel training, and a high-quality dataset for VSR makes a significant contribution. The substantial speedup achieved without sacrificing performance is a compelling result.  The weaknesses are acknowledged, but the overall impact is substantial.

### Three Minds, One Legend: Jailbreak Large Reasoning Model with Adaptive Stacked Ciphers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16241v1)
- **Authors**: Viet-Anh Nguyen, Shiqian Zhao, Gia Dao, Runyi Hu, Yi Xie, Luu Anh Tuan
- **Abstract**: Recently, Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated superior logical capabilities compared to traditional Large Language Models (LLMs), gaining significant attention. Despite their impressive performance, the potential for stronger reasoning abilities to introduce more severe security vulnerabilities remains largely underexplored. Existing jailbreak methods often struggle to balance effectiveness with robustness against adaptive safety mechanisms. In this work, we propose SEAL, a novel jailbreak attack that targets LRMs through an adaptive encryption pipeline designed to override their reasoning processes and evade potential adaptive alignment. Specifically, SEAL introduces a stacked encryption approach that combines multiple ciphers to overwhelm the models reasoning capabilities, effectively bypassing built-in safety mechanisms. To further prevent LRMs from developing countermeasures, we incorporate two dynamic strategies - random and adaptive - that adjust the cipher length, order, and combination. Extensive experiments on real-world reasoning models, including DeepSeek-R1, Claude Sonnet, and OpenAI GPT-o4, validate the effectiveness of our approach. Notably, SEAL achieves an attack success rate of 80.8% on GPT o4-mini, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines by a significant margin of 27.2%. Warning: This paper contains examples of inappropriate, offensive, and harmful content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Three Minds, One Legend: Jailbreak Large Reasoning Model with Adaptive Stacked Ciphers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEAL, a novel jailbreak attack targeting Large Reasoning Models (LRMs).  It leverages an adaptive encryption pipeline, specifically stacked ciphers, to overwhelm the reasoning processes of LRMs and evade adaptive safety mechanisms.  The core idea is to obfuscate unsafe intentions within multiple layers of encryption that LRMs can process on the surface, but are prevented from reaching the real (unsafe) objective. To dynamically make the system more robust, the research introduces random and reinforcement learning-based sampling strategies to adjust cipher length, order, and combination. Extensive experiments on models like DeepSeek-R1, Claude Sonnet, and GPT-4 demonstrate SEAL's effectiveness, achieving significantly higher attack success rates compared to existing methods, including transferability to different models. The paper argues that while LRMs exhibit improved reasoning capabilities, this can ironically introduce new vulnerabilities that traditional LLM jailbreak attacks fail to exploit.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to jailbreaking LRMs. The use of stacked adaptive ciphers *specifically tailored* to target the reasoning capabilities of LRMs is a step forward compared to transfer attacks from conventional LLMs. The integration of reinforcement learning to dynamically adjust the encryption strategy represents a noteworthy attempt to address adaptive defense mechanisms. Most of the existing works directly attack LLMs, but SEAL focuses on stronger, recently emerging LRMs.

* **Significance:**  The significance lies in highlighting a subtle but critical vulnerability in LRMs.  The paper demonstrates that *increased reasoning abilities don't necessarily equate to improved safety*; instead, they can open up new attack vectors.  This finding has important implications for the development and deployment of LRMs. By exposing this weakness, the paper can contribute to safer AI alignment.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across a range of LRM models.  The performance gains over existing jailbreak methods are significant, lending credibility to the effectiveness of SEAL.
    * **Adaptive Mechanism:** The adaptive nature of the encryption strategy is a crucial strength. It attempts to address a key challenge in jailbreaking: the ongoing arms race between attacks and defenses.
    * **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing jailbreak techniques when applied to LRMs and motivates the need for a different approach.
    * **Transferability Results:** Transferability results are included to add value to SEAL in different scenarios and models.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** The reinforcement learning component of SEAL introduces computational overhead. The paper could have benefited from a more thorough discussion of the practical implications of this overhead.  Additionally, the paper does not disclose the cost for conducting such large-scale experiments, which should be stated to add more value to the experiment section.
    * **Limited Exploration of RL Algorithms:** While the paper uses a gradient bandit algorithm, it acknowledges that other RL techniques could be explored.  A more detailed justification for choosing the gradient bandit algorithm, or a brief comparison to alternative RL approaches, would strengthen the argument.
    * **Dependency on GPT-4 as a Judge:**  Relying on GPT-4 as a judge introduces potential biases. While the authors address this by manually reviewing responses, it would have been better to have a more robust evaluation methodology which is truly unbiased.
    * **Generalizability of Safety Mechanism:** While the ciphers are dynamically selected, it remains unclear how the approach will generalize to future safety mechanisms. The RL process may eventually become too costly as new safety measures emerge.

* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research on LRM safety and alignment.  It emphasizes the importance of considering the reasoning capabilities of models when designing defense strategies. Future work could build upon SEAL by exploring more sophisticated RL algorithms, adaptive defense mechanisms that proactively counter stacked encryption, and more robust evaluation metrics which are not simply judged by GPT4.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by demonstrating a novel jailbreak attack that specifically targets the vulnerabilities of LRMs. The adaptive nature of the attack and the solid empirical evidence support its effectiveness. However, the computational cost, limited exploration of RL algorithms, and reliance on GPT-4 as a judge, and lack of evaluation against adaptive countermeasures temper the overall impact. In reality, future LLMs and LRMs might proactively tackle the issue of decoding stacked or complex ciphers, and the method may become obsolete quickly. Also, the idea of using stacked ciphers on prompts can be easily blocked, as the patterns and complexity of prompts are still limited by nature.

### Does Localization Inform Unlearning? A Rigorous Examination of Local Parameter Attribution for Knowledge Unlearning in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16252v1)
- **Authors**: Hwiyeong Lee, Uiji Hwang, Hyelim Lim, Taeuk Kim
- **Abstract**: Large language models often retain unintended content, prompting growing interest in knowledge unlearning. Recent approaches emphasize localized unlearning, which restricts parameter updates to specific regions in an effort to remove target knowledge while preserving unrelated general knowledge. However, their effectiveness remains uncertain due to the lack of robust and thorough evaluation of the trade-off between the competing goals of unlearning. In this paper, we begin by revisiting existing localized unlearning approaches. We then conduct controlled experiments to rigorously evaluate whether local parameter updates causally contribute to unlearning. Our findings reveal that the set of parameters that must be modified for effective unlearning is not strictly determined, challenging the core assumption of localized unlearning that parameter locality is inherently indicative of effective knowledge removal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper rigorously examines the effectiveness of localized parameter attribution in knowledge unlearning for language models. The authors revisit existing localized unlearning approaches and conduct controlled experiments where the "ground truth" parameter regions responsible for storing the target knowledge are explicitly known. Contrary to the core assumption of localized unlearning, their findings reveal that modifying parameters within the ground truth region does not necessarily lead to a better trade-off between forgetting and retention. They suggest that the model may achieve ideal unlearning through flexible parameter adaptation, challenging the traditional view of unlearning as a full parameter recovery process that relies on the updating of a fixed set of parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The controlled experiments are a significant strength.  By explicitly knowing the target parameter region, the authors eliminate the confounding factor of imperfect localization and directly test the underlying hypothesis of localized unlearning.
    *   **Challenging Established Assumptions:** The paper directly confronts a widely held belief in the unlearning community—that parameter locality is inherently indicative of effective knowledge removal. The results challenge the core assumption and open a new direction for unlearning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors adopt a robust evaluation framework using metrics beyond the traditional surface-level measures, offering a more reliable assessment of knowledge unlearning.  The use of AUES and MU95 addresses limitations in previous evaluation strategies.
    *   **Model Mixing Technique:** Utilizing a model mixing method provides an easy way to control the unlearning intensity and create the smooth FS-RS Curve, which is critical for comprehensively evaluating a trade-off between forgetting and retaining.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The analysis is primarily focused on MLPs within Transformer-based LLMs and does not consider other components like attention layers. Additionally, experiments use the TOFU dataset, which is synthetic, raising questions about generalizability to real-world scenarios with more complex and nuanced knowledge representations.
    *   **Simplification:** While the controlled experiment is a strength, the simplification of directly manipulating parameters may not fully reflect the complexities of real-world unlearning scenarios where indirect supervision is more common.
    *   **Lack of a Concrete Alternative:** While the paper successfully challenges localized unlearning, it does not provide a clear alternative strategy for determining which parameters should be modified during unlearning.
    *   **The 10% value vector experiments might not represent the overall characteristics:** Selecting 10% of value vectors may exclude some valuable vectors or include noisy vectors, which potentially skews the experimental results.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper’s novelty lies in its systematic and critical examination of a fundamental assumption in the field of knowledge unlearning. By decoupling localization accuracy from unlearning effectiveness, the authors shed light on the limitations of current localized unlearning approaches.
    *   The findings have significant implications for the direction of future research in knowledge unlearning. They suggest that alternative strategies beyond strict parameter locality may be more effective for achieving ideal unlearning.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates good rigor through its controlled experiments and sound evaluation framework that challenges an underlying assumption of localized knowledge unlearning methods. This insight can guide future research towards better unlearning methods, although the paper’s limited scope regarding parameter spaces and datasets prevents it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DualComp: End-to-End Learning of a Unified Dual-Modality Lossless Compressor
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16256v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Zhao, Zhengxue Cheng, Junxuan Zhang, Qunshan Gu, Qi Wang, Li Song
- **Abstract**: Most learning-based lossless compressors are designed for a single modality, requiring separate models for multi-modal data and lacking flexibility. However, different modalities vary significantly in format and statistical properties, making it ineffective to use compressors that lack modality-specific adaptations. While multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) offer a potential solution for modality-unified compression, their excessive complexity hinders practical deployment. To address these challenges, we focus on the two most common modalities, image and text, and propose DualComp, the first unified and lightweight learning-based dual-modality lossless compressor. Built on a lightweight backbone, DualComp incorporates three key structural enhancements to handle modality heterogeneity: modality-unified tokenization, modality-switching contextual learning, and modality-routing mixture-of-experts. A reparameterization training strategy is also used to boost compression performance. DualComp integrates both modality-specific and shared parameters for efficient parameter utilization, enabling near real-time inference (200KB/s) on desktop CPUs. With much fewer parameters, DualComp achieves compression performance on par with the SOTA LLM-based methods for both text and image datasets. Its simplified single-modality variant surpasses the previous best image compressor on the Kodak dataset by about 9% using just 1.2% of the model size.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DualComp paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DualComp, a novel, unified, and lightweight lossless compressor designed for both image and text data. Existing lossless compression methods are typically modality-specific, requiring separate models for different data types and lacking flexibility. DualComp addresses this limitation by leveraging a lightweight backbone (RWKV-7) and incorporating three key structural enhancements: modality-unified tokenization, modality-switching contextual learning, and modality-routing mixture-of-experts (MoE). A reparameterization training strategy is also used to improve compression performance without increasing inference complexity. The resulting model demonstrates competitive compression performance on both image and text datasets, achieving near real-time inference on desktop CPUs with significantly fewer parameters compared to large language model (LLM)-based methods. A simplified single-modality variant, DualComp-I, achieves state-of-the-art image compression performance on the Kodak dataset with a very small model size.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects:

*   **Unified Dual-Modality Compression:** To the best of my knowledge, DualComp is indeed the first learning-based lossless compressor that explicitly addresses modality heterogeneity (image and text) within a single framework.  This contrasts with existing methods that either support only one modality or treat different modalities uniformly, neglecting their distinct characteristics.
*   **Lightweight Design:** The paper makes a substantial contribution by demonstrating that competitive compression performance can be achieved with a significantly smaller model compared to LLM-based approaches. The carefully designed structural enhancements and the reparameterization training strategy contribute to efficient parameter utilization. The choice of RWKV as backbone is very appropriate and justified well by its advantageous trade-off between speed and efficiency. The ablation study in Table 3 is effective in validating the approach, showing how performance increases by each of the proposed modules.
*   **Practicality:** The focus on near real-time inference on desktop CPUs is a significant strength. This makes DualComp potentially practical for a wider range of applications compared to computationally expensive LLM-based compressors.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to:

*   **Simplify Multi-Modal Compression:** By providing a unified framework for compressing different modalities, DualComp can reduce system complexity and deployment costs in applications involving both image and text data.
*   **Enable Resource-Constrained Applications:** The lightweight design and near real-time inference capabilities of DualComp make it suitable for resource-constrained devices and applications where computational resources are limited.
*   **Provide a New Direction for Lossless Compression Research:** The paper's success in combining a lightweight backbone with modality-specific adaptations opens up a new avenue for research in lossless compression, potentially leading to more efficient and practical compressors for various data types.
*   **Comprehensive Experimental Results:** The authors are thorough with their experimentation and are fair in their comparisons. They did a good job showing the compression efficiency of the proposed method and included speed results for multiple hardware platforms.

**Weaknesses and Limitations:**

*   **Limited Modalities:**  The current implementation focuses only on image and text data. While these are two common modalities, extending the framework to other data types (e.g., audio, video, 3D data) would further enhance its versatility. The results reported on CLIC benchmarks in Figure 9 show a slight fall-off compared to state of the art techniques which is partially mitigated by having a simpler model.
*   **Training Data and Generalization:** The training set consists of 5,500 images from ImageNet and 100MB of text from enwik8. While this may be sufficient for demonstrating the feasibility of the approach, it is important to evaluate the model's performance on more diverse and larger datasets to assess its generalization capabilities.
*   **Parameter Allocation:**  As the authors acknowledge, the current model allocates equal parameters to both modalities for simplicity.  Exploring more efficient allocation strategies that better account for the compression difficulty of each modality could further improve performance.

**Score Rationale:**

Overall, the paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of lossless compression.  The unified dual-modality approach, the lightweight design, and the focus on practical inference make DualComp a promising alternative to existing methods. While there are some limitations, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The combination of the different techniques and structural modifications are well-justified and validated with rigorous experimental work.

**Score: 8**

The score reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and practicality, while also acknowledging its limitations. It is a substantial advancement, but there are opportunities for future work to address the limitations and further improve its performance and versatility.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### IRONIC: Coherence-Aware Reasoning Chains for Multi-Modal Sarcasm Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16258v1)
- **Authors**: Aashish Anantha Ramakrishnan, Aadarsh Anantha Ramakrishnan, Dongwon Lee
- **Abstract**: Interpreting figurative language such as sarcasm across multi-modal inputs presents unique challenges, often requiring task-specific fine-tuning and extensive reasoning steps. However, current Chain-of-Thought approaches do not efficiently leverage the same cognitive processes that enable humans to identify sarcasm. We present IRONIC, an in-context learning framework that leverages Multi-modal Coherence Relations to analyze referential, analogical and pragmatic image-text linkages. Our experiments show that IRONIC achieves state-of-the-art performance on zero-shot Multi-modal Sarcasm Detection across different baselines. This demonstrates the need for incorporating linguistic and cognitive insights into the design of multi-modal reasoning strategies. Our code is available at: https://github.com/aashish2000/IRONIC
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IRONIC, a novel in-context learning framework designed to improve multi-modal sarcasm detection by explicitly incorporating Multi-modal Coherence Relations (CRs).  IRONIC aims to address the limitations of existing Chain-of-Thought (CoT) approaches which often fail to leverage the complex semantic, pragmatic, and analogical cues necessary for sarcasm detection across image and text.  The framework consists of two main steps: (1) Relation Extraction, where the system identifies the most appropriate CR (Insertion, Concretization, Projection, Restatement, Extension) for a given image-text pair along with a rationale, and (2) Interpretative Reasoning, which uses the predicted CR, rationale, and task-specific instructions to determine if the sample contains multi-modal sarcasm. The authors evaluate IRONIC against state-of-the-art baselines, including Zero-Shot, Zero-Shot CoT, and S³ CoT (a multi-agent approach), on two datasets (MMSD2.0 and RedEval) using both proprietary (GPT-4o) and open-source models (InternVL3). The results demonstrate that IRONIC achieves state-of-the-art performance on zero-shot multi-modal sarcasm detection tasks, highlighting the importance of linguistic and cognitive insights in designing reasoning strategies for MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the explicit use of Multi-modal Coherence Relations as a structured reasoning pathway for multi-modal sarcasm detection.  While CoT approaches are not new, adapting CRs and integrating them into an in-context learning setting for this specific task is a significant contribution.  The paper bridges the gap between linguistic theory and MLLM reasoning, which is an important direction.  The idea of using a more structured approach rather than a free-form chain of thought is a useful contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in multi-modal understanding: discerning figurative language like sarcasm, which demands sophisticated reasoning.  The results convincingly show that IRONIC outperforms existing CoT baselines in zero-shot settings, implying a better generalization capability.  This has practical implications for improving sentiment analysis, social media monitoring, and human-computer interaction. The use of two datasets adds to the robustness of the evaluation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework.
    *   The methodology is well-defined and easy to follow.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive and include a variety of baselines and datasets.
    *   The results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of IRONIC.
    *   The analysis of why IRONIC works, especially in relation to model capabilities, is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the datasets are standard, they are still limited in scope. More diverse datasets, particularly in different languages and cultural contexts, would strengthen the results.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o as a primary model raises concerns about reproducibility for researchers without access.  Although InternVL3 is used, the performance is less conclusive, suggesting GPT-4o's capabilities are crucial.
    *   The manual selection of Coherence Relations could be a bottleneck in real-world applications.  An automated or learned approach for CR selection would improve scalability.
    *   There are no detailed hyperparameter configurations.
    *   More error analysis, especially analyzing when the models misclassify is necessary for future development.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore linguistic-inspired structures for improving MLLM reasoning. It highlights the limitations of relying solely on scaling and encourages the incorporation of cognitive insights. Future research may focus on automating CR selection, extending the framework to other figurative language tasks, and evaluating its effectiveness in low-resource settings.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to multi-modal sarcasm detection. The improvement over established baselines using CoT methods is significant. However, the reliance on GPT-4o limits reproducibility and the dataset scope is limited. The novelty and potential influence, balanced against these limitations, warrants a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LINEA: Fast and Accurate Line Detection Using Scalable Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16264v1)
- **Authors**: Sebastian Janampa, Marios Pattichis
- **Abstract**: Line detection is a basic digital image processing operation used by higher-level processing methods. Recently, transformer-based methods for line detection have proven to be more accurate than methods based on CNNs, at the expense of significantly lower inference speeds. As a result, video analysis methods that require low latencies cannot benefit from current transformer-based methods for line detection. In addition, current transformer-based models require pretraining attention mechanisms on large datasets (e.g., COCO or Object360). This paper develops a new transformer-based method that is significantly faster without requiring pretraining the attention mechanism on large datasets. We eliminate the need to pre-train the attention mechanism using a new mechanism, Deformable Line Attention (DLA). We use the term LINEA to refer to our new transformer-based method based on DLA. Extensive experiments show that LINEA is significantly faster and outperforms previous models on sAP in out-of-distribution dataset testing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LINEA: FAST AND ACCURATE LINE DETECTION USING SCALABLE TRANSFORMERS":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LINEA, a new transformer-based method for line segment detection that aims to improve both speed and accuracy compared to existing approaches.  LINEA utilizes a novel Deformable Line Attention (DLA) mechanism, which the authors claim eliminates the need for pre-training attention mechanisms on large datasets. The model architecture involves a hierarchical backbone, a hybrid encoder with GELAN modules, a query selection module, and a decoder with D-FINE heads. The authors demonstrate through experiments that LINEA models achieve faster inference times and competitive accuracy, especially in out-of-distribution testing, while requiring fewer FLOPs than other transformer-based line detectors. The experiments focus on the ShanghaiTech Wireframe and York Urban datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the Deformable Line Attention (DLA) mechanism, which allows the transformer model to avoid pre-training on massive datasets. This is significant because it addresses a major limitation of prior transformer-based methods in line detection. The application of a modified GELAN module and D-FINE heads is also potentially novel, but their specific contribution compared to the core DLA mechanism needs further dissection to ascertain how important they were to the outcome. The idea of adapting deformable attention for line segments (instead of general object detection) is also a reasonable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The importance of LINEA lies in its potential for real-time applications. Line detection is a fundamental task in computer vision, and faster, more efficient methods can benefit a variety of downstream tasks, including autonomous driving, SLAM, and augmented reality. Achieving high accuracy *without* requiring extensive pre-training opens up the possibility of training and deploying these models more easily and quickly. The out-of-distribution performance improvement on the YorkUrban dataset is promising, suggesting better generalization capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Speed:**  The demonstrated speed improvements (significantly lower latency and FLOPs) are a major strength, making it suitable for real-time applications.
    *   **Reduced Pre-training:** Eliminating the need for pre-training on large datasets is a significant advantage, lowering training costs and enabling easier adaptation to specific domains.
    *   **Out-of-Distribution Performance:** The competitive or improved performance on the YorkUrban dataset is a strong indicator of the method's robustness and generalization ability. The ablation studies support the importance of the components of the proposed method.
    *   **Clear Ablation Study:** A very clean ablation study is presented, showing the impact of each major modification.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Evaluation:** While the Wireframe dataset is common, reliance on only one training dataset limits the generalizability claims. Further evaluation on more diverse line detection datasets would strengthen the paper.

    *   **Incremental Improvements:** While DLA is novel, the overall architecture still relies on existing components like GELAN and D-FINE. The degree to which these contribute to the final performance relative to DLA needs greater clarification. How well is the performance of LINEA without these components?
    *   **Clarity on Implementation Details:** While the method is described, certain implementation specifics related to hyperparameter tuning and exact network structure need to be explicit and clear.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work is potentially influential because it directly addresses a practical bottleneck in transformer-based line detection (pre-training requirements) and demonstrates a pathway to more efficient and deployable models. Other researchers could build upon the DLA mechanism and integrate it into different line detection architectures or adapt it to other low-level vision tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7/10. The paper makes a solid contribution by introducing a new attention mechanism (DLA) that reduces the need for extensive pre-training and improves the inference speed of transformer-based line detection. The significant speed gains and the promising out-of-distribution results justify this rating. However, the reliance on existing components, the lack of evaluation on a more diverse set of datasets, and some ambiguity in the implementation details prevent a higher score. While novel and valuable, the novelty might not be *groundbreaking*, and more work is required to validate the approach's wide-ranging applicability. The paper's strength lies in providing a practical and efficient solution to a known problem, paving the way for real-time and resource-constrained line detection applications.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think-RM: Enabling Long-Horizon Reasoning in Generative Reward Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16265v1)
- **Authors**: Ilgee Hong, Changlong Yu, Liang Qiu, Weixiang Yan, Zhenghao Xu, Haoming Jiang, Qingru Zhang, Qin Lu, Xin Liu, Chao Zhang, Tuo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) has become a powerful post-training paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences. A core challenge in RLHF is constructing accurate reward signals, where the conventional Bradley-Terry reward models (BT RMs) often suffer from sensitivity to data size and coverage, as well as vulnerability to reward hacking. Generative reward models (GenRMs) offer a more robust alternative by generating chain-of-thought (CoT) rationales followed by a final reward. However, existing GenRMs rely on shallow, vertically scaled reasoning, limiting their capacity to handle nuanced or complex (e.g., reasoning-intensive) tasks. Moreover, their pairwise preference outputs are incompatible with standard RLHF algorithms that require pointwise reward signals. In this work, we introduce Think-RM, a training framework that enables long-horizon reasoning in GenRMs by modeling an internal thinking process. Rather than producing structured, externally provided rationales, Think-RM generates flexible, self-guided reasoning traces that support advanced capabilities such as self-reflection, hypothetical reasoning, and divergent reasoning. To elicit these reasoning abilities, we first warm-up the models by supervised fine-tuning (SFT) over long CoT data. We then further improve the model's long-horizon abilities by rule-based reinforcement learning (RL). In addition, we propose a novel pairwise RLHF pipeline that directly optimizes policies using pairwise preference rewards, eliminating the need for pointwise reward conversion and enabling more effective use of Think-RM outputs. Experiments show that Think-RM achieves state-of-the-art results on RM-Bench, outperforming both BT RM and vertically scaled GenRM by 8%. When combined with our pairwise RLHF pipeline, it demonstrates superior end-policy performance compared to traditional approaches.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think-RM: Enabling Long-Horizon Reasoning in Generative Reward Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Think-RM, a framework for improving the reasoning capabilities of Generative Reward Models (GenRMs) used in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF).  The key idea is to enable long-horizon reasoning within the GenRM itself, allowing it to model an internal thinking process. Think-RM is trained in two stages: (1) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on long, carefully selected chain-of-thought (CoT) data, and (2) rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to refine the reasoning process. The paper also proposes a novel pairwise RLHF pipeline that directly uses pairwise preference rewards from the GenRM, avoiding the need to convert them to pointwise rewards. The authors demonstrate that Think-RM outperforms existing GenRMs and Bradley-Terry reward models (BT RMs) on tasks requiring complex reasoning, particularly in out-of-distribution (OOD) settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions:

    *   The concept of training GenRMs for *long-horizon* reasoning is relatively unexplored and addresses a key limitation of existing GenRMs that rely on shallow reasoning.
    *   The two-stage training approach (SFT + rule-based RL) is effective in equipping non-reasoning LLMs with the capacity for more elaborate reasoning traces.
    *   The pairwise RLHF pipeline is a valuable innovation, aligning the reward model's output format with the RL training objective and simplifying the overall RLHF process. This is a significant methodological improvement.

    However, some components build upon existing techniques: SFT and RL are well-established methods in RLHF. The novelty primarily lies in *how* these methods are adapted and combined within the context of GenRMs. The idea of self reflection or iterative reasoning has been studied for some time, especially in models like the O series from OpenAI.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in RLHF: the construction of accurate and robust reward models. By improving the reasoning capabilities of GenRMs, Think-RM can potentially lead to better alignment between language models and human preferences, especially for complex tasks where nuances are important. The gains demonstrated on challenging benchmarks like RM-Bench and HelpSteer are substantial, signifying a real advance in the field. The pairwise RLHF pipeline also addresses a practical issue of reward format incompatibility and holds promise for more effective policy optimization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive and includes comparisons against strong baselines (BT RM, CoT-GenRMs) across a variety of tasks (ID, OOD).
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of the different components of Think-RM (e.g., SFT vs. SFT+RL, CoT filtering strategies).
    *   The code and data are publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the improvements are significant, the model still uses a lot of resources to train and implement such as 8 A100 GPUs. Training such models are not within the grasp of everyday researcher.
    *   The paper focuses on a relatively small base model (Llama-3-8B).  It would be interesting to see how Think-RM scales to larger models, as the benefits of long-horizon reasoning may become even more pronounced. However, the computational cost makes it difficult to experiment with bigger models.
    *   The RL portion of the training relies on hand-crafted rules for reward assignment. While effective, this approach might be brittle and require careful tuning for different tasks. It would be valuable to explore more automated or data-driven ways of defining the RL reward signal.
    *   The paper uses specific implementations from OpenRLHF [46] and VeRL [47], which may have their own nuances and limit generalization to different frameworks.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and training of reward models in RLHF. The long-horizon reasoning approach and the pairwise RLHF pipeline offer valuable directions for future research. It could motivate further exploration of methods for improving reasoning within GenRMs and simplifying the RLHF process.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the long-horizon reasoning approach, the significant improvements over strong baselines, and the introduction of a more effective pairwise RLHF pipeline, I assign a score of 7.

The paper provides a substantial contribution to the field by enhancing the reasoning abilities of GenRMs for RLHF and introducing a novel pairwise training pipeline. Although the core techniques (SFT, RL) are not entirely new, the way they are combined and adapted to address the long-horizon reasoning limitation in GenRMs is creative and demonstrably effective. The limitations, such as the reliance on hand-crafted rules and the scalability to larger models, somewhat detract from the overall impact, but the demonstrated improvements on challenging benchmarks justify a positive assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Transformer Copilot: Learning from The Mistake Log in LLM Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16270v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaru Zou, Yikun Ban, Zihao Li, Yunzhe Qi, Ruizhong Qiu, Ling Yang, Jingrui He
- **Abstract**: Large language models are typically adapted to downstream tasks through supervised fine-tuning on domain-specific data. While standard fine-tuning focuses on minimizing generation loss to optimize model parameters, we take a deeper step by retaining and leveraging the model's own learning signals, analogous to how human learners reflect on past mistakes to improve future performance. We first introduce the concept of Mistake Log to systematically track the model's learning behavior and recurring errors throughout fine-tuning. Treating the original transformer-based model as the Pilot, we correspondingly design a Copilot model to refine the Pilot's inference performance via logits rectification. We name the overall Pilot-Copilot framework the Transformer Copilot, which introduces (i) a novel Copilot model design, (ii) a joint training paradigm where the Copilot continuously learns from the evolving Mistake Log alongside the Pilot, and (iii) a fused inference paradigm where the Copilot rectifies the Pilot's logits for enhanced generation. We provide both theoretical and empirical analyses on our new learning framework. Experiments on 12 benchmarks spanning commonsense, arithmetic, and recommendation tasks demonstrate that Transformer Copilot consistently improves performance by up to 34.5%, while introducing marginal computational overhead to Pilot models and exhibiting strong scalability and transferability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Transformer Copilot: Learning from The Mistake Log in LLM Fine-tuning."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called "Transformer Copilot" designed to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Instead of solely focusing on minimizing generation loss, the framework retains and leverages the model's own learning signals by tracking "Mistake Logs". These logs systematically record the model's learning behavior, including input data, internal hidden states, and token-level errors throughout the fine-tuning process. A "Copilot" model is then trained to learn from these Mistake Logs and rectify the "Pilot" model's logits during inference, leading to enhanced generation. The key components include a new Copilot model architecture, a joint training paradigm, and a fused inference paradigm. Experiments across diverse tasks demonstrate improved performance with minimal computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of systematically tracking a model's learning behavior through "Mistake Logs" and using it to train a separate model to improve inference is a novel idea. While self-refinement methods exist, this work uniquely integrates the model's internal states and errors within the standard SFT paradigm to refine the *intermediate* reasoning process, not just post-hoc correction. Furthermore, the specific design of the Copilot model, the joint training strategy, and the fused inference process represent distinct contributions.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to bridge the gap between training and inference in LLMs. By leveraging internal learning signals, the framework addresses a key challenge: the suboptimal performance of fine-tuned LLMs caused by an inability to capture task-specific nuances or overfitting to training data. The experimental results, showing substantial performance improvements across multiple tasks and model sizes, suggest that the approach is effective. The efficiency (minimal overhead) and transferability aspects further enhance its practical value. The fact that a smaller pilot coupled with a transformer copilot can outperform larger models is quite valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem and Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of suboptimal inference performance in fine-tuned LLMs and provides a strong motivation for its proposed solution.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The Mistake Log and Copilot architecture are innovative and well-articulated.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough and cover a diverse range of tasks, model sizes, and architectures.
    *   **Efficiency and Transferability:** The demonstration of minimal computational overhead and transferability is crucial for real-world applicability.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** The inclusion of a theoretical guarantee (Theorem 4.1) adds rigor and helps explain the effectiveness of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Data Diversity:** While acknowledged, the limitation related to the Mistake Log's dependence on the richness and representativeness of the fine-tuning data needs further investigation. The performance in low-resource or domain-shifted scenarios requires further study.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** While comprehensive hyperparameters for the presented experiments were provided, the optimal performance hinges on appropriate hyperparameter selection that may require tuning. The optimal values depend heavily on the task.
    *   **Simplified Inference:** The rectified predicted distribution is obtained by adding linearly the Pilot output with the one from the Copilot model, which might not be always optimal.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore internal learning signals for LLM improvement. The Mistake Log concept and the Pilot-Copilot architecture could inspire new approaches to fine-tuning and inference. The findings on efficiency and transferability could also drive the development of more practical and adaptable LLMs.

*   **Justification for Score:** Considering the novelty of the approach, the significance of the problem addressed, the comprehensive evaluation, and the potential for future influence, but also acknowledging the limitations related to data diversity and parameter tuning, a score of 8 is justified. While the paper offers a significant contribution, further work is needed to address the limitations and fully realize its potential.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### How do Scaling Laws Apply to Knowledge Graph Engineering Tasks? The Impact of Model Size on Large Language Model Performance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16276v1)
- **Authors**: Desiree Heim, Lars-Peter Meyer, Markus Schröder, Johannes Frey, Andreas Dengel
- **Abstract**: When using Large Language Models (LLMs) to support Knowledge Graph Engineering (KGE), one of the first indications when searching for an appropriate model is its size. According to the scaling laws, larger models typically show higher capabilities. However, in practice, resource costs are also an important factor and thus it makes sense to consider the ratio between model performance and costs. The LLM-KG-Bench framework enables the comparison of LLMs in the context of KGE tasks and assesses their capabilities of understanding and producing KGs and KG queries. Based on a dataset created in an LLM-KG-Bench run covering 26 open state-of-the-art LLMs, we explore the model size scaling laws specific to KGE tasks. In our analyses, we assess how benchmark scores evolve between different model size categories. Additionally, we inspect how the general score development of single models and families of models correlates to their size. Our analyses revealed that, with a few exceptions, the model size scaling laws generally also apply to the selected KGE tasks. However, in some cases, plateau or ceiling effects occurred, i.e., the task performance did not change much between a model and the next larger model. In these cases, smaller models could be considered to achieve high cost-effectiveness. Regarding models of the same family, sometimes larger models performed worse than smaller models of the same family. These effects occurred only locally. Hence it is advisable to additionally test the next smallest and largest model of the same family.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the scaling laws of Large Language Models (LLMs) applied to Knowledge Graph Engineering (KGE) tasks. It utilizes data from a recent LLM-KG-Bench benchmark run, covering 26 open-source LLMs across various model sizes and families. The study analyzes how benchmark scores relate to model size, examining performance development both generally and within specific model families. The analysis reveals that while scaling laws generally apply, plateau and ceiling effects occur in some cases, suggesting that smaller models might be more cost-effective.  It also identifies cases where larger models within the same family perform worse than smaller counterparts, indicating that simply choosing the largest model isn't always optimal. The paper concludes by discussing the implications of these findings for selecting LLMs for KGE tasks and outlines directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Focus:** The paper provides a valuable empirical analysis of LLM performance on KGE tasks. The use of a standardized benchmark (LLM-KG-Bench) allows for a relatively fair comparison across different models.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The study addresses a practical question faced by practitioners: how to choose the right LLM for KGE tasks, considering both performance and cost. Identifying situations where smaller models are sufficient or even preferable has significant practical implications.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper explores various aspects of the scaling laws, including overall trends, family-specific behavior, and the presence of plateau effects.  The analysis goes beyond simply stating that "bigger is better" and delves into the nuances of the relationship between model size and performance in the context of KGE.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and the analysis is clearly presented with supporting tables and figures. The discussion section summarizes the key findings and their implications effectively.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of KGE Tasks:** The KGE tasks included in the LLM-KG-Bench might not fully represent the diverse range of challenges in real-world KGE scenarios. The focus on RDF and SPARQL, while important, might not capture other critical aspects. The limited tasks for RdfFriendCount and Text2Sparql are also acknowledged as being poorly performed by all open source models.
*   **Focus on Open-Source Models:** While understandable given resource constraints, the analysis primarily focuses on open-source LLMs. Including proprietary models' performance for reference is valuable, but the lack of size information limits a direct comparison within the scaling law analysis. This makes it challenging to generalize the findings definitively to all LLMs.
*   **Task Specificity:** The specific findings may be highly task-dependent. While the study identifies general trends, the optimal model size and choice may vary considerably depending on the specifics of the KGE task at hand. More tasks would make the benchmark and the study much stronger.
*   **Statistical Significance Considerations:** The authors do a good job in using Kruskal-Wallis tests and Dunn tests to verify significance. However, the lack of context with the task variations (serialization formats, number of error injections), make generalizing a little harder.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of literature on LLM evaluation, specifically in the context of KGE. While the general principle of scaling laws is well-established, the paper provides a valuable task-specific examination of these laws, revealing nuances and exceptions that are important for practical applications. The identification of plateau effects, the varying performance within model families, and the task type limitations all contribute new insights to the field. This task specific examination is more nuanced and valuable than just running established generic benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the understanding of LLM performance on KGE tasks. The use of an existing benchmark and rigorous analysis provide valuable empirical insights. However, the limitations in the task scope and the focus on open-source models prevent a more definitive and generalizable conclusion. The observations regarding plateau effects and within-family performance variations are noteworthy and potentially impactful.
