### PaTH Attention: Position Encoding via Accumulating Householder Transformations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16381v1)
- **Authors**: Songlin Yang, Yikang Shen, Kaiyue Wen, Shawn Tan, Mayank Mishra, Liliang Ren, Rameswar Panda, Yoon Kim
- **Abstract**: The attention mechanism is a core primitive in modern large language models (LLMs) and AI more broadly. Since attention by itself is permutation-invariant, position encoding is essential for modeling structured domains such as language. Rotary position encoding (RoPE) has emerged as the de facto standard approach for position encoding and is part of many modern LLMs. However, in RoPE the key/query transformation between two elements in a sequence is only a function of their relative position and otherwise independent of the actual input. This limits the expressivity of RoPE-based transformers. This paper describes PaTH, a flexible data-dependent position encoding scheme based on accumulated products of Householder(like) transformations, where each transformation is data-dependent, i.e., a function of the input. We derive an efficient parallel algorithm for training through exploiting a compact representation of products of Householder matrices, and implement a FlashAttention-style blockwise algorithm that minimizes I/O cost. Across both targeted synthetic benchmarks and moderate-scale real-world language modeling experiments, we find that PaTH demonstrates superior performance compared to RoPE and other recent baselines.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "PaTH Attention: Position Encoding via Accumulating Householder Transformations."

**Summary:**

This paper introduces PaTH, a novel position encoding scheme for attention mechanisms in large language models.  Instead of using static, data-independent methods like RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding), PaTH leverages a cumulative product of *data-dependent* Householder-like transformations to encode positional information.  This makes the position encoding dynamic and responsive to the input data.  The key idea is that each Householder transformation modifies the key/query representations based on the input at that position.  The paper derives an efficient, parallelizable algorithm for training PaTH by exploiting a compact representation of Householder matrix products.  Furthermore, it implements a FlashAttention-style blockwise algorithm to reduce I/O costs.  The paper presents experimental results on both synthetic (state-tracking) tasks and real-world language modeling, showing that PaTH outperforms RoPE and other baselines, particularly in scenarios requiring sequential reasoning and length extrapolation. The authors also explore combining PaTH with the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) and show improvements. They also show in theory that PaTH can solve an NC1-complete problem under AC0 reductions, a property not shared by the ROPE attention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using data-dependent, cumulative Householder transformations for position encoding is relatively novel. While data-dependent attention mechanisms and position encodings exist, the specific combination of cumulative Householder products and the associated efficient training algorithm differentiate this work. The theoretical analysis showing PaTH's ability to solve an NC1-complete problem, something traditional transformers cannot do, adds significant novelty. Also, the idea of seeing this formulation as a generalization of linear RNN's is a unique and important insight.

*   **Significance:**
    *   *Addressing Limitations of RoPE:* RoPE is currently the dominant position encoding method in many LLMs. The paper convincingly argues, both theoretically and empirically, that RoPE's data-independence limits its expressivity, especially for tasks requiring sequential reasoning and complex state tracking. PaTH directly addresses this limitation.
    *   *Empirical Validation:* The experimental results, while not on a massive scale (760M parameter models), provide strong evidence that PaTH improves performance on tasks where RoPE struggles. The synthetic tasks are especially compelling in demonstrating PaTH's ability to learn sequential dependencies. The language modeling results, even at a moderate scale, show tangible improvements over established baselines.
    *   *Efficient Implementation:* The development of a FlashAttention-like blockwise algorithm is crucial for making PaTH practical. Without an efficient implementation, the theoretical advantages would be difficult to realize. The kernel level optimization for triton is also important, to make PaTH usable.
    *   *Theoretical Support:* The theoretical result regarding the ability to solve NC1-complete problems provides a formal justification for the increased expressivity of PaTH.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear motivation and problem definition (limitations of RoPE).
    *   Novel and well-explained technical approach (data-dependent Householder transformations).
    *   Efficient training algorithm and practical implementation.
    *   Strong empirical results on both synthetic and real-world tasks.
    *   Theoretical guarantees on expressivity.
    *   Good connection to linear RNN's.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Limited Scale of Experiments:* While the results are promising, larger-scale experiments with models comparable to state-of-the-art LLMs (billions or trillions of parameters) would further strengthen the paper's claims. Also, most results are reported at 4096 context length, which is relatively short for modern transformers. Experiments showing improved performance on tasks at higher sequence lengths would be useful, particularly since there are claims regarding improvements to sequence length extrapolation.
    *   *Computational Overhead:* The paper acknowledges a modest slowdown compared to RoPE. Further optimization and hardware-specific tuning may be necessary to make PaTH more competitive in terms of computational cost, although I/O is kept the same. The improvements in long-context tasks are promising in this regard.
    *   *Ablation Studies:* More in-depth ablation studies could be useful to understand the impact of different components of PaTH (e.g., the sigmoid function for beta, low rank matrix representation) on performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   PaTH has the potential to become a valuable alternative to RoPE in LLMs, particularly for applications where sequential reasoning, state tracking, and length extrapolation are critical.
    *   The efficient training algorithm and FlashAttention-like implementation make it relatively easy to adopt.
    *   The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for future research on data-dependent position encodings.
    *   The result motivates future work on designing more data-dependent attention operators.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of attention mechanisms and position encoding. It addresses a clear limitation of existing methods (RoPE), introduces a novel and well-motivated approach (PaTH), provides an efficient implementation, and validates its effectiveness through comprehensive experiments and a theoretical framework. While the scale of the experiments could be larger, the results are sufficiently compelling to warrant significant interest. The paper makes a solid case that data-dependent attention operators are the future.

### Semantic Pivots Enable Cross-Lingual Transfer in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16385v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyu He, Tong Zhou, Yubo Chen, Delai Qiu, Shengping Liu, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable ability in cross-lingual tasks. Understanding how LLMs acquire this ability is crucial for their interpretability. To quantify the cross-lingual ability of LLMs accurately, we propose a Word-Level Cross-Lingual Translation Task. To find how LLMs learn cross-lingual ability, we trace the outputs of LLMs' intermediate layers in the word translation task. We identify and distinguish two distinct behaviors in the forward pass of LLMs: co-occurrence behavior and semantic pivot behavior. We attribute LLMs' two distinct behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency of words and find the semantic pivot from the pre-training dataset. Finally, to apply our findings to improve the cross-lingual ability of LLMs, we reconstruct a semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset using documents with a high proportion of semantic pivots. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of our approach in enhancing cross-lingual ability. Our research contributes insights into the interpretability of LLMs and offers a method for improving LLMs' cross-lingual ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) acquire cross-lingual abilities. The authors propose a Cross-Lingual Word Translation Dataset (CLWTD) and a corresponding metric to accurately quantify this ability. They analyze the intermediate layers of LLMs during word translation tasks and identify two distinct behaviors: "co-occurrence behavior" and "semantic pivot behavior." They attribute these behaviors to the co-occurrence frequency of words in the pre-training dataset.  Finally, they reconstruct a semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset and demonstrate improved cross-lingual ability in an LLM trained on this dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper addresses a crucial question in LLM research: how do LLMs achieve cross-lingual understanding?
*   **Novel Methodology:** The CLWTD offers a granular, word-level approach to evaluating cross-lingual ability, which is a useful alternative to sentence-level translation tasks. The analysis of LLM intermediate layers using "logit lens" to identify co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors is insightful.
*   **Interpretability Focus:** The paper contributes to the interpretability of LLMs by connecting the observed behaviors to the pre-training dataset's characteristics.  The link between co-occurrence frequency and semantic pivots helps explain the model's internal mechanisms.
*   **Practical Application:** The paper doesn't stop at analysis but proposes a method to improve cross-lingual ability by reconstructing a semantic pivot-aware dataset. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach.
*   **Relatively rigorous experiments:** The paper tests its hypothesis by training an OLMO-1B model on their dataset and comparing it to other models, and it shows a 0.013 improvement.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scale:** The experiments are conducted on a relatively small (1B parameter) OLMO model. While this is understandable given the limitations on available open-source models, it's difficult to generalize the findings to larger, state-of-the-art LLMs. This is explicitly acknowledged in the limitations section.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The reported improvement (0.013) is modest.  While statistically significant, it's unclear if this would translate to meaningful performance gains in real-world applications. It would benefit from more thorough testing.
*   **Dataset Construction Concerns:** The process of constructing the semantic pivot-aware pre-training dataset relies on DeepSeek for distractor generation and filtering, which may introduce biases or inaccuracies. The paper needs to clearly explain and justify this choice, or better yet, avoid relying on another LLM's judgments in the data creation process.
*   **Limited Scope of Pivot Analysis:** The selection of semantic pivots by ranking top 50 candidates, is a heuristic approach. A more principled method might yield different and potentially more effective pivots.  It also assumes there's a distinct, identifiable set of semantic pivots, which may not be accurate for all word pairs.
*   **Dependence on co-occurrence:** Co-occurrence frequency can be a superficial measure. The model is still learning from the dataset. However, it may not reflect the deeper semantic relationships, the model would need to take into account long-range and complex patterns in the dataset.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a novel perspective on how LLMs learn cross-lingual abilities. The distinction between co-occurrence and semantic pivot behaviors, and their link to pre-training data statistics, is a valuable contribution to the interpretability of these models. The proposed method for constructing semantic pivot-aware datasets is also potentially useful for improving cross-lingual performance, although the magnitude of the improvement needs further investigation.

The novelty lies in combining word-level analysis, intermediate layer probing, and pre-training data manipulation to understand and enhance cross-lingual capabilities. While previous works have explored similar avenues (e.g., Wendler et al., Zhong et al.), this paper offers a more comprehensive approach by connecting these elements together.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has some promising aspects. It clearly defines and addresses a relevant problem, proposes a well-defined methodology for investigation, and presents a potentially useful technique for improving the model's performance.

However, the limitations related to scale of models and dataset construction significantly diminish the paper's overall impact and generalizability. The relatively modest performance gains on a small model, while notable, limit the practical significance of the findings. Further investigation would be needed on larger models, with more robust experimentation, before this would be considered a significantly impactful contribution to the field.

### Resource for Error Analysis in Text Simplification: New Taxonomy and Test Collection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16392v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Vendeville, Liana Ermakova, Pierre De Loor
- **Abstract**: The general public often encounters complex texts but does not have the time or expertise to fully understand them, leading to the spread of misinformation. Automatic Text Simplification (ATS) helps make information more accessible, but its evaluation methods have not kept up with advances in text generation, especially with Large Language Models (LLMs). In particular, recent studies have shown that current ATS metrics do not correlate with the presence of errors. Manual inspections have further revealed a variety of errors, underscoring the need for a more nuanced evaluation framework, which is currently lacking. This resource paper addresses this gap by introducing a test collection for detecting and classifying errors in simplified texts. First, we propose a taxonomy of errors, with a formal focus on information distortion. Next, we introduce a parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts. This dataset has been human-annotated with labels based on our proposed taxonomy. Finally, we analyze the quality of the dataset, and we study the performance of existing models to detect and classify errors from that taxonomy. These contributions give researchers the tools to better evaluate errors in ATS, develop more reliable models, and ultimately improve the quality of automatically simplified texts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the need for improved error analysis in Automatic Text Simplification (ATS), especially in the context of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It identifies that existing evaluation metrics are inadequate for capturing the nuances of errors introduced by LLMs during text simplification. To address this gap, the authors introduce a new taxonomy of errors in ATS, with a specific focus on information distortion. They then create a parallel dataset of automatically simplified scientific texts annotated according to this taxonomy. Finally, they evaluate the performance of existing error detection models on this dataset, revealing their limitations.  The paper aims to provide researchers with a resource to better evaluate errors in ATS, develop more reliable models, and ultimately improve the quality of automatically simplified texts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive and task-specific taxonomy of errors for ATS, particularly focusing on information distortion, that goes beyond existing general error detection approaches. Previous works have looked at error evaluation, but often without a specific focus on the intricacies of text simplification and related information errors like hallucinations and oversimplification. The creation of an annotated dataset based on this taxonomy also adds value. Although there are other manually created and automatically created datasets available for ATS tasks, a manually annotated dataset with a high-quality taxonomy on error types is novel.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from the limitations of current evaluation methods for ATS. As the field becomes more reliant on powerful but potentially unreliable LLMs, a nuanced understanding of the types of errors introduced during simplification is crucial. The provided taxonomy and annotated dataset can potentially help:

*   **More accurate evaluation:**  The fine-grained error types will allow researchers to move beyond coarse-grained metrics and understand specific weaknesses of ATS models.
*   **Improved model development:** Identifying the specific types of errors that LLMs tend to make can guide the development of new techniques to mitigate those errors.
*   **Better understanding of LLMs in ATS:** By analyzing the annotated data, it is possible to understand how LLMs behave in the ATS task and what aspects of the model contributes to each error type.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined taxonomy:** The taxonomy is structured logically and covers a wide range of error types, going beyond simple fluency issues. The formal definition and detailed structure improves on previous work.
*   **Task-specific focus:**  The taxonomy is designed specifically for ATS, taking into account the unique challenges of simplification.
*   **Practical resource:** The annotated dataset provides a valuable resource for researchers to benchmark and improve error detection methods.
*   **Analysis of existing models:** The evaluation of existing hallucination detection models on the dataset provides a useful baseline and highlights the limitations of current approaches.
*   **Clear motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and the need for improved error analysis in ATS.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Inter-annotator agreement:**  While the paper addresses inter-annotator agreement, consistency rates varied across annotators. The low consistency rate of one annotator makes the data slightly less reliable and introduces possible bias to the dataset, although the authors justify this.
*   **Dataset size and representativeness:** A bigger dataset would have likely improved the analysis quality and reliability.
*   **Limited scope of models evaluated:** Only a limited set of existing models were used for evaluation. Evaluating a more extensive range of models would have produced stronger results and potentially revealed more subtle patterns.
*   **Lack of task-specific detection methods:** A strong evaluation of the effectiveness of existing methods would be to modify them to specifically target each type of error listed in the taxonomy. This allows a better evaluation of the completeness of the taxonomy.
*   **Evaluation Metric limitations:** The choice of AUROC and AUPRC, while standard, might not be the best for imbalanced datasets or for evaluating specific error types.

**Potential Impact:**

The paper has the potential to impact future research in ATS by providing a more comprehensive and nuanced framework for error analysis and a valuable resource for developing more reliable models.  The taxonomy can be used as a starting point for future refinements and extensions, and the dataset can be used to train and evaluate new error detection methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper offers a significant contribution to the field of Automatic Text Simplification, particularly regarding the evaluation and mitigation of errors introduced by LLMs. Although the size of the data and specific methods are limited, it introduces the first comprehensive taxonomy for errors in the ATS task and also contributes with a manual annotated dataset that follows it.

### Divide-Fuse-Conquer: Eliciting "Aha Moments" in Multi-Scenario Games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16401v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoqing Zhang, Huabin Zheng, Ang Lv, Yuhan Liu, Zirui Song, Flood Sung, Xiuying Chen, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have been observed to suddenly exhibit advanced reasoning abilities during reinforcement learning (RL), resembling an ``aha moment'' triggered by simple outcome-based rewards. While RL has proven effective in eliciting such breakthroughs in tasks involving mathematics, coding, and vision, it faces significant challenges in multi-scenario games. The diversity of game rules, interaction modes, and environmental complexities often leads to policies that perform well in one scenario but fail to generalize to others. Simply combining multiple scenarios during training introduces additional challenges, such as training instability and poor performance. To overcome these challenges, we propose Divide-Fuse-Conquer, a framework designed to enhance generalization in multi-scenario RL. This approach starts by heuristically grouping games based on characteristics such as rules and difficulties. Specialized models are then trained for each group to excel at games in the group is what we refer to as the divide step. Next, we fuse model parameters from different groups as a new model, and continue training it for multiple groups, until the scenarios in all groups are conquered. Experiments across 18 TextArena games show that Qwen2.5-32B-Align trained with the Divide-Fuse-Conquer strategy reaches a performance level comparable to Claude3.5, achieving 7 wins and 4 draws. We hope our approach can inspire future research on using reinforcement learning to improve the generalization of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" (DFC) framework to enhance the generalization capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) trained using Reinforcement Learning (RL) in multi-scenario game environments. Recognizing the challenges of training LLMs in diverse game settings (training instability, poor generalization), the DFC approach involves three main steps: (1) **Divide:** Heuristically grouping games based on characteristics like rules and difficulty. (2) **Fuse:** Merging model parameters from individually trained group-specific models into a single, unified model. (3) **Conquer:** Continuing RL training of the fused model across all game scenarios. The method also incorporates techniques like format reward shaping, half-negative sampling, mixed priority sampling, e-greedy disturbance, and hasty-action penalty to improve training stability, efficiency, and performance.  Experimental results on 18 TextArena games using a Qwen2.5-32B-Align model demonstrate that the DFC approach achieves competitive performance against a strong Claude3.5 baseline, suggesting improved generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The "Divide-Fuse-Conquer" framework offers a structured approach to a recognized problem: training LLMs with RL in complex, heterogeneous environments. While the individual components (grouping, merging, and curriculum learning) aren't entirely novel on their own, the specific *combination* and application within the context of LLM-based multi-scenario RL, *especially the nuanced Conquer stage with its stability and efficiency techniques,* is where the novelty lies. Prior works often handle multi-task learning in a more monolithic manner, or focus primarily on task decomposition without a structured way to integrate knowledge.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in scaling LLMs for real-world applications that often involve diverse and dynamic scenarios. Demonstrating improved generalization in game environments represents a significant step towards building more robust and adaptable agents. The detailed ablation study of the various techniques (reward shaping, sampling strategies, etc.) is valuable for the community as it offers practical guidance on how to stabilize and improve RL training for LLMs. The competitive results achieved against Claude3.5, despite using a smaller model (Qwen2.5-32B), further emphasize the potential benefits of the proposed framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem and motivation:** Clearly articulates the challenges of multi-scenario RL with LLMs.
    *   **Structured framework:** DFC provides a clear, step-by-step methodology.
    *   **Comprehensive techniques:** The incorporation of multiple training techniques (reward shaping, sampling methods, etc.) addresses different aspects of the problem.
    *   **Empirical validation:** Experiments across diverse games and comparison against a strong baseline provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the method.
    *   **Ablation Study:** Highlights the importance of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Heuristic grouping:** The initial "Divide" step relies on a heuristic game grouping process, which could be subjective and potentially sub-optimal. A more principled approach (e.g., using unsupervised learning on game characteristics) could further improve performance.
    *   **Limited model focus:** While the authors explore various sampling strategies, the analysis of various sampling strategies can potentially be further enriched.
    *   **Lack of formal generalization bounds:** The paper demonstrates empirical improvements in generalization, but lacks theoretical analysis or formal guarantees on generalization performance.
    *   **Experiment details:** Several parameters, such as the temperature, the KL penalty coefficient, and the rollout budget are not discussed in detail. More analysis of the impact of these hyperparameters could improve the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   **Inspire more structured RL frameworks:** The DFC approach can serve as a template for designing other structured methods for RL in complex environments.
    *   **Guide technique selection:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into which training techniques are most effective for different scenarios.
    *   **Motivate theoretical analysis:** The empirical results can motivate further theoretical work on understanding the generalization properties of RL-trained LLMs in multi-scenario settings.
    *   **Demonstrate competitive R1 performance**:  This work helps build the body of evidence surrounding use of R1 training methods and how competitive it can be for LLMs.

*   **Justification:**
    While the Divide-Fuse-Conquer framework leverages some existing concepts, the combination and careful application within the context of LLM reinforcement learning in diverse game scenarios represent a non-trivial and valuable contribution. The comprehensive suite of stability-improving techniques and the competitive results against a strong baseline justify a reasonably high score. However, the heuristic nature of the initial grouping and the lack of theoretical analysis somewhat limit the overall impact. A score of 7 reflects this balance, acknowledging the practical significance and solid empirical validation while also recognizing the areas for further improvement.

### From Surveys to Narratives: Rethinking Cultural Value Adaptation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16408v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Farid Adilazuarda, Chen Cecilia Liu, Iryna Gurevych, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Adapting cultural values in Large Language Models (LLMs) presents significant challenges, particularly due to biases and limited training data. Prior work primarily aligns LLMs with different cultural values using World Values Survey (WVS) data. However, it remains unclear whether this approach effectively captures cultural nuances or produces distinct cultural representations for various downstream tasks. In this paper, we systematically investigate WVS-based training for cultural value adaptation and find that relying solely on survey data can homogenize cultural norms and interfere with factual knowledge. To investigate these issues, we augment WVS with encyclopedic and scenario-based cultural narratives from Wikipedia and NormAd. While these narratives may have variable effects on downstream tasks, they consistently improve cultural distinctiveness than survey data alone. Our work highlights the inherent complexity of aligning cultural values with the goal of guiding task-specific behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

This paper investigates methods for adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to different cultural values. The authors critically assess the common practice of using World Values Survey (WVS) data for this adaptation. They find that relying solely on WVS can lead to homogenized cultural representations and interfere with factual knowledge. To address these limitations, the paper explores augmenting WVS data with narrative data from Wikipedia and NormAd (a dataset of cultural norms and scenarios). The study finds that while narrative data may have variable effects on downstream task performance, it consistently improves cultural distinctiveness compared to using survey data alone. The work highlights the challenges in aligning cultural values with task-specific behavior and emphasizes the need for further research into effective data sources for cultural adaptation.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic and critical evaluation of WVS-based training for cultural value adaptation in LLMs, which has become a widespread practice. It also presents the original idea of using narrative-based data to alleviate the homogenization effect when tuning the models on cultural values. The observation that adapting LLMs with survey data can interfere with factual knowledge and that narrative data improves cultural distinctiveness adds valuable insights. Although there were earlier explorations of aligning cultural values in LLMs, this paper contributes a significantly more in-depth analysis and a direct comparison with and without narrative data.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   They challenge the assumption that survey data alone is sufficient for cultural alignment, prompting researchers to reconsider their approach.
    *   The identification of knowledge interference is a crucial concern, as it highlights the potential trade-offs between cultural adaptation and preserving factual accuracy.
    *   The exploration of narrative data sources (Wikipedia and NormAd) suggests a promising avenue for improving cultural distinctiveness.
    *   The results emphasize the complexity of cultural adaptation and the need for more nuanced methods that account for both values and behavior.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper conducts a well-designed and rigorous experimental study across multiple LLMs, languages, and evaluation tasks.
    *   The analysis is thorough, examining both downstream task performance and cultural distinctiveness.
    *   The introduction of the C-DIST metric provides a valuable tool for quantifying cultural preservation.
    *   The paper clearly identifies limitations and outlines future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper demonstrates the potential of narrative data, the effects on downstream tasks are inconsistent. More work is needed to understand how to effectively integrate narrative data into LLM training pipelines.
    *   The paper focuses on a limited set of cultural contexts and tasks. Further research is needed to assess the generalizability of the findings to other cultures and domains.
    *   The evaluation metrics, particularly for assessing cultural relevance in freeform generation, could be further refined and validated.
    *   The NormAd dataset, though valuable, consists of curated examples and might not fully represent the full spectrum of cultural variations.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in cultural adaptation of LLMs by:

    *   Encouraging researchers to move beyond solely survey-based approaches.
    *   Motivating the development of new datasets and training methods that incorporate richer cultural narratives.
    *   Promoting more rigorous evaluation of cultural distinctiveness and factual knowledge retention.
    *   Guiding the creation of more culturally sensitive and effective LLMs for diverse global audiences.

*   **Critical Remarks:** While the study's insights are valuable, the paper doesn't offer a clear path to incorporating narrative data, beyond simply adding it to the training dataset. There are a lot of open questions to be addressed in future work, such as the type of information that may be incorporated and its proportion in the resulting dataset.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field. It challenges the current state of research with a robust analysis of a popular cultural adaptation method. The observation of knowledge interference and the demonstration that narrative data improves cultural distinctiveness offer valuable insights. The limitations and inconsistent results concerning downstream tasks prevent a higher score. Further research building on these insights is needed before the full potential of the proposed approach can be realized. However, the paper makes a significant step towards more nuanced and effective methods for cultural adaptation in LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16410v1)
- **Authors**: Guanting Dong, Yifei Chen, Xiaoxi Li, Jiajie Jin, Hongjin Qian, Yutao Zhu, Hangyu Mao, Guorui Zhou, Zhicheng Dou, Ji-Rong Wen
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities via large-scale reinforcement learning (RL). However, leveraging the RL algorithm to empower effective multi-tool collaborative reasoning in LLMs remains an open challenge. In this paper, we introduce Tool-Star, an RL-based framework designed to empower LLMs to autonomously invoke multiple external tools during stepwise reasoning. Tool-Star integrates six types of tools and incorporates systematic designs in both data synthesis and training. To address the scarcity of tool-use data, we propose a general tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline, which combines tool-integrated prompting with hint-based sampling to automatically and scalably generate tool-use trajectories. A subsequent quality normalization and difficulty-aware classification process filters out low-quality samples and organizes the dataset from easy to hard. Furthermore, we propose a two-stage training framework to enhance multi-tool collaborative reasoning by: (1) cold-start fine-tuning, which guides LLMs to explore reasoning patterns via tool-invocation feedback; and (2) a multi-tool self-critic RL algorithm with hierarchical reward design, which reinforces reward understanding and promotes effective tool collaboration. Experimental analyses on over 10 challenging reasoning benchmarks highlight the effectiveness and efficiency of Tool-Star. The code is available at https://github.com/dongguanting/Tool-Star.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper, evaluate its novelty and significance, and assign a score with justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "Tool-Star: Empowering LLM-Brained Multi-Tool Reasoner via Reinforcement Learning" introduces a reinforcement learning-based framework designed to enable Large Language Models (LLMs) to autonomously invoke multiple external tools during a stepwise reasoning process. Addressing the challenges of scarce tool-use data, the authors propose a general tool-integrated reasoning data synthesis pipeline, combining tool-integrated prompting with hint-based sampling to generate scalable training trajectories. They also implement a quality normalization and difficulty-aware classification process to filter low-quality samples and organize the dataset from easy to hard. The paper further presents a two-stage training framework consisting of cold-start fine-tuning and a multi-tool self-critic RL algorithm with hierarchical reward design, which promotes effective tool collaboration. Experimental analyses across 10 challenging reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of the Tool-Star framework.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents some novel elements in the context of tool-integrated reasoning for LLMs. The key areas where the paper attempts to make a contribution are:

*   **Data Synthesis Pipeline:** The automated pipeline combining tool-integrated prompting, hint-based sampling, quality normalization, and difficulty-aware classification appears to be a significant contribution. Generating high-quality training data for tool usage is a crucial bottleneck, and this pipeline offers a scalable solution. The hierarchical reward design within the self-critic RL algorithm is a good attempt at promoting better collaboration across multiple tools, and the cold-start SFT helps explore the reasoning pattern, thus lowering the difficulty in RL stage.
*   **Multi-Tool Self-Critic RL Algorithm:** While RL for tool use isn't new, the focus on multi-tool collaboration is a differentiating factor. The hierarchical reward mechanism, rewarding both answer correctness and effective tool collaboration, is a useful aspect.
*   **Two-Stage Training Framework:** Combines cold-start fine-tuning and multi-tool collaborative reasoning.

**However:**

*   **Incremental Advances:** While the components are well-motivated, the overall approach feels somewhat incremental. It's an assembly of existing techniques (RL, prompting, self-critic learning) with modifications tailored to the multi-tool setting. It's not a fundamentally new algorithm or a radical departure from existing paradigms.
*   **Limited Theoretical Foundation:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical analysis. There's limited discussion about the convergence properties of the RL algorithm, the optimality of the reward function, or any theoretical guarantees.
*   **Reliance on Existing Tools:** The framework leverages existing tools like search engines and code interpreters. The novelty lies in *how* these tools are orchestrated, not in the tools themselves. The framework's design needs to be robust enough to work with different tools.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Impact:** The paper has potential practical significance. LLMs that can effectively utilize external tools are highly valuable in real-world applications. The data synthesis pipeline and training framework offer a way to improve the capabilities of LLMs in these scenarios.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a strong empirical evaluation, demonstrating the effectiveness of Tool-Star across several challenging benchmarks. This provides evidence that the approach is more than just a theoretical concept.
*   **Reproducibility and Availability:** The availability of the code is a significant plus. This will facilitate further research and adoption by the community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** More detailed ablation studies could have shed light on the individual contributions of each component. For example, isolating the effect of hierarchical rewards or the self-critic mechanism more clearly.
*   **Lack of Generalization Analysis:** While the paper reports good performance on the benchmarks, there's limited discussion about the generalizability of the approach to new tools or tasks.
*   **Overclaiming:** The paper somewhat overclaims the novelty and significance of its contributions. While the approach is certainly useful and effective, the level of innovation is more incremental than revolutionary.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of tool-integrated reasoning for LLMs. The data synthesis pipeline and multi-tool RL algorithm are useful components, and the empirical results are strong. However, the approach is more incremental than revolutionary, and the paper lacks a strong theoretical foundation. It should also investigate how performance can be achieved and optimized for general tools.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The score reflects the combination of a novel, well-motivated framework with strong empirical results, balanced by incremental advances and limited theoretical analysis. The score falls between a "marginally above average" contribution and a "significant contribution". The code's availability pushes it towards a "significant contribution," especially with well-documented code base and tutorial.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Attributing Response to Context: A Jensen-Shannon Divergence Driven Mechanistic Study of Context Attribution in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16415v1)
- **Authors**: Ruizhe Li, Chen Chen, Yuchen Hu, Yanjun Gao, Xi Wang, Emine Yilmaz
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) leverages large language models (LLMs) combined with external contexts to enhance the accuracy and reliability of generated responses. However, reliably attributing generated content to specific context segments, context attribution, remains challenging due to the computationally intensive nature of current methods, which often require extensive fine-tuning or human annotation. In this work, we introduce a novel Jensen-Shannon Divergence driven method to Attribute Response to Context (ARC-JSD), enabling efficient and accurate identification of essential context sentences without additional fine-tuning or surrogate modelling. Evaluations on a wide range of RAG benchmarks, such as TyDi QA, Hotpot QA, and Musique, using instruction-tuned LLMs in different scales demonstrate superior accuracy and significant computational efficiency improvements compared to the previous surrogate-based method. Furthermore, our mechanistic analysis reveals specific attention heads and multilayer perceptron (MLP) layers responsible for context attribution, providing valuable insights into the internal workings of RAG models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARC-JSD, a novel and computationally efficient method for attributing generated responses to specific context segments in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) models. ARC-JSD leverages Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) to measure the difference in response distributions when conditioned on the full context versus ablated contexts. It ranks context sentences based on their JSD differences, identifying essential sentences without the need for fine-tuning or surrogate modeling, unlike previous methods. The paper demonstrates improved accuracy and computational efficiency compared to surrogate-based approaches across various RAG benchmarks and instruction-tuned LLMs. Furthermore, the authors perform mechanistic analysis, pinpointing specific attention heads and MLP layers responsible for context attribution within RAG LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of JSD for context attribution in RAG models and the elimination of fine-tuning or surrogate modeling. While the idea of ablating context sentences and observing the impact on the response isn't entirely new (Cohen-Wang et al.), the use of JSD to directly quantify distribution shifts is a significant improvement. This offers a more direct measure of the impact of each sentence, avoiding the limitations of methods relying on approximating this impact through fine-tuning or learning a surrogate model. This leads to both improved accuracy and faster computation, making context attribution less of a bottleneck when working with RAG systems. The mechanistic analysis aspect of the paper also contributes to novelty by offering insights into which parts of the neural network are critical for context awareness.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the trustworthiness and efficiency of RAG models. By providing a way to quickly and accurately identify the context segments contributing to a response, ARC-JSD can facilitate better error analysis, debugging, and ultimately more reliable RAG systems. The increased computational efficiency makes context attribution more accessible in real-world applications with long documents.
    The paper also contributes to the interpretability of LLMs by providing a method to determine which part of the network contribute to context awareness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** ARC-JSD offers a substantial speedup compared to surrogate model-based methods.
    *   **Accuracy:** The method achieves higher accuracy in identifying relevant context segments.
    *   **No Fine-tuning Required:** The method does not require fine-tuning or training of surrogate models, making it readily applicable to existing RAG pipelines.
    *   **Mechanistic Analysis:** Provides insights into attention heads and MLP layers involved in context attribution.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Innovation:** While the combination of JSD with ablation and the mechanistic analysis provides improvement over prior methods, it is important to acknowledge that the individual concepts have a basis in previous research. The novelty is in their combination and refinement to create a more practical and insightful method.
    *   **Layer-level Granularity:** The mechanistic analysis, while valuable, is limited to identifying entire layers and attention heads. The paper alludes to exploring specific neurons and circuits that would contribute towards the analysis.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While demonstrated on several QA datasets, the method's performance and applicability might vary depending on the nature of the RAG task and the type of LLM used.
*   **Potential Influence:** The method has the potential to become a standard tool for analyzing and debugging RAG models. It could encourage the development of more transparent and reliable RAG systems and help researchers design models with improved context awareness.

*Reasoning for the Score:*

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of RAG by introducing a practical and effective method for context attribution. The use of JSD and mechanistic analysis provides valuable insights.
It's a novel integration of a few existing concepts to address an important practical challenge, and improves over prior state of the art.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Circle-RoPE: Cone-like Decoupled Rotary Positional Embedding for Large Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16416v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcheng Wang, Jianyuan Guo, Hongguang Li, Yuchuan Tian, Ying Nie, Chang Xu, Kai Han
- **Abstract**: Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is a widely adopted technique for encoding relative positional information in large language models (LLMs). However, when extended to large vision-language models (LVLMs), its variants introduce unintended cross-modal positional biases. Specifically, they enforce relative positional dependencies between text token indices and image tokens, causing spurious alignments. This issue arises because image tokens representing the same content but located at different spatial positions are assigned distinct positional biases, leading to inconsistent cross-modal associations. To address this, we propose Per-Token Distance (PTD) - a simple yet effective metric for quantifying the independence of positional encodings across modalities. Informed by this analysis, we introduce Circle-RoPE, a novel encoding scheme that maps image token indices onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the linear path of text token indices, forming a cone-like structure. This configuration ensures that each text token maintains an equal distance to all image tokens, reducing artificial cross-modal biases while preserving intra-image spatial information. To further enhance performance, we propose a staggered layer strategy that applies different RoPE variants across layers. This design leverages the complementary strengths of each RoPE variant, thereby enhancing the model's overall performance. Our experimental results demonstrate that our method effectively preserves spatial information from images while reducing relative positional bias, offering a more robust and flexible positional encoding framework for LVLMs. The code is available at [https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE](https://github.com/lose4578/CircleRoPE).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Circle-RoPE: Cone-like Decoupled Rotary Positional Embedding for Large Vision-Language Models" addresses the problem of positional bias in large vision-language models (LVLMs) when using Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE).  The authors argue that directly applying RoPE to concatenated text and image tokens introduces unintended cross-modal dependencies, leading to sub-optimal performance.  To mitigate this, they propose Circle-RoPE, a novel positional encoding scheme that maps image token indices onto a circular trajectory orthogonal to the linear path of text token indices, forming a cone-like structure.  This aims to decouple positional information across modalities while preserving intra-image spatial information.  The paper also introduces Per-Token Distance (PTD) metric to quantify positional bias and presents Dual-Frame Fusion (DFF) and Alternating Geometric Encoding (AGE) strategies to further improve performance.  Experiments show the effectiveness of Circle-RoPE in various visual reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of Circle-RoPE, namely mapping image tokens to a circle orthogonal to the text token axis, represents a novel approach to decoupling positional information. The introduction of the PTD metric is useful for quantifying the problem. The DFF and AGE strategies are incremental but logical extensions. The overall integration into a framework (Circle RoPE) is well-presented.

*   **Significance:** The issue of positional bias in multimodal transformers is relevant. If the proposed method demonstrates consistent and significant improvements across a range of tasks, it could be a valuable contribution. The paper highlights a real and previously under-addressed challenge in LVLMs. It offers a tangible solution that is relatively easy to implement, and therefore has the potential for adoption in the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of cross-modal positional bias and the limitations of existing RoPE variants.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solution:** The proposed Circle-RoPE is logically motivated by the need to decouple text and image positional encodings while preserving spatial information.
    *   **Quantitative Metric:** The PTD metric provides a way to quantify the degree of positional bias, enabling more objective comparisons between different encoding schemes.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes experiments on a diverse set of datasets, comparing Circle-RoPE to state-of-the-art models and conducting ablation studies to analyze the contributions of different components.
    *   **Readability:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand. Figures are helpful in illustrating the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental nature of DFF and AGE:** While logical, the improvements offered from these components appear relatively small, suggesting that they might not be universally valuable.
    *   **Limited Ablation details:** While there is ablation study on various components. The paper would benefit from an analysis on the computational cost and additional parameters that come along with the proposed modules.
    *   **Reduced Training Data for Comparison:** The choice to train on a 10% subset of the Mammoth VL data to compare different models raises questions about the generalizability of these results and their relevance to SOTA when more data is used. The paper should state how much these models are improved by using larger amount of dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** If Circle-RoPE proves effective, it could influence the design of future positional encoding schemes for LVLMs, leading to improved performance in visual reasoning tasks.
    *   The method might be readily adopted due to its relative simplicity and ease of integration into existing transformer architectures.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper makes a novel contribution to the field of multimodal learning by addressing the cross-modal positional bias in existing vision-language models. The proposed Circle-RoPE approach is well-motivated and grounded in the goal of decoupling text and image positional encodings while maintaining spatial relationships. The use of the PTD metric provides a useful quantitative measure for evaluating positional bias. The DFF and AGE strategies, while showing some benefit, add a layer of complexity that doesn't deliver the same core value proposition, even though the results can be further improved on top of Circle-RoPE. The training scheme on a reduced data is also concerning for comparing the model with SOTA. However, the work addresses a crucial and previously relatively under-addressed problem, and the proposed method is both inventive and well-motivated, thus justifying this assignment.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### WebAgent-R1: Training Web Agents via End-to-End Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16421v1)
- **Authors**: Zhepei Wei, Wenlin Yao, Yao Liu, Weizhi Zhang, Qin Lu, Liang Qiu, Changlong Yu, Puyang Xu, Chao Zhang, Bing Yin, Hyokun Yun, Lihong Li
- **Abstract**: While reinforcement learning (RL) has demonstrated remarkable success in enhancing large language models (LLMs), it has primarily focused on single-turn tasks such as solving math problems. Training effective web agents for multi-turn interactions remains challenging due to the complexity of long-horizon decision-making across dynamic web interfaces. In this work, we present WebAgent-R1, a simple yet effective end-to-end multi-turn RL framework for training web agents. It learns directly from online interactions with web environments by asynchronously generating diverse trajectories, entirely guided by binary rewards depending on task success. Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of WebAgent-R1, boosting the task success rate of Qwen-2.5-3B from 6.1% to 33.9% and Llama-3.1-8B from 8.5% to 44.8%, significantly outperforming existing state-of-the-art methods and strong proprietary models such as OpenAI o3. In-depth analyses reveal the effectiveness of the thinking-based prompting strategy and test-time scaling through increased interactions for web tasks. We further investigate different RL initialization policies by introducing two variants, namely WebAgent-R1-Zero and WebAgent-R1-CoT, which highlight the importance of the warm-up training stage (i.e., behavior cloning) and provide insights on incorporating long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in web agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "WEBAGENT-R1: Training Web Agents via End-to-End Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WEBAGENT-R1, an end-to-end reinforcement learning (RL) framework for training web agents capable of multi-turn interactions.  The framework addresses the challenge of long-horizon decision-making in dynamic web environments. It learns directly from online interactions, guided by binary task success rewards. Key components include dynamic context compression to handle long HTML inputs and an asynchronous trajectory rollout strategy to improve training efficiency, based on a multi-turn extension of Group Relative Policy Optimization (M-GRPO). Experiments on the WebArena-Lite benchmark show significant improvements over existing methods and strong baselines, across different model sizes. Ablation studies explore the impact of behavior cloning, thinking-based prompting, and test-time scaling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of end-to-end RL for web agents isn't entirely new, but the combination of specific techniques presents a valuable engineering contribution. Dynamic context compression is a practical solution to the long-context problem inherent in web tasks. While asynchronous trajectory rollout is a common technique in RL, its application in this specific context is well-motivated. The M-GRPO extension of the GRPO algorithm (which already introduces policy updates with relative advantages for reinforcement learning) is of limited novelty. The novelty mainly lies in the specific combination and engineering of existing RL methods to tackle the web navigation domain.

*   **Significance/Impact:**  The results on WebArena-Lite are compelling. The gains over existing methods and strong proprietary models (like OpenAI-03) are significant, demonstrating the effectiveness of the framework. The ablation studies provide useful insights into the importance of behavior cloning as a warm-up and the benefits of incorporating thinking-based prompting. The analysis of training dynamics (reward, trajectory length, interactions) provides a more nuanced understanding of how the RL agent learns. The framework can serve as a useful benchmark and baseline for further research in this area. The most important contribution is demonstrating a practical, end-to-end RL approach for web agents that works well in practice, which could encourage further exploration in this direction.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-written paper.
    *   The end-to-end framework is practical and implementable.
    *   Strong empirical results on a challenging benchmark.
    *   Well-designed ablation studies that provide valuable insights.
    *   Addresses an important and relevant problem: automating web interactions with LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The M-GRPO contribution appears to be a straightforward application of GRPO to a multi-turn setting. There is little theoretical novelty claimed with this extension.
    *   The RL setup (binary rewards, limited exploration techniques) is relatively simple, although the results suggest it is effective. Fine-grained reward shaping or more sophisticated exploration strategies could potentially improve performance further.
    *   The experiments primarily focus on WebArena-Lite, which, while a valuable benchmark, is still a somewhat controlled environment.  Generalization to more diverse and less structured real-world web environments needs to be considered.
    *   The dependency on rule-based reward functions, though acknowledged, limits the scalability and adaptability to tasks where such functions are difficult to define.
    *   The method depends on supervised finetuning data (behavior cloning), and the performance improves substantially relative to the zero-shot or prompting baselines. This could limit the ability to bootstrap in environments with no or very limited available data.

**Justification:**

The paper provides a solid engineering contribution by combining existing RL techniques to create a functional and performant web agent. The performance improvements and ablation studies add to our understanding of training web agents, demonstrating a practical and effective end-to-end RL approach. The primary strength is the practical demonstration and solid empirical results. However, the algorithmic novelty is relatively limited (most parts are extensions of existing techniques) and the approach is highly tied to the benchmark characteristics (dependency of behavior cloning, rule-based rewards). Therefore, the score reflects the practical significance and solid evaluation, but acknowledges the moderate algorithmic novelty.

### Beyond Static Testbeds: An Interaction-Centric Agent Simulation Platform for Dynamic Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16429v1)
- **Authors**: Song Jin, Juntian Zhang, Yuhan Liu, Xun Zhang, Yufei Zhang, Guojun Yin, Fei Jiang, Wei Lin, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Evaluating and iterating upon recommender systems is crucial, yet traditional A/B testing is resource-intensive, and offline methods struggle with dynamic user-platform interactions. While agent-based simulation is promising, existing platforms often lack a mechanism for user actions to dynamically reshape the environment. To bridge this gap, we introduce RecInter, a novel agent-based simulation platform for recommender systems featuring a robust interaction mechanism. In RecInter platform, simulated user actions (e.g., likes, reviews, purchases) dynamically update item attributes in real-time, and introduced Merchant Agents can reply, fostering a more realistic and evolving ecosystem. High-fidelity simulation is ensured through Multidimensional User Profiling module, Advanced Agent Architecture, and LLM fine-tuned on Chain-of-Thought (CoT) enriched interaction data. Our platform achieves significantly improved simulation credibility and successfully replicates emergent phenomena like Brand Loyalty and the Matthew Effect. Experiments demonstrate that this interaction mechanism is pivotal for simulating realistic system evolution, establishing our platform as a credible testbed for recommender systems research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Static Testbeds: An Interaction-Centric Agent Simulation Platform for Dynamic Recommender Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RecInter, a novel agent-based simulation platform designed for evaluating dynamic recommender systems. Addressing a gap in existing simulation environments, RecInter emphasizes a robust interaction mechanism where simulated user actions (likes, reviews, purchases) dynamically update item attributes in real-time. Merchant Agents can reply to user reviews, further enriching the ecosystem. The platform leverages Multidimensional User Profiling, Advanced Agent Architecture, and Behavior Simulation Training using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) enriched data to ensure high-fidelity simulations. Experiments showcase that RecInter can replicate emergent phenomena like brand loyalty and the Matthew Effect. The authors claim this demonstrates the platform's credibility as a testbed for recommender systems research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the integrated interaction mechanism. While agent-based recommender system simulation is an established field, the real-time updating of item attributes based on user feedback, including merchant responses, provides a more realistic simulation of a dynamic environment. The use of CoT fine-tuning for behavior simulation training and multidimensional user profiling also add to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** A realistic simulation platform offers significant potential benefits. It reduces the need for resource-intensive A/B testing and helps researchers explore complex system dynamics more safely than deploying untested algorithms to real users. Replicating emergent phenomena provides some evidence to support the claim that the platform mimics realistic behavior.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper effectively identifies the limitations of current simulation platforms and the importance of dynamic user-platform interactions.
    *   **Well-defined platform:** The architecture of RecInter, including its key modules (user profiling, agent architecture, and interaction mechanism), is clearly explained.
    *   **Solid experimental validation:** The experiments demonstrate the impact of the interaction mechanism and show RecInter's ability to reproduce real-world phenomena.
    *   **Comprehensive baselines:** The comparison to well-established baselines (RecAgent, Agent4Rec, SimUSER) strengthens the claims of improved performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency and Bias:** The heavy reliance on LLMs for user profiling introduces potential biases and comprehensions limitations from these models. As the paper acknowledges, these biases could inadvertently be amplified within the simulation.
    *   **Action space limitation:** The fixed action space might restrict the agents from exhibiting a wide range of user behaviors.
    *   **Interaction scope:** The interaction model is primarily limited to user-item, merchant-item, and merchant-user dynamics. It doesn't explicitly model other complex interactions common in real-world ecosystems like user-to-user social influence or the impact of external events.
    *   **Evaluation:** Despite the LLM-based and human evaluations, there is still a need to further validate the simulated behaviors. The human evaluation only looked at a limited number of pairs and all three evaluators were graduate students in AI. A more diverse group would increase the validity of the results.
*   **Potential Influence:** RecInter has the potential to influence the design and evaluation of recommender systems. Its realistic interaction mechanism could lead to better understanding of user behavior and more effective algorithm design. Other researchers in the field could leverage this platform to test or evaluate new algorithms more safely and efficiently.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the combination of novelty and significance, along with the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is justified. The paper builds upon existing agent-based simulation research with a valuable addition. The interaction mechanism is a solid contribution, providing a more realistic simulation environment. The weaknesses related to LLM bias and limited interaction scopes somewhat temper the impact. While the experiments demonstrate potential, additional validation and broader scope are necessary to ensure more real-world applicability.

### Implicit Jailbreak Attacks via Cross-Modal Information Concealment on Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16446v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoxin Wang, Handing Wang, Cong Tian, Yaochu Jin
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) enable powerful cross-modal reasoning capabilities. However, the expanded input space introduces new attack surfaces. Previous jailbreak attacks often inject malicious instructions from text into less aligned modalities, such as vision. As MLLMs increasingly incorporate cross-modal consistency and alignment mechanisms, such explicit attacks become easier to detect and block. In this work, we propose a novel implicit jailbreak framework termed IJA that stealthily embeds malicious instructions into images via least significant bit steganography and couples them with seemingly benign, image-related textual prompts. To further enhance attack effectiveness across diverse MLLMs, we incorporate adversarial suffixes generated by a surrogate model and introduce a template optimization module that iteratively refines both the prompt and embedding based on model feedback. On commercial models like GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5 Pro, our method achieves attack success rates of over 90% using an average of only 3 queries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Implicit Jailbreak Attacks (IJA) to bypass safety alignment mechanisms in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). IJA conceals malicious instructions within images using least significant bit (LSB) steganography, paired with seemingly benign, image-related textual prompts. To enhance attack effectiveness across diverse MLLMs, the framework incorporates adversarial suffixes generated by a surrogate model and an attack template optimization module that iteratively refines both the prompt and embedding based on model feedback. Experiments on commercial models like GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5 Pro demonstrate high attack success rates (over 90%) with minimal queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of existing techniques (LSB steganography, adversarial suffixes) within a tailored framework for MLLM jailbreaking. While neither LSB steganography nor adversarial suffixes are new individually, their strategic integration to implicitly inject instructions into MLLMs through cross-modal reasoning presents a novel approach. The template optimization module adds another layer of adaptability. However, the novelty is incremental, building on established techniques rather than presenting a revolutionary breakthrough.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that even with increasing cross-modal alignment and safety mechanisms in MLLMs, they remain vulnerable to cleverly crafted attacks. The high attack success rates achieved on commercial models underscore the practical significance of the proposed framework. This highlights a critical need for more robust defense strategies. The work is also significant in its focus on implicit attacks, as opposed to explicit adversarial prompts, which are becoming easier to detect. The practical nature of achieving high attack success rates with only 3 queries enhances the significance. The framework's ability to adapt to different MLLM architectures, demonstrated through the template optimization module, further adds to its significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed IJA framework demonstrates high attack success rates on commercial MLLMs.
    *   The framework combines multiple techniques to achieve stealth and adaptability.
    *   The experiments are thorough, using multiple datasets and models, and including ablation studies.
    *   The focus on implicit attacks is a timely and relevant contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components of the framework (LSB steganography, adversarial suffixes) are not entirely novel.
    *   The reliance on a surrogate model introduces additional computational overhead.
    *   The framework is currently limited to image-text multimodal models and LSB-based steganography.
    * The framework could be further enhanced by examining alternative steganographic and embedding approaches.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research on MLLM security by highlighting the vulnerabilities arising from cross-modal reasoning and implicit attacks. It will motivate the development of more effective defense mechanisms against such attacks, particularly those that target steganographic information concealment. The modular design of the framework could also inspire other researchers to explore different combinations of existing techniques to achieve similar goals.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper demonstrates a practically effective attack strategy that circumvents existing safety alignment mechanisms in MLLMs. Its modular framework and focus on implicit attacks makes it a meaningful contribution to the field. However, the incremental nature of its novelty (building on well-established techniques) and the limitations in its scope (image-text models, LSB steganography) prevent it from achieving a higher score.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Psychology-driven LLM Agents for Explainable Panic Prediction on Social Media during Sudden Disaster Events
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16455v1)
- **Authors**: Mengzhu Liu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Chuan Ai, Chen Gao, Xinghong Li, Lingnan He, Kaisheng Lai, Yingfeng Chen, Xin Lu, Yong Li, Quanjun Yin
- **Abstract**: During sudden disaster events, accurately predicting public panic sentiment on social media is crucial for proactive governance and crisis management. Current efforts on this problem face three main challenges: lack of finely annotated data hinders emotion prediction studies, unmodeled risk perception causes prediction inaccuracies, and insufficient interpretability of panic formation mechanisms. We address these issues by proposing a Psychology-driven generative Agent framework (PsychoAgent) for explainable panic prediction based on emotion arousal theory. Specifically, we first construct a fine-grained open panic emotion dataset (namely COPE) via human-large language models (LLMs) collaboration to mitigate semantic bias. Then, we develop a framework integrating cross-domain heterogeneous data grounded in psychological mechanisms to model risk perception and cognitive differences in emotion generation. To enhance interpretability, we design an LLM-based role-playing agent that simulates individual psychological chains through dedicatedly designed prompts. Experimental results on our annotated dataset show that PsychoAgent improves panic emotion prediction performance by 12.6% to 21.7% compared to baseline models. Furthermore, the explainability and generalization of our approach is validated. Crucially, this represents a paradigm shift from opaque "data-driven fitting" to transparent "role-based simulation with mechanistic interpretation" for panic emotion prediction during emergencies. Our implementation is publicly available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PsychoAgent-19DD.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Psychology-driven LLM Agents for Explainable Panic Prediction on Social Media during Sudden Disaster Events":

**Summary:**
The paper addresses the problem of predicting public panic on social media during sudden disaster events. The authors argue that existing approaches suffer from issues like lack of finely annotated data, unmodeled risk perception, and limited interpretability. To address these, they propose a framework called PsychoAgent, which utilizes a psychology-driven generative agent. It constructs a fine-grained panic emotion dataset (COPE) using human-LLM collaboration.  PsychoAgent integrates cross-domain heterogeneous data grounded in psychological mechanisms. It uses LLM-based role-playing agents with dedicated prompts to simulate individual psychological chains related to panic formation. Experimental results on the COPE dataset show improved panic emotion prediction performance compared to baseline models. The paper also validates the explainability and generalization capabilities of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects.
    *   The creation of COPE is a significant contribution, providing a new, finely-grained panic emotion dataset that addresses annotation challenges.
    *   The use of psychology-driven generative agents to model panic formation, incorporating risk perception and cognitive differences, is a fresh approach.
    *   The explainability focus, using role-playing LLMs, adds value compared to opaque data-driven methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper has potential significance for several reasons.
    *   Accurate panic prediction is crucial for disaster management and proactive governance.
    *   The interpretability of the model enables deeper insights into the emotional dynamics during crises.
    *   The work highlights the limitations of current LLMs when directly applied to emotional classification and offers an alternative decoupling strategy.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong motivation for the research problem and clear articulation of the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   Well-defined methodology involving data collection, agent design, and experimental validation.
    *   Thorough experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of PsychoAgent compared to baselines and in ablation studies.
    *   Good writing and clear explanations of complex concepts.
    *   Addresses an important real-world problem with practical implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relying on LLMs introduces potential biases and inaccuracies from the LLM itself, which may not be fully captured in the multi-expert evaluation. While the approach does use a multi-expert verification scheme, it's still limited to the knowledge of the LLMs being used as experts.
    *   The reliance on LLMs that may exhibit political correctness guardrails. The ethical considerations around simulating potential misinformation is not discussed at all.
    *   A more detailed discussion of the computational resources required to train and run the PsychoAgent framework would be helpful. The scalability discussion focuses on model size, but the computational cost of the CoT simulations is unclear.
    *   The generalizability of the COPE dataset is somewhat limited, since it only focuses on Hurricane Sandy.

*   **Overall Impact:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by proposing a novel and interpretable approach for panic prediction on social media. The focus on explainability and the use of psychology-driven modeling are significant advancements in this field. While there are limitations related to the reliance on LLMs, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, and it has the potential to influence future research in disaster management and affective computing.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper achieves a high score due to its significant novelty in approaching panic prediction, its well-defined methodology, and its comprehensive experimental validation. The development of the COPE dataset and the use of a psychology-driven, explainable LLM agent framework represent meaningful advancements. The practical significance for disaster management is also a contributing factor. While weaknesses exist, primarily related to LLM biases and the limited scope of the dataset, they do not significantly detract from the paper's overall contribution. An "8" indicates that the paper is a strong contribution to the field, offering valuable insights and paving the way for future research, but stopping short of being a paradigm shift.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### MAGIC: Motion-Aware Generative Inference via Confidence-Guided LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16456v1)
- **Authors**: Siwei Meng, Yawei Luo, Ping Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in static 3D generation have intensified the demand for physically consistent dynamic 3D content.
