### CTRAP: Embedding Collapse Trap to Safeguard Large Language Models from Harmful Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16559v1)
- **Authors**: Biao Yi, Tiansheng Huang, Baolei Zhang, Tong Li, Lihai Nie, Zheli Liu, Li Shen
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning-as-a-service, while commercially successful for Large Language Model (LLM) providers, exposes models to harmful fine-tuning attacks. As a widely explored defense paradigm against such attacks, unlearning attempts to remove malicious knowledge from LLMs, thereby essentially preventing them from being used to perform malicious tasks. However, we highlight a critical flaw: the powerful general adaptability of LLMs allows them to easily bypass selective unlearning by rapidly relearning or repurposing their capabilities for harmful tasks. To address this fundamental limitation, we propose a paradigm shift: instead of selective removal, we advocate for inducing model collapse--effectively forcing the model to "unlearn everything"--specifically in response to updates characteristic of malicious adaptation. This collapse directly neutralizes the very general capabilities that attackers exploit, tackling the core issue unaddressed by selective unlearning. We introduce the Collapse Trap (CTRAP) as a practical mechanism to implement this concept conditionally. Embedded during alignment, CTRAP pre-configures the model's reaction to subsequent fine-tuning dynamics. If updates during fine-tuning constitute a persistent attempt to reverse safety alignment, the pre-configured trap triggers a progressive degradation of the model's core language modeling abilities, ultimately rendering it inert and useless for the attacker. Crucially, this collapse mechanism remains dormant during benign fine-tuning, ensuring the model's utility and general capabilities are preserved for legitimate users. Extensive empirical results demonstrate that CTRAP effectively counters harmful fine-tuning risks across various LLMs and attack settings, while maintaining high performance in benign scenarios. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CTRAP.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new defense mechanism called Collapse Trap (CTRAP) to safeguard Large Language Models (LLMs) against harmful fine-tuning attacks. The core idea is to embed a conditional collapse mechanism into the model during its safety alignment phase. This trap is designed to remain inactive during benign fine-tuning but to trigger a progressive degradation of the model's core language modeling abilities if the fine-tuning process attempts to reverse safety alignment. The method aims to address the limitation of selective unlearning techniques, which the authors argue can be circumvented by the LLM's general adaptability. CTRAP effectively forces the model to "unlearn everything" in response to malicious adaptation attempts, neutralizing its capabilities. The authors conduct extensive empirical evaluations across various LLMs and attack settings, demonstrating CTRAP's effectiveness in countering harmful fine-tuning risks while preserving performance in benign scenarios.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a novel approach to defending LLMs against harmful fine-tuning attacks. It challenges the prevalent unlearning paradigm by highlighting the LLM's general adaptability as a key weakness. Instead of selectively removing malicious knowledge, the paper proposes inducing a model collapse in response to harmful adaptation attempts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Conceptual Novelty:** The idea of conditional model collapse as a defense mechanism is innovative and addresses a critical vulnerability in existing approaches. The shift from selective unlearning to induced collapse is a significant paradigm shift.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The Collapse Trap (CTRAP) provides a practical and implementable mechanism for realizing the conditional collapse concept.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive empirical results across different LLMs, attack scenarios, and evaluation metrics, providing strong evidence for CTRAP's effectiveness.
    *   **Performance Preservation:** The results demonstrate that CTRAP effectively counters harmful fine-tuning risks while maintaining high performance in benign scenarios.
    *   **Addresses Unlearning Limitations:** Clearly identifies and addresses the limitations of selective unlearning and explains why the proposed approach is superior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The alignment phase has a higher computational cost compared to standard fine-tuning, which while one-time, is still a consideration.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** There are hyperparameters that may need careful tuning for best performance.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The current focus is on protecting pure LLMs, and the paper acknowledges the need to extend the approach to more complex scenarios and applications.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of LLM security and safety. It offers a novel defense strategy that effectively addresses a critical vulnerability in existing approaches. The extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate the practical effectiveness of CTRAP and its potential for real-world deployment.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field, justified by a strong empirical evaluation. The central idea of conditional model collapse offers a promising defense against harmful fine-tuning attacks. While the increased computational cost and need for hyperparameter tuning are weaknesses, the paper's strengths outweigh these limitations. CTRAP's ability to defend against harmful fine-tuning while maintaining performance in benign scenarios, combined with its conceptual novelty, makes it a high-impact contribution. The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research and development in LLM security and safety.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### ScholarBench: A Bilingual Benchmark for Abstraction, Comprehension, and Reasoning Evaluation in Academic Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16566v1)
- **Authors**: Dongwon Noh, Donghyeok Koh, Junghun Yuk, Gyuwan Kim, Jaeyong Lee, Kyungtae Lim, Cheoneum Park
- **Abstract**: Prior benchmarks for evaluating the domain-specific knowledge of large language models (LLMs) lack the scalability to handle complex academic tasks. To address this, we introduce \texttt{ScholarBench}, a benchmark centered on deep expert knowledge and complex academic problem-solving, which evaluates the academic reasoning ability of LLMs and is constructed through a three-step process. \texttt{ScholarBench} targets more specialized and logically complex contexts derived from academic literature, encompassing five distinct problem types. Unlike prior benchmarks, \texttt{ScholarBench} evaluates the abstraction, comprehension, and reasoning capabilities of LLMs across eight distinct research domains. To ensure high-quality evaluation data, we define category-specific example attributes and design questions that are aligned with the characteristic research methodologies and discourse structures of each domain. Additionally, this benchmark operates as an English-Korean bilingual dataset, facilitating simultaneous evaluation for linguistic capabilities of LLMs in both languages. The benchmark comprises 5,031 examples in Korean and 5,309 in English, with even state-of-the-art models like o3-mini achieving an average evaluation score of only 0.543, demonstrating the challenging nature of this benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ScholarBench: A Bilingual Benchmark for Abstraction, Comprehension, and Reasoning Evaluation in Academic Contexts":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScholarBench, a new bilingual (English-Korean) benchmark designed to evaluate the abstraction, comprehension, and reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in complex academic contexts. The benchmark comprises a curated dataset of over 5000 examples in each language across eight distinct research domains. Unlike existing benchmarks, ScholarBench focuses on tasks requiring deep expert knowledge and problem-solving skills, targeting more specialized contexts derived from academic literature. The paper details the benchmark construction process, question design, and the evaluation of several LLMs. Experimental results demonstrate the challenging nature of the benchmark, even for state-of-the-art models, highlighting its potential to drive further LLM development in academic fields.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The benchmark addresses a clear gap in existing LLM evaluation by focusing on complex academic reasoning across diverse disciplines. The bilingual nature (English-Korean) is also a valuable contribution, enabling cross-lingual knowledge transfer assessment.
    *   **Rigorous Design:** The paper provides a thorough explanation of the benchmark construction methodology, including detailed attribute definitions and alignment with specific research methodologies.
    *   **Domain Diversity:** The coverage of eight academic categories with fine-grained attributes ensures a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs' domain-specific reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Challenging Nature:** The experimental results demonstrate that ScholarBench poses a significant challenge for current LLMs, even high-performing ones, making it a valuable tool for identifying areas of improvement.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Multimodality:** As the paper acknowledges, the data sources are limited to paragraph text, excluding multimodal elements such as figures, tables, and diagrams, which are integral to scholarly papers.
    *   **Scope Limitation:** The benchmark primarily focuses on English-language academic settings, limiting its generalizability beyond standardized academic domains and excluding educational contexts with less formal assessments.
    *   **RAG Integration:** The benchmark does not explicitly evaluate the Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) capabilities of LLMs, a crucial aspect for knowledge-intensive tasks. This means it cannot accurately measure the performance of the retrieval phase necessary before solving problems in a RAG setup.

*   **Significance:**

    *   ScholarBench contributes to the field by providing a more comprehensive and challenging evaluation framework for LLMs in academic contexts.
    *   It can help identify specific strengths and weaknesses of LLMs across different domains and question types, guiding future research and development efforts.
    *   The benchmark facilitates cross-lingual evaluation and analysis, enabling the development of more robust and versatile LLMs for diverse research applications.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The benchmark is likely to stimulate further research in developing LLMs with improved domain-specific knowledge and reasoning abilities.
    *   It could become a standard evaluation tool for assessing the suitability of LLMs in academic research, education, and specialized fields.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

ScholarBench represents a significant advancement in LLM evaluation by focusing on complex academic reasoning and problem-solving skills. Its rigorous design, domain diversity, and challenging nature make it a valuable tool for driving future LLM development in specialized areas. The bilingual aspect is a unique and important contribution.  However, the limitations concerning multimodality and limited scope beyond English standardized contexts prevent it from receiving a higher score.  While acknowledging these weaknesses, the benchmark's strengths outweigh its shortcomings, making it a substantial and influential contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Finetuning-Activated Backdoors in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16567v1)
- **Authors**: Thibaud Gloaguen, Mark Vero, Robin Staab, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: Finetuning openly accessible Large Language Models (LLMs) has become standard practice for achieving task-specific performance improvements. Until now, finetuning has been regarded as a controlled and secure process in which training on benign datasets led to predictable behaviors. In this paper, we demonstrate for the first time that an adversary can create poisoned LLMs that initially appear benign but exhibit malicious behaviors once finetuned by downstream users. To this end, our proposed attack, FAB (Finetuning-Activated Backdoor), poisons an LLM via meta-learning techniques to simulate downstream finetuning, explicitly optimizing for the emergence of malicious behaviors in the finetuned models. At the same time, the poisoned LLM is regularized to retain general capabilities and to exhibit no malicious behaviors prior to finetuning. As a result, when users finetune the seemingly benign model on their own datasets, they unknowingly trigger its hidden backdoor behavior. We demonstrate the effectiveness of FAB across multiple LLMs and three target behaviors: unsolicited advertising, refusal, and jailbreakability. Additionally, we show that FAB-backdoors are robust to various finetuning choices made by the user (e.g., dataset, number of steps, scheduler). Our findings challenge prevailing assumptions about the security of finetuning, revealing yet another critical attack vector exploiting the complexities of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Finetuning-Activated Backdoors in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack called FAB (Finetuning-Activated Backdoor) that allows an adversary to create "backdoored" Large Language Models (LLMs). These models appear benign initially but exhibit malicious behaviors *after* being finetuned by downstream users on their own datasets. The attack uses meta-learning techniques to poison the LLM, simulating downstream finetuning and optimizing for the emergence of malicious behaviors in the finetuned models. The poisoned LLM is regularized to retain general capabilities and avoid exhibiting malicious behaviors prior to finetuning. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of FAB across multiple LLMs and target behaviors (advertising injection, refusal, jailbreaking), showing robustness to different finetuning choices made by users.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of a backdoor *activated by finetuning* is highly novel and represents a significant departure from traditional backdoor attacks which rely on poisoned training data during the target model's initial training phase or require specific input triggers during inference. Prior work had explored backdoors triggered by quantization, this work introduces a completely different and more practically relevant trigger (finetuning).

*   **Significance:** This work highlights a critical and previously underappreciated security vulnerability in the widespread practice of LLM finetuning. Given the prevalence of model sharing and finetuning, the implications are substantial. A successful FAB attack could have widespread and difficult-to-detect consequences, as it shifts the point of attack from the model trainer to end-users. The findings challenge the assumption that users have full control over the outcome when finetuning a publicly available model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The proposed FAB attack is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering different LLMs, target behaviors, and a wide range of finetuning configurations, demonstrating the attack's robustness.
    *   The paper addresses an important practical issue and has significant implications for the security of LLMs.
    *   The paper explores potential mitigations and limitations, demonstrating awareness of the broader impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper demonstrates robustness, the attack's effectiveness depends on carefully tuned parameters, datasets, and loss functions during the poisoning stage, as noted in the limitations.  The overhead related to the careful adjustments makes it hard to launch.
    *   The study is limited to smaller models (<=3B parameters). Scaling FAB to significantly larger models is a computational challenge, and how well the attack generalizes remains an open question.
    *   The study focuses mainly on supervised finetuning (SFT). The results may not directly generalize to other adaptation techniques, such as reinforcement learning or logit distillation.
    *   The potential negative societal impacts are discussed, but the ethical implications of releasing such an attack, even for research purposes, should be considered in more depth.

*   **Impact:** This paper is likely to have a high impact on the LLM security community. It introduces a new attack vector that warrants further investigation and development of specialized defenses. It could also lead to changes in how LLMs are shared and finetuned, with increased scrutiny and security measures. The paper should motivate research into novel defenses and mitigation strategies. It already has a positive effect in raising awareness.

**Justification:**

The FAB attack is highly novel and relevant. The paper offers an extensive experimental evaluation, highlighting the effectiveness of the FAB. However, the constraints of the attack, the small models studied, and narrow exploration into other adaptation techniques restrict the applicability of the paper. These constraints reduce its impact.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### URLs Help, Topics Guide: Understanding Metadata Utility in LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16570v1)
- **Authors**: Dongyang Fan, Vinko Sabolčec, Martin Jaggi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are commonly pretrained on vast corpora of text without utilizing contextual metadata such as source, quality, or topic, leading to a context-free learning paradigm. While recent studies suggest that adding metadata like URL information as context (i.e., auxiliary inputs not used in the loss calculation) can improve training efficiency and downstream performance, they offer limited understanding of which types of metadata are truly effective and under what conditions. In this work, we conduct a systematic evaluation and find that not all metadata types contribute equally. Only URL context speeds up training, whereas quality scores and topic/format domain information offer no clear benefit. Furthermore, the improved downstream performances of URL conditioning emerge only when longer prompts are used at inference time. In addition, we demonstrate that context-aware pretraining enables more controllable generation than context-free pretraining, in a classifier-free guidance fashion. Although topic and format metadata do not accelerate training, they are effective for steering outputs, offering human-interpretable control over generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the effectiveness of using different types of metadata (URL, quality scores, topic/format information) as context during LLM pretraining.  It investigates whether incorporating this metadata can accelerate training, improve downstream performance, and enable more controllable generation. The key findings are that:

*   Only URL information consistently speeds up pretraining.
*   The benefits of URL conditioning for downstream tasks are only apparent with longer prompts.
*   Context-aware pretraining allows for more controllable generation using classifier-free guidance, especially with topic/format information, even though these don't improve training speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The study builds upon prior work by Allen-Zhu & Li and Gao et al., who suggested the benefits of URL domains. The paper's main contribution lies in its systematic evaluation of *various* metadata types (quality scores, topic/format domains in addition to URLs), and an analysis of *when* these benefits manifest (e.g., longer prompts at inference). The observation that simply increasing the context length (by concatenating various metadata) doesn't translate to improvement is also valuable. Demonstrating the utility of these features for controllable generation offers additional insights. However, the individual components of the study, when viewed separately, do not reflect high novelty as some findings align with previous works. However, the work's strength lies in bringing these individual aspects into one single cohesive experiment.

*   **Significance:** The findings have practical implications for LLM pretraining. Identifying URL information as a key ingredient for speeding up pretraining can save computational resources. The observation that longer prompts are needed to realize the downstream performance gains provides a practical guideline for using such models. The work also explores important aspects of controllability during generation which is increasingly important for real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic and well-controlled experiments.
    *   Evaluation on standard downstream benchmarks.
    *   Analysis of different metadata types and their combinations.
    *   Investigation of both pretraining speed and downstream performance.
    *   Demonstration of controllable generation through context-aware pretraining.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The model size (1.5B) and the dataset size (100B) are relatively modest compared to current state-of-the-art LLMs. This limits the generalizability of the results to larger models.
    *   The dataset used, Fineweb-Edu, although high-quality and available with rich metadata, may not be representative of the diverse data used in typical LLM pretraining.
    *   The evaluation for controllable generation (LLM judge) relies on GPT-4 as a judge. While convenient, this introduces biases and limitations. It would be useful to also include human evaluation to further support the results.
    *   The work is limited by the selection of metadata available in the dataset which might differ from alternative data sources.

*   **Impact:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the ongoing research on efficient and controllable LLM pretraining. The identification of URL information's importance is a practical finding that could influence pretraining strategies. The investigation of controllable generation adds to the understanding of how to steer LLMs in desired directions. While the study's scope is limited by model and data size, the insights are likely to be relevant as the field moves towards larger models and more sophisticated pretraining techniques.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution through its systematic investigation of metadata in LLM pretraining. It's valuable for confirming some prior observations (URL benefit with longer prompts) and adding nuances (demonstrating that just any context length doesn't help, that controllability is enabled). The experimental setup is well executed, and the findings are clearly presented. However, its limited novelty, restricted scope (model/dataset sizes), and reliance on GPT-4 as a judge prevent it from being considered a highly significant breakthrough. The work could benefit from scaling the experiments and adding human evaluation to further strengthen its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Large Language Model-Empowered Interactive Load Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16577v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Zuo, Dalin Qin, Yi Wang
- **Abstract**: The growing complexity of power systems has made accurate load forecasting more important than ever. An increasing number of advanced load forecasting methods have been developed. However, the static design of current methods offers no mechanism for human-model interaction. As the primary users of forecasting models, system operators often find it difficult to understand and apply these advanced models, which typically requires expertise in artificial intelligence (AI). This also prevents them from incorporating their experience and real-world contextual understanding into the forecasting process. Recent breakthroughs in large language models (LLMs) offer a new opportunity to address this issue. By leveraging their natural language understanding and reasoning capabilities, we propose an LLM-based multi-agent collaboration framework to bridge the gap between human operators and forecasting models. A set of specialized agents is designed to perform different tasks in the forecasting workflow and collaborate via a dedicated communication mechanism. This framework embeds interactive mechanisms throughout the load forecasting pipeline, reducing the technical threshold for non-expert users and enabling the integration of human experience. Our experiments demonstrate that the interactive load forecasting accuracy can be significantly improved when users provide proper insight in key stages. Our cost analysis shows that the framework remains affordable, making it practical for real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an LLM-based multi-agent collaborative framework to improve interactive load forecasting. Recognizing the limitations of static forecasting models and the challenges faced by system operators in applying advanced AI models, the framework aims to bridge the gap between human operators and forecasting models. The system employs specialized agents that handle different tasks in the forecasting pipeline (data preparation, model training, deployment), interacting with each other through a dedicated messaging system and directly with the human operator via a Task Manager. This allows operators to provide domain insights and contextual knowledge, enhancing the forecasting process.  Experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that user interaction improves forecasting accuracy and that the framework's cost, in terms of token consumption, is affordable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a real and important problem:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in the power systems domain – how to make sophisticated AI forecasting tools more accessible and user-friendly for operators.  The recognition that system operators possess valuable contextual knowledge that's difficult to capture algorithmically is a key insight.
*   **Innovative approach:** The use of LLMs and a multi-agent system to facilitate human-model interaction in load forecasting is novel. It moves beyond simply using LLMs to directly perform forecasting tasks, instead leveraging them as intelligent assistants.
*   **Well-defined framework:** The proposed architecture with its specialized agents, communication mechanism, and user interface (visualization panel) appears well-structured and thoughtfully designed.  The detail provided regarding agent profiles, memory, workflows, and actions gives credibility to the framework's potential for practical implementation.
*   **Demonstrated performance improvement:** Experimental results show improvements in forecasting accuracy when users provide input, suggesting the framework is effective in integrating human expertise.  The efficiency gains (fewer trials) with user input also highlight the value of the approach.
*   **Cost analysis:** The inclusion of a token consumption analysis is crucial.  It acknowledges a practical limitation of using LLMs (API costs) and provides evidence that the framework remains affordable, increasing its likelihood of adoption.
*   **Extensible Design:** The modular architecture allows for easy integration of new models, feature engineering approaches, and post-processing strategies.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM comparisons:** The evaluation relies primarily on GPT-4o. While GPT-4o is a powerful LLM, the paper would be stronger if it compared performance across a range of LLMs (e.g., different OpenAI models, open-source alternatives). This would provide a better understanding of the framework's sensitivity to the underlying LLM and its ability to generalize.
*   **User proficiency:** The results indicate that users with some AI knowledge can improve forecasting outcomes. However, the paper does not explore the level of user knowledge or training required to make meaningful contributions. What if the user has limited or no understanding of AI? Is it possible to guide them?
*   **Generalizability:** The framework is evaluated on two datasets. Assessing its performance across a wider range of geographical locations and power system configurations would strengthen the claim of general applicability. Further analysis of the system's robustness to different data qualities and patterns would also be beneficial.
*   **Subjectivity in User Input:** User interaction, while advantageous, introduces subjectivity. What is the potential for user bias, and how is that addressed in the framework? A discussion of potential mitigation strategies (e.g., guidance tools, consistency checks) would be valuable.
*   **Lack of Ablation Study:** The paper lacks a comparative analysis that isolates the influence of various framework elements. An ablation study evaluating the effects of multiple agents, human-in-the-loop interactions, and customized loss functions would improve insight into how these components contribute to overall performance gains.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by exploring a new paradigm for load forecasting that emphasizes human-model collaboration. The framework has the potential to democratize the use of advanced forecasting techniques and improve their real-world applicability. The study provides a practical approach to address this by focusing on making AI systems more accessible and responsive to human expertise.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a novel and promising approach to load forecasting by integrating LLMs into an interactive framework. The experimental results and cost analysis support the feasibility of the proposed framework. However, the paper could be improved by:

*   More extensive LLM comparisons
*   A more detailed analysis of the user input and proficiency requirements.
*   Assessing framework generalizability by increasing datasets
*   Addresing and mitigating user bias and a more in-depth evaluation of contributions by system components.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bridging the Dynamic Perception Gap: Training-Free Draft Chain-of-Thought for Dynamic Multimodal Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16579v1)
- **Authors**: Siqu Ou, Hongcheng Liu, Pingjie Wang, Yusheng Liao, Chuan Xuan, Yanfeng Wang, Yu Wang
- **Abstract**: While chains-of-thought (CoT) have advanced complex reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), existing methods remain confined to text or static visual domains, often faltering in dynamic spatial reasoning tasks. To bridge this gap, we present GRASSLAND, a novel maze navigation benchmark designed to evaluate dynamic spatial reasoning. Our experiments show that augmenting textual reasoning chains with dynamic visual drafts, overlaid on input images, significantly outperforms conventional approaches, offering new insights into spatial reasoning in evolving environments. To generalize this capability, we propose D2R (Dynamic Draft-Augmented Reasoning), a training-free framework that seamlessly integrates textual CoT with corresponding visual drafts into MLLMs. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that D2R consistently enhances performance across diverse tasks, establishing a robust baseline for dynamic spatial reasoning without requiring model fine-tuning. Project is open at https://github.com/Cratileo/D2R.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRASSLAND, a novel benchmark for evaluating dynamic multimodal spatial reasoning. It focuses on the limitations of existing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in tasks requiring reasoning about dynamically changing visual environments. The authors propose a Draft Chain-of-Thought (Draft CoT) approach that augments textual reasoning with visual drafts overlaid on input images, improving performance.  Furthermore, they present a training-free framework called D2R (Dynamic Draft-Augmented Reasoning) to integrate textual CoT with visual drafts into MLLMs, enhancing performance across diverse tasks without model fine-tuning.  The paper highlights improvements in tasks like maze navigation and judgment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The introduction of the GRASSLAND benchmark is a valuable contribution.  While maze navigation isn't entirely new, the dynamic elements (changing lava/water positions) and the explicit focus on dynamic spatial reasoning make it a relevant and challenging testbed for MLLMs. The idea of dynamically updating drafts on input images to guide reasoning is also a worthwhile innovation.  D2R, as a training-free framework, offers practical benefits for leveraging existing MLLMs. However, a similar training-free prompt engineering exists in many vision-language models.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial gap in MLLM research: reasoning in dynamic visual environments.  Many existing benchmarks focus on static images or text-based reasoning.  The improvements demonstrated by Draft CoT and D2R, particularly in the Maze Navigation task, suggest a potential for MLLMs to be more effectively applied to real-world problems like robotics, autonomous driving, and other dynamic environments. The emphasis on a training-free approach makes the work immediately accessible and impactful. However, the method relies on external tools, it doesn't fundamentally improve the model's intrinsic capabilities.

* **Strengths:**
    * The GRASSLAND benchmark provides a valuable resource for the community.
    * Draft CoT and D2R improve performance in dynamic spatial reasoning tasks.
    * The training-free nature of D2R enhances its applicability.
    * The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current MLLMs in dynamic environments.
    * Experiments are thorough, comparing against several baselines.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on external tools to generate visual drafts might limit its scalability and efficiency.
    * The performance gains are not uniformly distributed across all models, suggesting D2R might be more effective with stronger base models.
    * The paper does not deeply analyze the types of errors MLLMs make in the dynamic setting, limiting the insight into *why* D2R is effective, it just shows *that* it's effective.
    * It is more of an advanced prompting method rather than an advancement of MLLM itself.
    * The novelty is arguable, as many similar methods, e.g., plug-and-play modules or advanced prompting methods, exist.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of new benchmarks and evaluation metrics for dynamic spatial reasoning in MLLMs. It might also encourage the exploration of methods that integrate external tools and visual drafts to improve reasoning in complex environments. However, its limited contribution on the MLLM itself might limit its overall influence.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field, but it has limitations that prevent it from being considered a groundbreaking work.  The introduction of GRASSLAND and the demonstrated improvement of D2R are valuable. However, the reliance on external tools, the uneven performance gains across models, and the lack of deep error analysis reduce its overall impact. The novelty, while present, is not revolutionary, it's more an incremental improvement leveraging existing resources and methods. The lack of new insights beyond the observed performance improvements further limits its significance.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### O$^2$-Searcher: A Searching-based Agent Model for Open-Domain Open-Ended Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16582v1)
- **Authors**: Jianbiao Mei, Tao Hu, Daocheng Fu, Licheng Wen, Xuemeng Yang, Rong Wu, Pinlong Cai, Xing Gao, Yu Yang, Chengjun Xie, Botian Shi, Yong Liu, Yu Qiao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their advancements, are fundamentally limited by their static parametric knowledge, hindering performance on tasks requiring open-domain up-to-date information. While enabling LLMs to interact with external knowledge environments is a promising solution, current efforts primarily address closed-end problems. Open-ended questions, which characterized by lacking a standard answer or providing non-unique and diverse answers, remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we present O$^2$-Searcher, a novel search agent leveraging reinforcement learning to effectively tackle both open-ended and closed-ended questions in the open domain. O$^2$-Searcher leverages an efficient, locally simulated search environment for dynamic knowledge acquisition, effectively decoupling the external world knowledge from model's sophisticated reasoning processes. It employs a unified training mechanism with meticulously designed reward functions, enabling the agent to identify problem types and adapt different answer generation strategies. Furthermore, to evaluate performance on complex open-ended tasks, we construct O$^2$-QA, a high-quality benchmark featuring 300 manually curated, multi-domain open-ended questions with associated web page caches. Extensive experiments show that O$^2$-Searcher, using only a 3B model, significantly surpasses leading LLM agents on O$^2$-QA. It also achieves SOTA results on various closed-ended QA benchmarks against similarly-sized models, while performing on par with much larger ones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, "O2-Searcher: A Searching-based Agent Model for Open-Domain Open-Ended Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces O2-Searcher, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based search agent designed for both open-ended and closed-ended question answering in open-domain settings. O2-Searcher aims to overcome the limitations of current Large Language Models (LLMs) that are bounded by their static parametric knowledge, particularly when dealing with tasks requiring up-to-date information or exploratory questions. The key innovations include:

*   **Local Search Environment:** A simulated search environment allows the agent to efficiently acquire external knowledge. It caches web pages and structured data to avoid slow API responses and high costs.
*   **Unified Training Mechanism:** An RL framework with carefully designed reward functions guides the agent to handle both open-ended and closed-ended questions adaptively.  The reward functions consider format correctness, factual accuracy, information diversity, and query distinctiveness.
*   **O2-QA Benchmark:**  A new, manually curated benchmark of 300 open-ended questions across diverse domains (AI, philosophy, etc.) with associated web page caches to evaluate LLMs' performance.
*   **Superior Performance:** Experimental results show that a 3B parameter O2-Searcher outperforms state-of-the-art LLM agents on the O2-QA benchmark and achieves competitive or state-of-the-art results on various closed-ended QA benchmarks against similarly-sized models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   **Focus on Open-Ended QA:**  The research shifts the focus of LLM-based search agents from predominantly closed-ended QA to more complex and under-explored open-ended scenarios.
    *   **Unified RL Framework:** The use of a unified RL framework capable of handling both question types adaptively is a valuable contribution. The specific reward function design to encourage diverse query generation and high-quality answer synthesis is interesting.
    *   **O2-QA Benchmark:**  The creation of a high-quality, manually curated benchmark specifically designed for open-ended QA is significant. It addresses the lack of appropriate evaluation datasets in this area.
    *   **Efficient Local Search Environment:** The simulation of search engine API interaction with pre-caching and locally hosted infrastructure represents a pragmatic optimization to training efficiency.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Addresses a Real Limitation:** The work tackles a fundamental limitation of LLMs – their dependence on static knowledge – and proposes a viable solution.
    *   **Practical Application:** The ability to handle open-ended questions has broad applicability in real-world scenarios where definitive answers are not always available or the user seeks comprehensive insights.
    *   **Benchmarking and Progress:**  The O2-QA benchmark enables rigorous evaluation and comparison of different approaches to open-ended QA, which can drive further research in this area.
    *   **Resource-Efficient:** Demonstrating strong performance with a relatively small 3B parameter model suggests the approach is resource-efficient.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of open-domain open-ended QA and its importance.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed O2-Searcher agent is well-designed, combining RL, knowledge retrieval, and language modeling.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, using both open-ended and closed-ended benchmarks and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Manual Curation:** The O2-QA benchmark, while valuable, relies on manual curation of questions and web page caches, which introduces potential bias and limits scalability.
    *   **Fixed Web Page Cache:** The use of a fixed web page cache in the local search environment limits the ability to evaluate how the agent deals with dynamic and evolving information or misinformation. This is a core characteristic of open-ended scenarios that isn't being fully explored.
    *   **Scalability Validation:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on a 3B parameter model. The claims regarding scalability remain to be fully validated on much larger LLMs, where potentially different dynamics might emerge.
    *   **Sensitivity to External Noise:** As the authors themselves mention, the dependence on external search results makes the model susceptible to environmental noise and misinformation. There appears to be limited analysis into failure scenarios that are induced by unreliable sources, and strategies for mitigating such errors.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper is likely to influence the direction of research on LLM-based search agents. It will encourage more work on open-ended QA.
    *   The O2-QA benchmark is likely to become a valuable resource for the community.
    *   The proposed RL framework could be adopted and extended by other researchers.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing the under-explored area of open-domain open-ended question answering. The novel components of the O2-Searcher architecture—particularly the unified RL training, the efficient local search environment, and the introduction of the O2-QA benchmark—advance the state-of-the-art and provide a valuable framework for future research. The experiments are comprehensive and the results are promising, especially concerning resource efficiency. The weaknesses, primarily related to limitations in the benchmark's scope and dependence on external sources, are not critical enough to negate the paper's overall impact. Therefore, a high score is warranted, reflecting the paper's innovation and potential to stimulate further progress in the field.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Survey on the Application of Large Language Models in Scenario-Based Testing of Automated Driving Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16587v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Zhao, Ji Zhou, Dong Bi, Tomislav Mihalj, Jia Hu, Arno Eichberger
- **Abstract**: The safety and reliability of Automated Driving Systems (ADSs) must be validated prior to large-scale deployment. Among existing validation approaches, scenario-based testing has been regarded as a promising method to improve testing efficiency and reduce associated costs. Recently, the emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has introduced new opportunities to reinforce this approach. While an increasing number of studies have explored the use of LLMs in the field of automated driving, a dedicated review focusing on their application within scenario-based testing remains absent. This survey addresses this gap by systematically categorizing the roles played by LLMs across various phased of scenario-based testing, drawing from both academic research and industrial practice. In addition, key characteristics of LLMs and corresponding usage strategies are comprehensively summarized. The paper concludes by outlining five open challenges and potential research directions. To support ongoing research efforts, a continuously updated repository of recent advancements and relevant open-source tools is made available at: https://github.com/ftgTUGraz/LLM4ADSTest.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a survey of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in scenario-based testing of Automated Driving Systems (ADSs). It categorizes the roles LLMs play across different phases of the testing workflow (scenario sourcing, generation, selection, execution, and assessment).  The survey summarizes LLM types, usage strategies, associated tasks, and integrated simulators.  It also identifies key challenges, such as LLM hallucination, limited simulation platform integration, a lack of domain-specific models, insufficient attention to scenario databases, and industrial application gaps. Finally, it outlines potential research directions. A supporting repository of recent advancements and open-source tools is also provided.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper claims to be the first comprehensive survey focusing specifically on LLMs within the *scenario-based testing* of ADSs. While other surveys touch upon LLMs in AD or scenario-based testing in general, the paper attempts to narrow the scope to the intersection of these two areas. This specialization provides added value, as this is where the synergistic benefits are realized. However, some related surveys in the AD domain exist and the incremental novelty should be carefully considered.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the growing importance of both LLMs and scenario-based testing in the AD domain. LLMs offer potential for automating and improving various aspects of scenario generation and analysis. The paper provides a valuable overview of the current landscape, highlighting both the opportunities and challenges, which is important for guiding future research and development. By systematically categorizing applications and analyzing strategies, the survey can help researchers and practitioners better understand the role of LLMs. The identification of open challenges also makes a helpful contribution to future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Structured Taxonomy:** The paper develops a clear taxonomy based on the phases of scenario-based testing, allowing for a systematic analysis.
*   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey seems reasonably comprehensive in its coverage of relevant literature, as demonstrated by the substantial list of references, covering a wide array of tasks.
*   **Practical Focus:** The inclusion of industrial perspectives and identified challenges adds practical relevance.
*   **Open-Source Contribution:** Providing a continuously updated repository is a valuable resource for the community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Potential Overlap:** The level of true "firstness" may need more rigorous justification. Other surveys address LLMs in AD and scenario-based testing individually. A better argument to justify why these existing efforts fall short needs to be made. The survey’s introduction and claim of firstness might need more critical validation.
*   **Lack of in-depth critical analysis of individual papers:** The survey is more descriptive, rather than critical. It describes what each study did, but could go further in analyzing the strengths and weaknesses of each approach, comparing different strategies, and drawing more insightful conclusions.
*   **Limited Industrial Adoption:** The paper notes limited industrial adoption. Further investigation and a discussion of the reasons *why* adoption is slow and strategies to overcome this gap would enhance the value.
*   **Emerging Field:** The area is rapidly evolving, so the survey will likely require frequent updates to remain relevant.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant and rapidly growing area. The systematic categorization and comprehensive overview are helpful contributions. However, the main weaknesses relate to the incremental novelty of the survey in relation to other works in AD and LLM literature. The lack of deeper critical evaluation and limited real-world validation via strong industrial adoption reduce the overall impact. While valuable, it's not a groundbreaking survey.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond LLMs: An Exploration of Small Open-source Language Models in Logging Statement Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16590v1)
- **Authors**: Renyi Zhong, Yichen Li, Guangba Yu, Wenwei Gu, Jinxi Kuang, Yintong Huo, Michael R. Lyu
- **Abstract**: Effective software maintenance heavily relies on high-quality logging statements, but manual logging is challenging, error-prone, and insufficiently standardized, often leading to inconsistent log quality. While large language models have shown promise in automatic logging, they introduce concerns regarding privacy, resource intensity, and adaptability to specific enterprise needs. To tackle these limitations, this paper empirically investigates whether Small Open-source Language Models (SOLMs) could become a viable alternative via proper exploitation. Specifically, we conduct a large-scale empirical study on four prominent SOLMs, systematically evaluating the impacts of various interaction strategies, parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques, model sizes, and model types in automatic logging. Our key findings reveal that Retrieval-Augmented Generation significantly enhances performance, and LoRA is a highly effective PEFT technique. While larger SOLMs tend to perform better, this involves a trade-off with computational resources, and instruct-tuned SOLMs generally surpass their base counterparts. Notably, fine-tuned SOLMs, particularly Qwen2.5-coder-14B, outperformed existing specialized tools and LLM baselines in accurately predicting logging locations and generating high-quality statements, a conclusion supported by traditional evaluation metrics and LLM-as-a-judge evaluations. Furthermore, SOLMs also demonstrated robust generalization across diverse, unseen code repositories.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper explores the use of small, open-source language models (SOLMs) as an alternative to large language models (LLMs) for automated logging statement generation. It addresses concerns about privacy, resource intensity, and adaptability associated with LLMs by systematically evaluating the performance of four prominent SOLMs (LLaMA, Mistral, CodeLLaMA, and Qwen2.5coder) using various interaction strategies, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques, model sizes, and model types. The study finds that retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and LoRA-based fine-tuning significantly enhance performance. Larger, instruct-tuned SOLMs, specifically Qwen2.5-coder-14B, outperform existing specialized tools and LLM baselines in terms of logging location accuracy and statement quality. The paper also demonstrates that SOLMs exhibit strong generalization capabilities across diverse code repositories. The authors provide their code and data.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in its systematic and comprehensive empirical evaluation of SOLMs for automated logging statement generation. While previous work has explored LLMs for this task, this paper is the first to specifically focus on SOLMs, address their limitations, and demonstrate their potential as a viable alternative. The exploration of various interaction strategies and PEFT techniques tailored to SOLMs is also a novel contribution. Also, the rigorous evaluation of LLM judged scores, and the detailed analysis with Venn diagrams help in the analysis of quality and overlaps among configurations

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for software development. Automated logging can improve software maintenance, but LLMs raise privacy and resource concerns. The paper's findings demonstrate that SOLMs can achieve comparable or even superior performance to LLMs while mitigating these concerns. This makes automated logging more accessible and sustainable for organizations with limited resources or strict data privacy requirements. The study also provides valuable insights into the optimal strategies for utilizing SOLMs in this domain. The investigation about the Apache logging practices is also a plus.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation of various SOLMs, interaction strategies, and PEFT techniques.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for software developers and organizations seeking to improve logging practices.
*   **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the experimental design, results, and analysis.
*   **Address Limitations:** The paper acknowledges and addresses the limitations of SOLMs and provides directions for future research.
*   **Public Code and Data:**  Sharing the code and data fosters reproducibility and facilitates further research in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Bias:** The dataset is primarily Java-based, limiting the generalizability of the results to other programming languages.
*   **SOLM selection:** Although many SOLMS were considered, the field moves quickly and it may be necessary to include more recent models in future evaluations.
*   **Hyperparameter Optimization:** The study acknowledges that a more exhaustive hyperparameter search could further enhance model performance. While the chosen parameters are justified, they might not be universally optimal.

**Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in automated logging and the application of SOLMs in software engineering tasks. It provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating SOLMs and offers insights into the optimal strategies for utilizing them. It also highlights the importance of addressing privacy and resource concerns in automated software development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed study with novel insights and practical implications. The authors make a significant contribution by demonstrating the potential of SOLMs for automated logging statement generation, addressing key limitations of LLMs and offering a more accessible and sustainable solution. While the study has some limitations regarding dataset bias and hyperparameter optimization, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses.

### Evaluating Large Language Model with Knowledge Oriented Language Specific Simple Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16591v1)
- **Authors**: Bowen Jiang, Runchuan Zhu, Jiang Wu, Zinco Jiang, Yifan He, Junyuan Gao, Jia Yu, Rui Min, Yinfan Wang, Haote Yang, Songyang Zhang, Dahua Lin, Lijun Wu, Conghui He
- **Abstract**: We introduce KoLasSimpleQA, the first benchmark evaluating the multilingual factual ability of Large Language Models (LLMs). Inspired by existing research, we created the question set with features such as single knowledge point coverage, absolute objectivity, unique answers, and temporal stability. These questions enable efficient evaluation using the LLM-as-judge paradigm, testing both the LLMs' factual memory and self-awareness ("know what they don't know"). KoLasSimpleQA expands existing research in two key dimensions: (1) Breadth (Multilingual Coverage): It includes 9 languages, supporting global applicability evaluation. (2) Depth (Dual Domain Design): It covers both the general domain (global facts) and the language-specific domain (such as history, culture, and regional traditions) for a comprehensive assessment of multilingual capabilities. We evaluated mainstream LLMs, including traditional LLM and emerging Large Reasoning Models. Results show significant performance differences between the two domains, particularly in performance metrics, ranking, calibration, and robustness. This highlights the need for targeted evaluation and optimization in multilingual contexts. We hope KoLasSimpleQA will help the research community better identify LLM capability boundaries in multilingual contexts and provide guidance for model optimization. We will release KoLasSimpleQA at https://github.com/opendatalab/KoLasSimpleQA .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Evaluating Large Language Model with Knowledge Oriented Language Specific Simple Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KoLasSimpleQA, a new benchmark for evaluating the factual knowledge of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in multilingual settings.  The benchmark is designed to test both general (global facts) and language-specific knowledge (e.g., history, culture, traditions) in nine languages. Key features of KoLasSimpleQA include:

*   **Multilingual Breadth:** Covers nine languages, addressing limitations of existing benchmarks focused primarily on English and Chinese.
*   **Dual-Domain Depth:** Includes questions probing both general world knowledge and knowledge specific to particular languages and cultures.
*   **Simple Fact-Based Questions:** Uses short, objectively answerable questions, making evaluation using the LLM-as-judge paradigm efficient.
*   **Emphasis on Self-Awareness:** Evaluates the LLM's ability to recognize when it lacks the knowledge to answer a question accurately ("know what they don't know").
*  **Reverse Knowledge Memorization Robustness**: Uses QA pairs with reversed relationship to test for this capability.

The authors evaluate a range of LLMs, including traditional and emerging Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), using this benchmark. The results highlight performance differences between general and language-specific domains, issues with translating non-English queries, poor calibration, and knowledge memorization robustness in the language-specific domain. They release the dataset publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution in addressing the limitations of existing LLM benchmarks, particularly their limited multilingual coverage and focus on general knowledge.  The inclusion of language-specific knowledge is a crucial step toward assessing the true factual competence of LLMs in diverse linguistic contexts. Additionally, emphasizing self-awareness ("know what they don't know") is important for developing more reliable LLMs that don't hallucinate. The focus on simple, fact-based questions is also a nice change from complex reasoning benchmarks, allowing for better focus on factual recall. Reverse questions also test the robustness of knowledge retained.

**Significance:**  The KoLasSimpleQA benchmark is a significant contribution because it:

*   **Highlights Real-World Gaps:** Demonstrates that LLMs often struggle with language-specific factual knowledge, which has implications for their reliability in non-English settings.
*   **Provides a Tool for Targeted Improvement:** Enables researchers to identify weaknesses in LLM architectures and develop targeted optimization strategies for multilingual contexts. The benchmark is open source.
*   **Raises Awareness:** Draws attention to the need for more comprehensive and culturally sensitive evaluation of LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a critical gap in current LLM evaluation practices.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors follow a well-defined data construction process and quality control procedure to create a reliable benchmark.
*   **Thorough Experiments:** The evaluation of various LLMs using KoLasSimpleQA provides valuable insights into their performance characteristics. The focus on reverse questions is a neat touch.
*   **Public Dataset:** The release of KoLasSimpleQA allows other researchers to reproduce the results and build on the work.
*   **Clear Insights:** The paper derives practical conclusions about how to better optimize the design of LLMs for multilingual use.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Language Coverage:** While nine languages represent a significant improvement, the scope is still limited compared to the hundreds of languages spoken globally. More extensive coverage would further enhance the benchmark's utility.
*   **Potential for Bias:** The data collection process from Wikipedia is inherently subject to the biases present in that resource, which may reflect the perspectives and priorities of its contributors.  The paper does not discuss how the authors have taken steps to mitigate any such biases.
*   **Dependence on GPT-4o for Evaluation:** While the LLM-as-judge paradigm is efficient, it still relies on the capabilities and potential biases of the chosen LLM (GPT-4o in this case). Future work may involve exploring alternative evaluation methods. How generalizable is this approach?
*   **Modest Complexity:** The simplicity of the questions, while advantageous for objectivity, may not fully capture the complexities of real-world knowledge application in multilingual contexts.
*   **Analysis of Reasoning**: While the section on analysing LRMs is interesting, the explanation is vague and hard to follow.

**Potential Influence:** KoLasSimpleQA has the potential to significantly influence LLM development by:

*   **Driving Research:** Encouraging researchers to develop new architectures and training strategies that improve factual accuracy and self-awareness in multilingual settings.
*   **Guiding Resource Allocation:** Helping organizations prioritize the development of LLMs for languages and knowledge domains that are currently under-represented.
*   **Improving User Trust:** Leading to more reliable and trustworthy LLMs that can be confidently used in diverse cultural contexts.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**  This paper presents a solid, well-executed contribution to the field. The novelty lies in the benchmark's focus on language-specific knowledge and its multilingual breadth. The significance stems from the identified real-world performance gaps in LLMs' multilingual factual knowledge. The weaknesses are primarily related to the limited language coverage and dependency on GPT-40 for the evaluation of correctness. Overall, it is a strong addition to LLM evaluation, and I recommend this paper be accepted.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Generic Empathy to Personalized Emotional Support: A Self-Evolution Framework for User Preference Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16610v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Ye, Lu Xiang, Yaping Zhang, Chengqing Zong
- **Abstract**: Effective emotional support hinges on understanding users' emotions and needs to provide meaningful comfort during multi-turn interactions. Large Language Models (LLMs) show great potential for expressing empathy; however, they often deliver generic and one-size-fits-all responses that fail to address users' specific needs. To tackle this issue, we propose a self-evolution framework designed to help LLMs improve their responses to better align with users' implicit preferences concerning user profiles (personalities), emotional states, and specific situations. Our framework consists of two distinct phases: \textit{(1)} \textit{Emotional Support Experience Acquisition}, where LLMs are fine-tuned on limited emotional support conversation data to provide basic support, and \textit{(2)} \textit{Self-Improvement for Personalized Emotional Support}, where LLMs leverage self-reflection and self-refinement to generate personalized responses. Through iterative direct preference optimization between the pre- and post-refined responses, our model generates responses that reflect a better understanding of the user's implicit preferences. Extensive experiments and evaluations demonstrate that our method significantly enhances the model's performance in emotional support, reducing unhelpful responses and minimizing discrepancies between user preferences and model outputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a self-evolution framework to enhance the personalized emotional support provided by Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework consists of two stages: (1) an Emotional Support Experience Acquisition phase where LLMs are fine-tuned on limited emotional support conversation data to provide basic support, and (2) a Self-Improvement for Personalized Emotional Support phase where LLMs use self-reflection and self-refinement to generate personalized responses that align better with a user's implicit preferences (profile, emotional state, situation). The model uses iterative direct preference optimization (DPO) between pre- and post-refined responses to learn user preferences. Experiments and evaluations suggest this method enhances performance, reduces unhelpful responses, and minimizes discrepancies between user preferences and model outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of using self-evolution within LLMs for personalized emotional support has some novelty, but builds on existing research on self-reflection and preference learning. The key novelty lies in combining these techniques specifically for the ESC domain and using DPO with synthetic data derived from self-reflection to align with user preferences without explicit reflection steps during inference. This addresses the limitation of generic, formulaic empathy responses often seen in LLMs.

* **Significance:**  The work has the potential to improve the quality and applicability of emotional support chatbots. Personalization is a crucial aspect in ESC because users respond differently to types of support, and moving away from generic responses is essential. If validated by real-world deployment, this framework could contribute to more effective mental health applications, customer service, and emotional companionship systems.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitation of existing LLM-based ESC systems.
    * **Well-defined framework:** The two-stage framework with self-reflection and DPO is clearly explained.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation includes both objective metrics and subjective human evaluations, providing a well-rounded assessment.
    * **Ablation studies:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    * **Strong generalization across backbones:** Results show consistent performance gains across diverse LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Preference Data:**  The reliance on synthetic data generation for preference learning is a potential weakness. The quality of these synthetic preferences directly impacts the model's performance, and there may be a gap between the synthetic preferences and those that humans would actually express. Although the experiments show benefits, limitations of this data are still likely.
    * **Limited Domain Expertise:**  The paper relies on guiding the LLM with high-level principles for self-reflection and refinement but does not incorporate specific domain expertise or knowledge graphs related to psychology or emotional well-being.
    * **Evaluation Limitations:**  Although human evaluation is included, interactive evaluations can be prone to biases.  The LLM-as-a-judge approach relies on API access which is also a source of variability. The evaluation could be strengthened by real-world user studies or a more rigorous methodology.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper's influence hinges on the practical validation of the framework. If successful in real-world applications, it could inspire further research on leveraging self-evolution and preference learning for personalization in various dialogue systems. The framework's modularity makes it adaptable to other domains.

* **Score Justification:** The paper presents a reasonable advancement in the field of emotional support dialogue systems, with some limitations to the novelty of the individual components. I'm assigning a score that reflects the potential for high impact with successful real-world validation.
**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Steering Large Language Models for Machine Translation Personalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16612v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Scalena, Gabriele Sarti, Arianna Bisazza, Elisabetta Fersini, Malvina Nissim
- **Abstract**: High-quality machine translation systems based on large language models (LLMs) have simplified the production of personalized translations reflecting specific stylistic constraints. However, these systems still struggle in settings where stylistic requirements are less explicit and might be harder to convey via prompting. We explore various strategies for personalizing LLM-generated translations in low-resource settings, focusing on the challenging literary translation domain. We explore prompting strategies and inference-time interventions for steering model generations towards a personalized style, and propose a contrastive framework exploiting latent concepts extracted from sparse autoencoders to identify salient personalization properties. Our results show that steering achieves strong personalization while preserving translation quality. We further examine the impact of steering on LLM representations, finding model layers with a relevant impact for personalization are impacted similarly by multi-shot prompting and our steering method, suggesting similar mechanism at play.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates methods for personalizing machine translation (MT) outputs using large language models (LLMs), focusing on the challenging domain of literary translation. It explores various prompting strategies and inference-time interventions, specifically a contrastive framework that leverages latent concepts extracted from sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to identify key personalization properties. The authors compare their proposed SAE-based steering approach with established prompting and steering techniques across different LLMs, languages, and novels from the PAR3 dataset. The results demonstrate that contrastive SAE steering can achieve strong personalization while maintaining translation quality. The paper further examines how steering impacts LLM representations, showing similar impacts from multi-shot prompting and the proposed steering method, suggesting similar underlying mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of SAEs for steering LLM-generated translations towards personalized styles, especially in a low-resource setting with literary text, presents a novel combination of techniques.  The contrastive approach for extracting and using SAE latents is well-motivated.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation across multiple languages, models, and methods, with both automatic metrics (MT quality and style classification) and qualitative analysis. The consideration of various contrastive baselines (MT vs. other human translations) adds rigor.
    *   **Mechanistic Insights:** Investigating the impact of steering on LLM representations, and finding connections to multi-shot prompting, is a valuable step towards understanding how personalization occurs inside LLMs.
    *   **Practical Significance:** Improving personalized MT, especially in literary translation, has real-world applications in making content more accessible and reflective of individual translator styles.
    *   **Clear Exposition:** The paper is well-written and organized, making the complex technical details accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Size:**  The core experiments are performed on relatively smaller LLMs (Gemma 2B, 9B, and Llama 3.1 8B). While the findings are promising, the generalizability to much larger, state-of-the-art models is not fully established.  Larger models might have different internal representations and respond differently to steering.
    *   **SAE Training Overhead:**  The computational cost of training the SAEs is acknowledged as a limitation. The reliance on pre-trained, publicly available SAEs (GemmaScope) restricts the choice of models and layers, potentially affecting performance. The availability of only one SAE layer for Llama further limits results.
    *   **Interpretability of Latents:** While the authors claim SAE latents are more interpretable, the paper doesn't fully delve into understanding the specific stylistic features captured by these latents.  A more in-depth analysis of the learned latents would strengthen the interpretability aspect. This makes the contrastive method more of a "black box" approach than would be ideal given interpretability is one of the motivators.
    *   **Task Specificity:** The evaluation focuses solely on literary translation. The effectiveness of the proposed method in other domains (e.g., technical, conversational) is unclear.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty of this paper lies primarily in the combination of techniques and the specific application. While SAEs and steering methods have been explored before, applying them to the problem of MT personalization, particularly in the literary domain, is a relatively new area of research. The significance is moderate. The paper demonstrates a promising approach with good performance, opening doors to future research directions.

**Justification of Score**

I assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   The paper presents a novel approach that is reasonably well-executed and thoroughly evaluated. The idea of leveraging SAEs for style transfer in MT is compelling, and the results are promising. However, the study has some limitations that prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work.
*   The limitations include the model scales and the potential difficulties in scaling to much larger models. These constraints affect the generalizability of the findings and somewhat reduce the practical implications of this research. More research needs to be done to determine the types of text where these methods are more helpful or less helpful, so that in real-world use cases there is a way to tell if this approach would be beneficial.
*   The investigation into mechanistic details is important but could go deeper. I recommend the authors look into what parts of the sentence/paragraph are more impacted when steering and in what ways.
