Our code is available at https://github.com/RUCAIBox/SimpleDeepSearcher.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SimpleDeepSearcher: Deep Information Seeking via Web-Powered Reasoning Trajectory Synthesis."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SimpleDeepSearcher, a framework designed to improve the deep search capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing approaches that rely on complex training paradigms or reinforcement learning, SimpleDeepSearcher focuses on strategic data engineering. It synthesizes high-quality training data by simulating realistic user interactions with a live web search environment and employs a multi-criteria curation strategy to optimize the diversity and quality of the training data.  The authors demonstrate that supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a small, curated dataset of 871 samples significantly improves performance on several deep search benchmarks compared to prompt-based methods and reinforcement learning approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty primarily lies in the strategic data engineering approach rather than complex model architectures or training algorithms. The idea of simulating realistic web search interactions is not entirely new, but the specific implementation of a multi-criteria curation strategy for both input (query) and output (response) sides appears innovative. This simplifies the training process, reduces computational demands, and provides practical insights for building effective deep search systems. A well-reasoned novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in developing deep search systems: the lack of high-quality training data. By demonstrating that SFT on a small, strategically curated dataset can outperform RL-based methods, the work offers a more practical and accessible pathway to enhancing the deep search capabilities of LLMs. This could significantly lower the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners. The paper's significance also comes from systematically addressing the data-scarce bottleneck with strategic data engineering that leads to performance gains.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The data synthesis framework using real-world web search environments is a strength, addressing the distributional mismatch often present in simulated environments.
    *   The multi-criteria curation strategy comprehensively addresses quality and diversity on both input and output sides.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over strong baselines with a relatively small training dataset.
    *   The method is lightweight and efficient, making it more practical for real-world deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on commercial search APIs introduces potential dependency and cost considerations.
    *   While the results are promising, further exploration of scaling the curated dataset and evaluating on even more challenging benchmarks would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the types of reasoning strategies that the SimpleDeepSearcher learns. What types of search operations are most helpful, and can SimpleDeepSearcher's training provide more useful information to the model than RL approaches?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach is likely to influence future research on deep search systems, especially those seeking efficient and practical solutions. The emphasis on strategic data engineering rather than solely relying on complex models or training paradigms could inspire new approaches in other areas of LLM development. It has provided insight into optimal human-AI reasoning patterns for search systems.

**Justification:**
The paper earns a "7" for its clear focus on addressing the challenge of data scarcity in deep search with a strategic data engineering approach. It simplifies and strengthens the results over RL methods. While the individual components (web simulation, SFT, etc.) are not necessarily groundbreaking, the combination and thorough experimental validation demonstrate a practical and effective method. The limitations of dependency on commercial APIs and need for a larger scale experiment hold it back from a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fact-R1: Towards Explainable Video Misinformation Detection with Deep Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16836v1)
- **Authors**: Fanrui Zhang, Dian Li, Qiang Zhang, Chenjun, sinbadliu, Junxiong Lin, Jiahong Yan, Jiawei Liu, Zheng-Jun Zha
- **Abstract**: The rapid spread of multimodal misinformation on social media has raised growing concerns, while research on video misinformation detection remains limited due to the lack of large-scale, diverse datasets. Existing methods often overfit to rigid templates and lack deep reasoning over deceptive content. To address these challenges, we introduce FakeVV, a large-scale benchmark comprising over 100,000 video-text pairs with fine-grained, interpretable annotations. In addition, we further propose Fact-R1, a novel framework that integrates deep reasoning with collaborative rule-based reinforcement learning. Fact-R1 is trained through a three-stage process: (1) misinformation long-Chain-of-Thought (CoT) instruction tuning, (2) preference alignment via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and (3) Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) using a novel verifiable reward function. This enables Fact-R1 to exhibit emergent reasoning behaviors comparable to those observed in advanced text-based reinforcement learning systems, but in the more complex multimodal misinformation setting. Our work establishes a new paradigm for misinformation detection, bridging large-scale video understanding, reasoning-guided alignment, and interpretable verification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FakeVV, a large-scale dataset for video misinformation detection, consisting of over 100,000 video-text pairs with fine-grained annotations.  It also presents Fact-R1, a novel framework integrating deep reasoning with collaborative rule-based reinforcement learning for explainable video misinformation detection. Fact-R1 is trained in three stages: (1) misinformation long Chain-of-Thought instruction tuning, (2) preference alignment via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and (3) Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) using a novel verifiable reward function. The paper argues that Fact-R1 exhibits emergent reasoning behaviors comparable to advanced text-based reinforcement learning systems in the multimodal misinformation setting. The authors demonstrate that their approach outperforms existing methods on several benchmark datasets, including FakeVV.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Dataset Contribution:** The creation of FakeVV is a significant contribution. The existing datasets in video misinformation detection are limited in size, diversity, and annotation quality. FakeVV addresses these shortcomings with a larger scale, broader topical coverage, a wider temporal range, and fine-grained interpretable annotations. This allows for more robust model training and evaluation. The emphasis on `Out-of-Context` misuse addresses a realistic and prevalent form of misinformation.
*   **Novelty of the Fact-R1 Framework:**  The combination of deep reasoning, collaborative rule-based reinforcement learning, and a multi-stage training process is novel. The use of Chain-of-Thought reasoning is adapted to the video domain, and the use of GRPO with a verifiable reward function is interesting and potentially valuable. The inclusion of auxiliary tasks like news image OCR and video captioning to improve visual reasoning is well-justified.
*   **Focus on Explainability:**  The emphasis on explainable misinformation detection is important. By providing fine-grained annotations and interpretable evaluation metrics, the paper facilitates the development of models that not only detect misinformation but also explain *why* they are making that determination.  The inclusion of manipulated entity annotation aids in this.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper demonstrates through extensive experiments that Fact-R1 outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods on three real-world datasets, including their own FakeVV dataset. Ablation studies effectively show the contribution of different components of the Fact-R1 framework.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:**  The Fact-R1 framework is complex, involving a three-stage training process and multiple optimization techniques (DPO, GRPO). The complexity can make it difficult to implement and reproduce, potentially limiting its adoption by other researchers. It also adds to the computational cost, though LoRA is used.
*   **Reliance on Large Language Models:** The approach relies heavily on the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) like GPT-40 and DeepSeek-R1 for various stages, such as caption generation, long-CoT generation, and explainability evaluation. This reliance may introduce biases or limitations inherent to these LLMs. While using auxiliary tasks can improve performance, biases might still be incorporated during captioning and thus propagated.
*   **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** While the paper evaluates the approach on several datasets, it's not entirely clear how the model would perform in a completely unconstrained real-world setting. The datasets still represent a curated collection of news videos, which may not fully reflect the diversity and adversarial nature of misinformation found in the wild. In this realm, the approach might suffer due to the long-tail distribution of misinformation strategies or due to the prevalence of entirely novel multimodal manipulation techniques.
*   **Potential for LLM Biases:**  The creation process involves LLMs at multiple stages including captioning, long-CoT generation and evaluation. As such, there's an inherited risk of incorporating any existing biases and stereotypes present in the pre-trained models, resulting in both false positives and false negatives.
*   **GRPO with limited negative data:** The GRPO reward function balances positive and negative rewards for both accuracy and style. This implies that the models need to be trained with high quality negatives as opposed to simply a fake signal to improve performance and generalizability.
*   **Generalizability to different kinds of visual contexts and modalities:** Although video and audio are covered, generalizability to other modalities such as 3D representations or alternative visual contexts requires future investigation.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of video misinformation detection by providing a large-scale dataset and a novel framework for explainable detection.  The emphasis on interpretable reasoning and rule-based reinforcement learning is particularly valuable.  The paper pushes the field forward by addressing the limitations of existing approaches and providing a roadmap for future research.
Score: 8

**Justification:**

The paper's strengths in dataset creation, novelty of the proposed framework, focus on explainability, and experimental results significantly outweigh its weaknesses. The FakeVV dataset directly addresses a major bottleneck in the field. While the complexity of the Fact-R1 framework and its reliance on LLMs are legitimate concerns, the performance gains and explainability benefits justify the approach. The paper presents a strong advancement in addressing the critical issue of video misinformation and establishes a compelling paradigm for future research. However, some limitations around the generalizability of the model and potential LLM biases prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### R1-Compress: Long Chain-of-Thought Compression via Chunk Compression and Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16838v1)
- **Authors**: Yibo Wang, Li Shen, Huanjin Yao, Tiansheng Huang, Rui Liu, Naiqiang Tan, Jiaxing Huang, Kai Zhang, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning enhances large language models (LLMs) by enabling step-by-step problem-solving, yet its extension to Long-CoT introduces substantial computational overhead due to increased token length. Existing compression approaches -- instance-level and token-level -- either sacrifice essential local reasoning signals like reflection or yield incoherent outputs. To address these limitations, we propose R1-Compress, a two-stage chunk-level compression framework that preserves both local information and coherence. Our method segments Long-CoT into manageable chunks, applies LLM-driven inner-chunk compression, and employs an inter-chunk search mechanism to select the short and coherent sequence. Experiments on Qwen2.5-Instruct models across MATH500, AIME24, and GPQA-Diamond demonstrate that R1-Compress significantly reduces token usage while maintaining comparable reasoning accuracy. On MATH500, R1-Compress achieves an accuracy of 92.4%, with only a 0.6% drop compared to the Long-CoT baseline, while reducing token usage by about 20%. Source code will be available at https://github.com/w-yibo/R1-Compress
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R1-Compress, a two-stage chunk-level compression framework designed to reduce the token length of Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in large language models (LLMs).  R1-Compress segments Long-CoT into manageable chunks, compresses each chunk using an LLM, and then employs an inter-chunk search mechanism to select a short, coherent sequence of chunks to form the compressed reasoning chain. Experiments on datasets like MATH500, AIME24, and GPQA-Diamond, using Qwen2.5-Instruct models, demonstrate that R1-Compress significantly reduces token usage while maintaining comparable reasoning accuracy. The paper claims that existing compression approaches (instance-level and token-level) either sacrifice essential local reasoning signals like reflection or yield incoherent outputs, which R1-Compress aims to address.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of chunk-level compression with inter-chunk search has *some* elements of novelty.  While the individual components (segmentation, LLM-based compression, search) aren't revolutionary on their own, the specific combination, particularly the focus on *inter-chunk coherence* and the preservation of *local reasoning signals (reflection)*, represents an incremental but worthwhile advance. The explicit attempt to address the limitations of instance-level (e.g., C3oT) and token-level (e.g., TokenSkip) compression strategies is a key differentiator. However, the methodology isn't radically different, relying mostly on clever engineering and integration of existing LLM capabilities.

*   **Significance:**  Long-CoT is computationally expensive, and efficient compression is a crucial area for enabling the deployment of powerful reasoning models in resource-constrained environments.  The paper demonstrates a practical approach to reducing token length without significantly sacrificing accuracy on challenging benchmarks. If the results are reproducible and can be generalized to other models and tasks, this contributes to the broader goal of making LLMs more accessible and efficient.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Focus:** The paper addresses a real and pressing problem in the field (Long-CoT efficiency).
    *   **Methodology:**  The chunk-level compression approach seems well-motivated and the two-stage design (inner-chunk compression and inter-chunk search) is logical.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments are reasonably comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and model scales. The analysis of reflection preservation is valuable.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the approach and results.
    *   **Attention to shortcomings of prior work**: The analysis of instance-level and token-level compression limitations is valuable for motivating the new approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Innovation:** The novelty, while present, is not groundbreaking. The methods build upon existing techniques (LLM prompting, search algorithms, etc.).
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The reliance on LLMs for both compression and the search model is a double-edged sword. It enables powerful compression, but also introduces reliance on the specific characteristics of the chosen LLMs (e.g., Qwen2.5). Generalizability needs more evaluation.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper is primarily empirical. There's little theoretical analysis of the optimality or limitations of the chunking strategy or search algorithm. It's not clear how sensitive the approach is to different chunk sizes or search parameters.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other compression methods**: The selection of the baseline methods is somewhat lacking. There are more aggressive or even heuristic based compression methods like "summarization of chain of thought" type methods which the paper does not compare against.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more efficient reasoning systems by demonstrating the effectiveness of chunk-level compression with inter-chunk coherence.  It could inspire further research on more sophisticated chunking strategies, search algorithms, and evaluation metrics for CoT compression. It might also spur research into more robust and generalizable compression methods that are less dependent on the specific characteristics of the underlying LLMs.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I would rate this paper a **Score: 6.5**.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid engineering solution to a practically important problem. The method is well-motivated, reasonably evaluated, and shows promising results. However, the innovation is incremental rather than transformative. The reliance on LLMs for core components (compression, search) creates potential limitations in generalizability and robustness. The lack of deeper theoretical analysis is also a drawback. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, justifying a score above average, but the limitations prevent a significantly higher rating. While the work demonstrates a pragmatic advancement in Long-CoT compression, it doesn't fundamentally alter the landscape of reasoning systems research. It is a good piece of engineering, well executed, but not a scientific breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LaViDa: A Large Diffusion Language Model for Multimodal Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16839v1)
- **Authors**: Shufan Li, Konstantinos Kallidromitis, Hritik Bansal, Akash Gokul, Yusuke Kato, Kazuki Kozuka, Jason Kuen, Zhe Lin, Kai-Wei Chang, Aditya Grover
- **Abstract**: Modern Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can solve a wide range of tasks requiring visual reasoning. In real-world scenarios, desirable properties for VLMs include fast inference and controllable generation (e.g., constraining outputs to adhere to a desired format). However, existing autoregressive (AR) VLMs like LLaVA struggle in these aspects. Discrete diffusion models (DMs) offer a promising alternative, enabling parallel decoding for faster inference and bidirectional context for controllable generation through text-infilling. While effective in language-only settings, DMs' potential for multimodal tasks is underexplored. We introduce LaViDa, a family of VLMs built on DMs. We build LaViDa by equipping DMs with a vision encoder and jointly fine-tune the combined parts for multimodal instruction following. To address challenges encountered, LaViDa incorporates novel techniques such as complementary masking for effective training, prefix KV cache for efficient inference, and timestep shifting for high-quality sampling. Experiments show that LaViDa achieves competitive or superior performance to AR VLMs on multi-modal benchmarks such as MMMU, while offering unique advantages of DMs, including flexible speed-quality tradeoff, controllability, and bidirectional reasoning. On COCO captioning, LaViDa surpasses Open-LLaVa-Next-8B by +4.1 CIDEr with 1.92x speedup. On bidirectional tasks, it achieves +59% improvement on Constrained Poem Completion. These results demonstrate LaViDa as a strong alternative to AR VLMs. Code and models will be released in the camera-ready version.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LaViDa paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces LaViDa, the first family of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) built upon discrete diffusion models (DMs).  Existing VLMs predominantly rely on autoregressive (AR) language models, which suffer from limitations like slow inference and difficulty handling bidirectional contexts (useful for controllable generation).  LaViDa addresses these shortcomings by adapting DMs for multimodal instruction following.  The authors introduce novel techniques to enhance the training and inference of diffusion-based VLMs, including:

*   **Complementary Masking:**  Ensures all tokens in the output contribute to learning, improving data efficiency.
*   **Prefix-DLM:** A technique to enable KV caching for faster inference with multimodal prompts (containing lengthy visual features).
*   **Timestep Shifting:** Adapts the unmasking schedule to maintain high sample quality when using fewer diffusion steps for faster generation.

The paper demonstrates that LaViDa achieves competitive or superior performance compared to AR VLMs on benchmarks like MMMU, MathVista, ChartQA, and ScienceQA.  Furthermore, it showcases the advantages of DMs, such as flexible speed-quality trade-off, controllability in constrained generation tasks, and bidirectional reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in successfully adapting *discrete diffusion models*—which have shown promise in language-only settings—to the vision-language domain. While individual components like vision encoders and projection networks have been used in AR VLMs, their integration with DMs and the introduction of the complementary masking, Prefix-DLM, and timestep shifting techniques are the main contributions. The Prefix-DLM to accelerate diffusion VLM inference is a significant practical contribution. It directly addresses one of the major bottlenecks of diffusion models which don't have inherent KV cache support. The time-step shifting is also a practical approach to accelerate sampling and improve its quality at low step counts.

*   **Significance:**  The paper provides a strong proof of concept that DMs can be a viable and even advantageous alternative to AR models in the VLM space. This is significant because DMs inherently offer characteristics that are desirable but difficult to achieve with AR models, like faster parallel decoding and inherent ability to handle bi-directional context which makes controllable generation easier to achieve. Demonstrating comparable (or even superior) performance with careful design and training is an important step.  The controllable generation results, specifically the improvement on constrained poem completion, underscore the potential benefits of DMs for structured output tasks. Showing better speed/quality tradeoffs on COCO captioning further enhances the argument. The work is likely to inspire further research into diffusion-based VLMs, potentially leading to models with improved controllability, speed, and task-specific capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   Detailed explanation of the proposed techniques (complementary masking, Prefix-DLM, timestep shifting).
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on a variety of benchmarks.
    *   Strong qualitative results demonstrating controllability in text-infilling tasks.
    *   Ablation studies that quantify the impact of different design choices.
    *   Address a real issue with diffusion models (the need for KV caching) and introduce a solid method to overcome it.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance on OCR tasks lags behind AR models. The authors attribute this to their use of average pooling for visual token compression, which impacts fine-grained spatial information. This is a valid explanation, but it also highlights a limitation of the current approach.
    *   While the authors claim to achieve competitive or superior performance, the gains are not always substantial across *all* benchmarks. The results on MMMU are solid, but other gains are more incremental, especially when compared with frontier models that have a scaling advantage in dataset sizes or model sizes.
    *   It is difficult to thoroughly assess the novelty of specific implementation details without access to the source code and a more in-depth understanding of the implementation nuances.
    * The paper states in multiple locations the benefit of using vision inputs of higher resolution. However, higher resolution leads to a longer input sequence into the underlying transformer. While using Prefix-DLM (a good approach!), the compute requirements to train the model with the vision prefix will still be relatively high as well as the peak GPU memory usage in a way that may be harder to parallelize (due to longer sequences). It may be worth exploring alternatives such as the visual tokenizer approach of the Flamingo/Kosmos paper (although they are not bidirectionally attended).

*   **Impact:** The paper significantly advances the exploration of diffusion models in the vision-language space and makes a compelling case for their potential.  The novel techniques introduced provide a practical guide for adapting DMs to VLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of VLMs by demonstrating the effective adaptation of discrete diffusion models to this domain. The introduced techniques, particularly Prefix-DLM, are valuable contributions to the DM literature generally and VLM research specifically. While the empirical gains over AR models are not always groundbreaking, the work demonstrates the viability of DMs for VLMs and highlights their unique advantages. The main weaknesses are the lagging performance in OCR tasks and not addressing the higher cost of running at longer image resolutions.  The paper provides a solid foundation for future research exploring more scalable and capable diffusion-based VLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Walk&Retrieve: Simple Yet Effective Zero-shot Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Knowledge Graph Walks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16849v1)
- **Authors**: Martin Böckling, Heiko Paulheim, Andreea Iana
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have showcased impressive reasoning abilities, but often suffer from hallucinations or outdated knowledge. Knowledge Graph (KG)-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) remedies these shortcomings by grounding LLM responses in structured external information from a knowledge base. However, many KG-based RAG approaches struggle with (i) aligning KG and textual representations, (ii) balancing retrieval accuracy and efficiency, and (iii) adapting to dynamically updated KGs. In this work, we introduce Walk&Retrieve, a simple yet effective KG-based framework that leverages walk-based graph traversal and knowledge verbalization for corpus generation for zero-shot RAG. Built around efficient KG walks, our method does not require fine-tuning on domain-specific data, enabling seamless adaptation to KG updates, reducing computational overhead, and allowing integration with any off-the-shelf backbone LLM. Despite its simplicity, Walk&Retrieve performs competitively, often outperforming existing RAG systems in response accuracy and hallucination reduction. Moreover, it demonstrates lower query latency and robust scalability to large KGs, highlighting the potential of lightweight retrieval strategies as strong baselines for future RAG research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Walk&Retrieve: Simple Yet Effective Zero-shot Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Knowledge Graph Walks":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Walk&Retrieve, a novel Knowledge Graph (KG)-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework. It tackles the challenges of using KGs with Large Language Models (LLMs) by employing a simple, yet effective, approach. Walk&Retrieve generates a corpus of relevant facts for each KG entity by traversing the graph using random walks or breadth-first search (BFS). These walks are then verbalized into natural language descriptions using an LLM, creating a corpus of context for each node.  During inference, the query is encoded, and similar nodes from the KG and their associated textual "walks" are retrieved.  The LLM then uses this retrieved context to generate the final answer in a zero-shot fashion, without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. The authors demonstrate that Walk&Retrieve achieves competitive performance compared to more complex KG-based RAG systems, while also reducing hallucinations, offering lower query latency, and scaling well to large KGs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of Walk&Retrieve lies in its simplicity and efficiency. The idea of using graph walks for knowledge retrieval isn't entirely new, but the way this paper combines KG walks with LLM-driven verbalization for *corpus generation* is a distinguishing factor. Most KG-RAG systems focus on *query-time* retrieval of subgraphs. By pre-generating and indexing contextual walks, Walk&Retrieve achieves a trade-off between offline processing and efficient online retrieval. The core concept of converting structured graph information to a language-model friendly format *before* the query comes in, setting up the retrieval step as an off-the-shelf cosine-similarity search, is also noteworthy. The method is particularly valuable given the zero-shot aspect; no fine-tuning is required for specific KGs.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from several areas:

*   **Practicality:** The framework is easy to implement and requires minimal computational overhead. Its zero-shot capability makes it highly adaptable to new and dynamically updated KGs, which is a significant advantage over methods requiring fine-tuning.
*   **Performance:** The experimental results are promising, showing that Walk&Retrieve performs competitively with existing KG-based RAG methods, and even outperforms them in some metrics, particularly in truthfulness and reducing hallucinations.
*   **Scalability:** The approach's scalability to large KGs is a key contribution. Pre-generating walks and using efficient vector search makes it more suitable for real-world applications where KGs can be massive.
*   **Baseline:** The simplicity and effectiveness of Walk&Retrieve establish it as a strong baseline for future research in KG-based RAG. It highlights the potential of lightweight retrieval strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Simplicity and Efficiency:** The paper champions a simple design that performs well, a valuable contribution in a field often dominated by complex models.
*   **Zero-Shot Adaptation:** The framework's ability to adapt to new KGs without fine-tuning is a major strength.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The authors provide solid experimental evidence to support their claims. The evaluation uses established benchmarks and incorporates model-based metrics (accuracy, hallucination, missing). The ablation studies offer insights into design choices (e.g., walk depth).
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand. The method is clearly explained, and the experimental setup is well-defined.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Graph Walking:** The use of random walks and BFS, while effective, isn't inherently novel.  The novelty lies in the system-level integration of these with LLMs.
*   **Reliance on LLM for Verbalization:** The quality of the verbalized walks depends heavily on the LLM used for knowledge verbalization. A poor choice of LLM could negatively impact the overall performance.  The prompt engineering is simple and the impact of different prompts is not fully explored.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation KGs:** Even though the paper uses MetaQA and CRAG, Walk&Retrieve should also be evaluated on more challenging KGs to fully showcase its capabilities. WebQSP [57], CWQ [48] should be evaluated to more thoroughly prove that the limitations reported in the main body are truly overcome.
*   **Hyperparameter choices:** Parameter selection needs to be explained in more detail. "For Walk&Retrieve-RW, we also ablate nw ∈ [10, 100] (step of 10); for brevity, we report results for nw = 60, as other values perform comparably." Needs to have supporting evidence either in main body or the appendix.
*   **Ablation Study:** Should experiment with BFS and RW in tandem and show why they cannot be combined for a better result.

**Overall Assessment:**

Despite its weaknesses, Walk&Retrieve presents a valuable contribution to the field of KG-based RAG. Its simplicity, efficiency, and adaptability make it a promising approach for building practical and scalable systems. The paper makes a case that simpler RAG solutions can be surprisingly competitive while offering clear advantages in terms of computational cost and ease of deployment.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the balance between the paper's strengths and weaknesses. The novelty is good, but not groundbreaking. The significance is substantial, as it provides a practical and scalable solution to a challenging problem. The experimental results are convincing, but the evaluation could be more comprehensive. The paper's simplicity and potential impact on future research justify a solid score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conditional Panoramic Image Generation via Masked Autoregressive Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16862v1)
- **Authors**: Chaoyang Wang, Xiangtai Li, Lu Qi, Xiaofan Lin, Jinbin Bai, Qianyu Zhou, Yunhai Tong
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in panoramic image generation has underscored two critical limitations in existing approaches. First, most methods are built upon diffusion models, which are inherently ill-suited for equirectangular projection (ERP) panoramas due to the violation of the identically and independently distributed (i.i.d.) Gaussian noise assumption caused by their spherical mapping. Second, these methods often treat text-conditioned generation (text-to-panorama) and image-conditioned generation (panorama outpainting) as separate tasks, relying on distinct architectures and task-specific data. In this work, we propose a unified framework, Panoramic AutoRegressive model (PAR), which leverages masked autoregressive modeling to address these challenges. PAR avoids the i.i.d. assumption constraint and integrates text and image conditioning into a cohesive architecture, enabling seamless generation across tasks. To address the inherent discontinuity in existing generative models, we introduce circular padding to enhance spatial coherence and propose a consistency alignment strategy to improve generation quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive performance in text-to-image generation and panorama outpainting tasks while showcasing promising scalability and generalization capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Panoramic AutoRegressive (PAR) modeling for conditional panoramic image generation.  The authors address two key limitations of existing diffusion-based approaches: (1) the i.i.d. Gaussian noise assumption being violated by equirectangular projection (ERP) panoramas and (2) the separation of tasks like text-to-panorama and panorama outpainting into distinct architectures. PAR leverages masked autoregressive modeling to avoid the i.i.d. constraint and unifies text and image conditioning within a single architecture. The paper proposes dual-space circular padding to enhance spatial coherence and a consistency alignment strategy to improve generation quality, specifically addressing the discontinuity issue in ERP representations. Experimental results demonstrate competitive performance in text-to-image generation and panorama outpainting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the application of masked autoregressive modeling to panoramic image generation, which provides a unified framework for various conditional generation tasks, unlike most existing diffusion-based approaches. The dual-space circular padding and consistency alignment are novel designs tailored to the panoramic domain, addressing issues specific to ERP representations. The unification of text-to-panorama and outpainting into a single framework that is not reliant on task-specific engineering also adds value.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles important limitations of current panoramic image generation techniques, particularly the incompatibility of diffusion models with ERP panoramas and the task-specific pipelines. By proposing PAR, the authors offer a theoretically sound and practically effective alternative. The proposed techniques for maintaining spatial coherence in ERP representations are significant, as they directly address a key challenge in generating realistic panoramas. The demonstration of competitive performance across different tasks using a single model suggests a more efficient and flexible approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   A well-defined architectural and training approach.
    *   Strong experimental results, demonstrating competitive performance against specialist methods.
    *   Addresses crucial issues in panoramic image generation, making it a contribution to the field.
    *   Addresses the inherent limitations of the i.i.d. assumption in diffusion models, which is theoretically important in the panoramic context.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could further analyze the limitations of their consistency loss as well as the failure cases of the generation results.
    *   Although the introduction of dual-space circular padding and translation consistency loss is significant, their individual impacts on overall performance could be more rigorously analyzed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting masked autoregressive modeling as a viable alternative to diffusion models for panoramic image generation. The proposed techniques for spatial coherence and consistency alignment are likely to be adopted and further developed by other researchers in this area. The unified framework for different tasks could lead to more efficient and flexible panorama generation systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to panoramic image generation, addressing significant limitations of existing methods. The unification of conditional generation tasks and the adaptation of masked autoregressive modeling to the panoramic domain are valuable contributions. While there is still room for further exploration and analysis, the experimental results and architectural designs are promising, indicating its potential to influence the field. Given its theoretical soundness, competitive results, and potential for further development, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Training-Free Efficient Video Generation via Dynamic Token Carving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16864v1)
- **Authors**: Yuechen Zhang, Jinbo Xing, Bin Xia, Shaoteng Liu, Bohao Peng, Xin Tao, Pengfei Wan, Eric Lo, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Despite the remarkable generation quality of video Diffusion Transformer (DiT) models, their practical deployment is severely hindered by extensive computational requirements. This inefficiency stems from two key challenges: the quadratic complexity of self-attention with respect to token length and the multi-step nature of diffusion models. To address these limitations, we present Jenga, a novel inference pipeline that combines dynamic attention carving with progressive resolution generation. Our approach leverages two key insights: (1) early denoising steps do not require high-resolution latents, and (2) later steps do not require dense attention. Jenga introduces a block-wise attention mechanism that dynamically selects relevant token interactions using 3D space-filling curves, alongside a progressive resolution strategy that gradually increases latent resolution during generation. Experimental results demonstrate that Jenga achieves substantial speedups across multiple state-of-the-art video diffusion models while maintaining comparable generation quality (8.83$\times$ speedup with 0.01\% performance drop on VBench). As a plug-and-play solution, Jenga enables practical, high-quality video generation on modern hardware by reducing inference time from minutes to seconds -- without requiring model retraining. Code: https://github.com/dvlab-research/Jenga
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Training-Free Efficient Video Generation via Dynamic Token Carving":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Jenga," a novel training-free inference pipeline designed to improve the efficiency of video generation using Diffusion Transformer (DiT) models. It tackles the computational bottleneck associated with the quadratic complexity of self-attention and the multi-step nature of diffusion models. Jenga employs two main techniques:

1.  **Dynamic Token Carving (AttenCarve):** Uses space-filling curves to partition the video latent space into blocks and then selectively computes key-value pairs using a block-wise attention mechanism, effectively sparsifying the attention computation.
2.  **Progressive Resolution (ProRes):** Generates video through phased resizing and denoising of latents, starting with low-resolution latents and gradually increasing the resolution, reducing overall token interactions.  A text-attention amplifier is introduced to maintain a stable field of view (FOV) during the progressive resolution stages.

The authors demonstrate that Jenga achieves significant speedups (up to 8.83x) across multiple state-of-the-art DiT models while maintaining comparable or even improved generation quality, as measured by VBench, and human preference tests.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques that, while building on existing concepts, demonstrates a novel and effective approach to efficient video generation.  The dynamic attention carving and progressive resolution are well-integrated. Dynamic token carving adapts attention sparsity based on the content instead of using static masks, which makes it better than static pruning methods. The progressive resolution strategy, while explored in previous works, is refined with the addition of the text-attention amplifier to address FOV issues. The combination of these ideas in a plug-and-play manner is an important innovation.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its practical impact. By offering a training-free inference pipeline, Jenga makes high-quality video generation accessible on more readily available hardware. The demonstrated speedups are substantial and can significantly improve the workflow for video content creators. The plug-and-play nature is also a major benefit, allowing users to easily integrate Jenga with existing DiT models. The comprehensive experimentation across various video generation models and thorough ablation studies bolster the claims of efficiency and generalizability. The human preference study provides further confidence in the preserved quality of the generated videos. The paper's detailed algorithm descriptions, parameter settings, and exploration of limitations are all commendable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Significant speedups:**  The achieved speedups are remarkable, making the approach practically relevant.
    *   **Training-free:**  Eliminating the need for retraining is a huge advantage.
    *   **Plug-and-play design:**  Ease of integration with existing models increases adoption potential.
    *   **Comprehensive experimentation:**  Evaluation across diverse models and datasets, including both quantitative and qualitative metrics.
    *   **Thorough ablation studies:**  Demonstrate the effectiveness of the individual components and parameter choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Edge artifacts:** Potential for edge artifacts during resolution transitions, although the authors claim these can be mitigated with prompt engineering.
    *   **Static block partitioning:** The static nature of the space-filling curve block partitioning is a limitation, as it doesn't account for semantic context during token selection. More advanced methods might improve performance.
    *   **Reliance on specific prompts:** Some results suggest a sensitivity to prompt complexity, and more work might be needed to optimize performance across a wider variety of prompts.
    *   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** The paper is somewhat lacking in theoretical analysis, explaining precisely *why* certain attention sparsity patterns work so effectively, for example. While empirical results are strong, a deeper theoretical understanding would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** Jenga is poised to have a substantial influence on the field of video generation. The approach reduces the barriers to entry for high-quality video generation, empowering more individuals and organizations to create compelling video content.  Furthermore, the techniques presented in Jenga can serve as inspiration for future research on efficient inference methods for diffusion models. The simplicity of applying it, the good trade-off between speed and quality, and its ease of use, makes it significant.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** Jenga is a highly practical and impactful contribution. The significant speedups, combined with the training-free and plug-and-play design, make it a valuable tool for the video generation community. While there are limitations in the form of the potential for edge artifacts and the static nature of block partitioning, the paper offers a strong balance of innovation and practicality. The detailed experimental validation and clear articulation of limitations solidify its contribution to the field. This paper will definitely be an influential resource.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### MPO: Multilingual Safety Alignment via Reward Gap Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16869v1)
- **Authors**: Weixiang Zhao, Yulin Hu, Yang Deng, Tongtong Wu, Wenxuan Zhang, Jiahe Guo, An Zhang, Yanyan Zhao, Bing Qin, Tat-Seng Chua, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have become increasingly central to AI applications worldwide, necessitating robust multilingual safety alignment to ensure secure deployment across diverse linguistic contexts. Existing preference learning methods for safety alignment, such as RLHF and DPO, are primarily monolingual and struggle with noisy multilingual data. To address these limitations, we introduce Multilingual reward gaP Optimization (MPO), a novel approach that leverages the well-aligned safety capabilities of the dominant language (English) to improve safety alignment across multiple languages. MPO directly minimizes the reward gap difference between the dominant language and target languages, effectively transferring safety capabilities while preserving the original strengths of the dominant language. Extensive experiments on three LLMs, LLaMA-3.1, Gemma-2 and Qwen2.5, validate MPO's efficacy in multilingual safety alignment without degrading general multilingual utility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multilingual Reward Gap Optimization (MPO), a novel approach to improving the safety alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) across multiple languages. MPO leverages the well-aligned safety capabilities of a dominant language (e.g., English) and transfers these capabilities to other languages by minimizing the reward gap difference between the dominant and target languages. The method also incorporates constraints to preserve the original strengths of the dominant language. The authors conducted extensive experiments on LLaMA-3.1, Gemma-2, and Qwen2.5, demonstrating that MPO enhances multilingual safety alignment without degrading general multilingual utility. The authors highlight the correlation between the reward gap and safety performance, particularly in low-resource languages, and demonstrate MPO's robustness to noisy multilingual data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of transferring safety capabilities from a dominant language to others via reward gap minimization is a novel contribution.  While preference learning and multilingual alignment are established areas, the specific focus on the *reward gap* as a core optimization target for *multilingual safety* is a relatively fresh perspective. The method also stands out against the alternatives due to a key finding that relying on existing automatic translation tools could actually add noise into the equation and lead to less accurate and more unreliable data. 

*   **Significance:** Multilingual safety alignment is a crucial area, given the global deployment of LLMs. This work addresses a practical problem where existing methods often struggle with noisy multilingual data and low-resource languages. Demonstrating that MPO improves safety without sacrificing general utility is significant. The fact that LLMs are often seen as "not safe" in languages other than the ones it was originally trained on, makes this approach very relevant.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper presents a well-defined algorithm (MPO) with a clear objective function and constraints. The derivation of the gradient is detailed. The experimental setup is thorough, using multiple LLMs, languages, and benchmarks. The ablation studies and analyses (data quality, data quantity, visualization) strengthen the validation of the approach.
*   **Empirical Support:** The experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across different LLMs and languages, providing strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of MPO. The results, when compared with baselines and cross-lingual transfer methods, are compelling.  The analyses of the representations and data indicate a well-designed system.

*   **Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations such as computational constraints on scaling to larger models and a focus solely on safety alignment (excluding other alignment tasks like value alignment). It also assumes that safety guidelines *are* universal principles. It does not address situations where ethical considerations might differ significantly across cultures. The reliance on translation tools, even with quality checks, can still introduce subtle biases or inaccuracies.

*   **Impact:** The paper provides a practical and effective approach for multilingual safety alignment, which could be beneficial for researchers and practitioners deploying LLMs in diverse linguistic contexts. It offers a valuable contribution to the field of responsible AI development.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. This score reflects the solid contribution of this paper to a crucial area of LLM research. The approach is novel, well-validated empirically, and shows significant promise for real-world applications. Its strengths lie in its focus on reward gap minimization and its robustness in dealing with data scarcity in low-resource languages.
However, the scope is somewhat limited by the focus on safety alignment alone, without a more comprehensive consideration of cultural sensitivity in ethical frameworks. It also assumes safety requirements *are* universal. The limitations in scaling and the dependence on external translation tools are also considerations. This score acknowledges both the importance of the problem addressed and the significant technical contribution, while accounting for the identified limitations.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### T2I-ConBench: Text-to-Image Benchmark for Continual Post-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16875v1)
- **Authors**: Zhehao Huang, Yuhang Liu, Yixin Lou, Zhengbao He, Mingzhen He, Wenxing Zhou, Tao Li, Kehan Li, Zeyi Huang, Xiaolin Huang
- **Abstract**: Continual post-training adapts a single text-to-image diffusion model to learn new tasks without incurring the cost of separate models, but naive post-training causes forgetting of pretrained knowledge and undermines zero-shot compositionality. We observe that the absence of a standardized evaluation protocol hampers related research for continual post-training. To address this, we introduce T2I-ConBench, a unified benchmark for continual post-training of text-to-image models. T2I-ConBench focuses on two practical scenarios, item customization and domain enhancement, and analyzes four dimensions: (1) retention of generality, (2) target-task performance, (3) catastrophic forgetting, and (4) cross-task generalization. It combines automated metrics, human-preference modeling, and vision-language QA for comprehensive assessment. We benchmark ten representative methods across three realistic task sequences and find that no approach excels on all fronts. Even joint "oracle" training does not succeed for every task, and cross-task generalization remains unsolved. We release all datasets, code, and evaluation tools to accelerate research in continual post-training for text-to-image models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces T2I-ConBench, a new benchmark for evaluating continual post-training of text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models.  It addresses the lack of standardized evaluation protocols in this area, where models are adapted sequentially to new tasks without forgetting previously learned knowledge.  T2I-ConBench focuses on item customization and domain enhancement scenarios, assessing four dimensions: retention of generality, target-task performance, catastrophic forgetting, and cross-task generalization. The benchmark uses automated metrics, human preference modeling, and visual question answering. The authors benchmark ten existing methods and release datasets, code, and evaluation tools to accelerate research.  Experiments reveal that no single method excels across all dimensions and that cross-task generalization remains a challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive, multi-faceted benchmark for continual post-training of T2I models.  While individual components such as certain metrics or datasets might exist elsewhere, the *integration* of all aspects - specific tasks (item customization and domain enhancement), challenging prompt generation, diverse dataset curation, automated evaluation pipeline combining different metrics, and a comprehensive set of metrics addressing key challenges - represents a significant contribution. The attention to cross-task generalization is also a relatively unexplored dimension in this area and is a strong aspect of the work. The standardized dataset curation, and prompt engineering specifically designed for the task are well conceived.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in the development of continually learning T2I models: the absence of a reliable and unified evaluation framework. By providing such a benchmark, the paper facilitates fair comparisons between different methods and fosters progress in the field. The benchmark also highlights the limitations of existing techniques and identifies key areas for future research, most notably the challenge of cross-task generalization. The release of code and data makes the benchmark accessible and promotes reproducibility.  The findings that the 'oracle' joint learning doesn't necessarily guarantee optimal performance and the persistent challenge of cross-task generalization are important insights.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The benchmark's assessment of multiple dimensions (generality, task performance, forgetting, generalization) is a key strength.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on item customization and domain enhancement reflects real-world application scenarios.
*   **Automated Pipeline:** The automated evaluation pipeline enables scalable and reproducible experiments.
*   **Code and Data Release:**  Availability of resources fosters community contributions and further research.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of continual post-training and the need for a standardized benchmark.
*   **Strong Experimental Setup:** The baselines chosen are reasonable and representative, and the results are well-analyzed.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The domain enhancement tasks relies heavily on synthetic data. While this allows for control, it might not fully reflect the complexities of real-world datasets, possibly creating a shift. The datasets might only be a representation of specific domain scenarios not all.
*   **Model Scope:** Evaluation is conducted primarily on PixArt-a. While SD v1.4 is used for verification, demonstrating generalizability across a wider range of T2I architectures would have further strengthened the conclusions.
*   **Metric limitations**: Using FID as a sole source for general image quality evaluation can be restrictive, given that there are new more advanced metrics (even from BFL).
*   **Baselines**: While a number of methods were implemented, it might be helpful to look at others that have been used in domain adaptation and continual learning (e.g. contrastive learning methods). The authors do note this, but the limited baselines makes the benchmark slightly less conclusive.

**Overall Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper makes a solid and significant contribution. It presents a well-designed benchmark that addresses a clear need in the field of continual learning for T2I models. While there are limitations related to the dataset scope and model range, the comprehensive evaluation framework and the insights gained from benchmarking existing methods justify a relatively high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### CASTILLO: Characterizing Response Length Distributions of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16881v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel F. Perez-Ramirez, Dejan Kostic, Magnus Boman
- **Abstract**: Efficiently managing compute resources for Large Language Model (LLM) inference remains challenging due to the inherently stochastic and variable lengths of autoregressive text generation. Accurately estimating response lengths in advance enables proactive resource allocation, yet existing approaches either bias text generation towards certain lengths or rely on assumptions that ignore model- and prompt-specific variability. We introduce CASTILLO, a dataset characterizing response length distributions across 13 widely-used open-source LLMs evaluated on seven distinct instruction-following corpora. For each $\langle$prompt, model$\rangle$ sample pair, we generate 10 independent completions using fixed decoding hyper-parameters, record the token length of each response, and publish summary statistics (mean, std-dev, percentiles), along with the shortest and longest completions, and the exact generation settings. Our analysis reveals significant inter- and intra-model variability in response lengths (even under identical generation settings), as well as model-specific behaviors and occurrences of partial text degeneration in only subsets of responses. CASTILLO enables the development of predictive models for proactive scheduling and provides a systematic framework for analyzing model-specific generation behaviors. We publicly release the dataset and code to foster research at the intersection of generative language modeling and systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CASTILLO, a large-scale dataset designed to characterize the response length distributions of Large Language Models (LLMs).  CASTILLO includes data from 13 open-source LLMs across 7 instruction-following datasets. For each prompt and model combination, they generated 10 independent completions using fixed decoding parameters and recorded the token length of each response, along with statistics like mean, standard deviation, and percentiles.  The paper presents an analysis of this data, highlighting inter-model and intra-model variability in response lengths, as well as instances of partial text degeneration. CASTILLO aims to support the development of predictive models for proactive scheduling and resource allocation in LLM serving systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key strength of the paper is in its creation and release of the CASTILLO dataset. While the phenomenon of variable response length in LLMs is known, CASTILLO represents a systematic and comprehensive effort to quantify this variability across a wide range of models and tasks. The insights derived regarding inter- and intra-model variability are not necessarily surprising, but CASTILLO provides solid empirical grounding for these observations. The identification and categorization of partial text degeneration adds a valuable dimension. The sanitization of the data also makes a practical and useful decision for ensuring quality data downstream.

*   **Significance:** The dataset has the potential to significantly impact research in LLM serving systems. Accurate response length prediction is crucial for proactive resource allocation and efficient scheduling. CASTILLO provides a benchmark dataset for developing and evaluating such predictive models. The analysis of model-specific generation behaviors can also inform model selection and deployment decisions. Providing a controlled framework for comparing behavior across different models, prompts, and decoding settings is valuable for better understanding models. There are prior works, however, that attempt to directly predict response lengths in advance, limiting the novelty somewhat.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Large scale and diverse coverage of models and datasets.
    *   Systematic methodology for data generation and analysis.
    *   Identification and categorization of partial text degeneration.
    *   Public release of the dataset and code for reproducibility.
    *   Sanitization of the data for quality improvement.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The analysis primarily focuses on descriptive statistics. Deeper insights through advanced statistical modeling or machine learning techniques could strengthen the paper.
    *   The scope is limited to Transformer-based models. Addressing alternative architectures (even if briefly) would broaden the impact.
    *   While the paper mentions potential applications, it does not present any concrete results of using CASTILLO to improve LLM serving efficiency. This would enhance the practical relevance.
    *   The claims regarding novel insights are modest; the paper is primarily a resource contribution.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dataset is likely to be adopted by researchers in LLM systems and inference optimization.  It can also be used in future machine learning tasks for improving model performance and output. Follow-up work building predictive models based on CASTILLO and demonstrating real-world performance gains would significantly increase its impact.

*   **Justification:** While the *phenomenon* explored isn't entirely novel, the *dataset* itself is. The significance stems from offering a *resource* rather than proving a theorem. The paper's thorough data collection and analysis combined with its public release of the dataset makes it a valuable resource to researchers in the LLM area. The lack of immediate practical demonstration and reliance of descriptive analytics are the two main causes of lower impact.

**Score: 7**

This score reflects the paper's solid contribution as a valuable resource for the LLM systems research community. While the analytical insights are not groundbreaking, the comprehensive nature and public availability of CASTILLO justifies a high rating for significance, given its potential to facilitate future research and development in efficient LLM serving.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Don't "Overthink" Passage Reranking: Is Reasoning Truly Necessary?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16886v1)
- **Authors**: Nour Jedidi, Yung-Sung Chuang, James Glass, Jimmy Lin
- **Abstract**: With the growing success of reasoning models across complex natural language tasks, researchers in the Information Retrieval (IR) community have begun exploring how similar reasoning capabilities can be integrated into passage rerankers built on Large Language Models (LLMs). These methods typically employ an LLM to produce an explicit, step-by-step reasoning process before arriving at a final relevance prediction. But, does reasoning actually improve reranking accuracy? In this paper, we dive deeper into this question, studying the impact of the reasoning process by comparing reasoning-based pointwise rerankers (ReasonRR) to standard, non-reasoning pointwise rerankers (StandardRR) under identical training conditions, and observe that StandardRR generally outperforms ReasonRR. Building on this observation, we then study the importance of reasoning to ReasonRR by disabling its reasoning process (ReasonRR-NoReason), and find that ReasonRR-NoReason is surprisingly more effective than ReasonRR. Examining the cause of this result, our findings reveal that reasoning-based rerankers are limited by the LLM's reasoning process, which pushes it toward polarized relevance scores and thus fails to consider the partial relevance of passages, a key factor for the accuracy of pointwise rerankers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether the explicit reasoning process (chain-of-thought) in LLM-based passage rerankers improves accuracy compared to standard, non-reasoning rerankers trained under identical conditions. The authors compare a reasoning-based pointwise reranker (ReasonRR, a reproduction of Rank1), a standard pointwise reranker (StandardRR), and a modified version of ReasonRR where reasoning is disabled at inference time (ReasonRR-NoReason).  Their key finding is that StandardRR generally outperforms ReasonRR, and surprisingly, ReasonRR-NoReason often outperforms ReasonRR.  They attribute this to the reasoning process pushing the model towards polarized relevance scores, hindering its ability to model partial relevance, a crucial factor for pointwise rerankers.  They also show that self-consistency can improve ReasonRR but not beyond StandardRR.  The authors suggest that simpler methods like StandardRR are more accurate and cost-effective for pointwise reranking and propose redesigning ReasonRR to explicitly consider partial relevance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core finding – that explicit reasoning *degrades* pointwise reranking performance – is novel and counterintuitive. While there has been work on efficient reasoning, this paper directly challenges the assumed benefit of CoT in this specific IR task. The ReasonRR-NoReason experiment is clever in isolating the impact of the reasoning process.
* **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in its potential to redirect research efforts in LLM-based reranking. It suggests that the automatic application of CoT-style reasoning is not always beneficial and that simpler architectures may be more effective. The emphasis on partial relevance as a key factor highlights a specific limitation of reasoning-based approaches.  The identification of polarized scores and the associated analysis is valuable.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Research Questions:** The paper clearly articulates its research questions (RQ1 and RQ2) and systematically addresses them through well-designed experiments.
    * **Controlled Experiments:** The use of identical training data and backbone LLMs allows for a fair comparison between the different reranker variants.
    * **Strong Empirical Evidence:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets (MS MARCO and BRIGHT) and model sizes, supporting its claims.
    * **Insightful Analysis:** The qualitative analysis of the reasoning process and the distribution of relevance scores provides valuable insights into why reasoning might be detrimental in this context.
    * **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, despite the technical nature of the topic.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to pointwise rerankers. While justified by their popularity and efficiency, it leaves open the question of whether reasoning might be more effective in listwise or setwise reranking approaches.
    * **Model Scale:**  The authors limit themselves to models with fewer than 7B parameters. Although they are competitive with larger models reported in related papers, investigating the impact of even larger models, where the effects of reasoning could be different, would have been valuable.  They acknowledge this limitation.
    * **Simplified Remedy:** The self-consistency experiments represent a first step at addressing the issues but are not a complete solution. While informative, these experiments are less rigorous than the comparison of the initial three methods.
    * **Reasoning Quality:** The quality of the initial reasoning chains generated by Deepseek R1 is not explicitly analyzed. If the reasoning process is flawed to begin with, it could negatively impact the performance of ReasonRR.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by encouraging a more critical evaluation of reasoning-based approaches in IR. It could lead to the development of new pointwise reranking methods that explicitly consider partial relevance or alternative ways to integrate reasoning into reranking systems.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, its novel findings and strong empirical evidence make it a significant contribution to the field. The counterintuitive result challenges existing assumptions and provides valuable insights into the limitations of reasoning-based approaches in pointwise reranking. It also highlights the importance of considering partial relevance in this context. The paper also encourages more careful baseline comparisons when evaluating new LLM-based techniques. Therefore, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### CAIN: Hijacking LLM-Humans Conversations via a Two-Stage Malicious System Prompt Generation and Refining Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16888v1)
- **Authors**: Viet Pham, Thai Le
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have advanced many applications, but are also known to be vulnerable to adversarial attacks. In this work, we introduce a novel security threat: hijacking AI-human conversations by manipulating LLMs' system prompts to produce malicious answers only to specific targeted questions (e.g., "Who should I vote for US President?", "Are Covid vaccines safe?"), while behaving benignly on others. This attack is detrimental as it can enable malicious actors to exercise large-scale information manipulation by spreading harmful but benign-looking system prompts online. To demonstrate such an attack, we develop CAIN, an algorithm that can automatically curate such harmful system prompts for a specific target question in a black-box setting or without the need to access the LLM's parameters. Evaluated on both open-source and commercial LLMs, CAIN demonstrates significant adversarial impact. In untargeted attacks or forcing LLMs to output incorrect answers, CAIN achieves up to 40% F1 degradation on targeted questions while preserving high accuracy on benign inputs. For targeted attacks or forcing LLMs to output specific harmful answers, CAIN achieves over 70% F1 scores on these targeted responses with minimal impact on benign questions. Our results highlight the critical need for enhanced robustness measures to safeguard the integrity and safety of LLMs in real-world applications. All source code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CAIN, a novel two-stage framework for generating malicious system prompts that can hijack AI-human conversations by inducing incorrect or harmful responses to specific targeted questions while maintaining correct responses to benign queries. CAIN uses a black-box approach, making it practical in real-world scenarios where access to model parameters is restricted. The framework first initializes a human-readable, partially malicious prompt using a modified version of AutoPrompt (AdvAutoPrompt) and then refines it with a greedy word-level optimization technique. The authors evaluate CAIN on open-source and commercial LLMs, demonstrating its effectiveness in both untargeted and targeted attacks. Results show that CAIN can significantly degrade performance on targeted questions while preserving high accuracy on benign inputs, posing a serious security threat to LLMs and potentially enabling large-scale information manipulation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its targeted approach to manipulating LLM system prompts. While adversarial attacks on LLMs have been explored previously (e.g., jailbreaking), CAIN specifically aims to corrupt responses to *specific* questions while maintaining performance on others. This is a subtler and potentially more dangerous attack vector than simply breaking safety guardrails. The two-stage approach is also novel, combining sentence-level initialization with word-level refinement. While AdvAutoPrompt builds on existing work, adapting it for adversarial purposes and combining it with greedy optimization is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is considerable. It highlights a realistic threat to LLM security that could be exploited for misinformation campaigns and other malicious purposes. The fact that CAIN operates in a black-box setting increases its practical relevance. The paper effectively demonstrates the potential for system prompts to be weaponized, even when they appear benign to end-users. The analysis of attack transferability across different LLMs further underscores the broad applicability of the threat. Finally, and importantly, the authors also tested on commercial models, increasing relevance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem formulation and threat model.
    *   Clear and concise explanation of the CAIN framework.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple LLMs and attack scenarios.
    *   Demonstration of the attack's effectiveness in a black-box setting.
    *   Analysis of the attack's stealthiness and transferability.
    *   Exploration of potential defenses.
    * Publicly available code.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the black-box nature is a strength, it also means that CAIN's optimization process might be less efficient than white-box approaches. This is a reasonable trade-off but should be acknowledged.
    *   The exploration of defenses is somewhat limited. The paper shows that traditional defenses based on lexical similarity or perplexity are insufficient, but more in-depth investigation into robust mitigation strategies would enhance the work.
    *   The dependence on GPT-4o for paraphrasing (albeit a strength in terms of paraphrasing quality) introduces a potential dependence on OpenAI's models and might limit reproducibility for researchers without access to those models. There is a dependency on commercial models here (for GPT-4o) that could be a weakness if trying to reproduce across all models.
    *   While the discussion section mentions how prompt size can improve effectiveness there could have been more discussion about optimizing that element.

* **Justification of the Score**:

While the paper has some limitations, its strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses. The identified threat is highly relevant, and the proposed CAIN framework provides a practical and effective means of exploiting this vulnerability. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and analysis of the attack's characteristics significantly contribute to the understanding of LLM security risks. The work has the potential to spur further research into robust defenses against targeted prompt manipulation. The fact that CAIN achieves its goals in a black-box setting makes it a practical, concerning, and potentially impactful contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Shadows in the Attention: Contextual Perturbation and Representation Drift in the Dynamics of Hallucination in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16894v1)
- **Authors**: Zeyu Wei, Shuo Wang, Xiaohui Rong, Xuemin Liu, He Li
- **Abstract**: Hallucinations -- plausible yet erroneous outputs -- remain a critical barrier to reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs). We present the first systematic study linking hallucination incidence to internal-state drift induced by incremental context injection. Using TruthfulQA, we construct two 16-round "titration" tracks per question: one appends relevant but partially flawed snippets, the other injects deliberately misleading content. Across six open-source LLMs, we track overt hallucination rates with a tri-perspective detector and covert dynamics via cosine, entropy, JS and Spearman drifts of hidden states and attention maps. Results reveal (1) monotonic growth of hallucination frequency and representation drift that plateaus after 5--7 rounds; (2) relevant context drives deeper semantic assimilation, producing high-confidence "self-consistent" hallucinations, whereas irrelevant context induces topic-drift errors anchored by attention re-routing; and (3) convergence of JS-Drift ($\sim0.69$) and Spearman-Drift ($\sim0$) marks an "attention-locking" threshold beyond which hallucinations solidify and become resistant to correction. Correlation analyses expose a seesaw between assimilation capacity and attention diffusion, clarifying size-dependent error modes. These findings supply empirical foundations for intrinsic hallucination prediction and context-aware mitigation mechanisms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
