The paper investigates the dynamics of hallucination in large language models (LLMs) by systematically tracking internal-state drift caused by injecting incremental contextual information. It constructs "titration" tracks using TruthfulQA, appending relevant (but flawed) and irrelevant snippets to questions. The study analyzes overt hallucination rates using a tri-perspective detector and covert dynamics by measuring drifts in hidden states and attention maps across several open-source LLMs. The findings reveal a monotonic increase in hallucination frequency and representation drift, reaching a plateau after a few rounds. Relevant contexts lead to high-confidence, self-consistent hallucinations, while irrelevant contexts induce topic-drift errors anchored by attention re-routing. The convergence of JS-Drift and Spearman-Drift marks an "attention-locking" threshold where hallucinations become resistant to correction. Correlation analyses show a balance between assimilation capacity and attention diffusion, clarifying size-dependent error modes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and fine-grained approach to studying hallucination dynamics. While previous studies have explored hallucinations, this work offers a controlled context manipulation paradigm, allowing for a detailed examination of internal-state drift. Tracking both overt hallucination rates and covert internal state changes provides a more comprehensive understanding than isolated approaches. The "attention-locking" threshold is also a potentially novel observation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its contribution to understanding the underlying mechanisms of hallucination. Identifying the relationship between context-induced representation drift and hallucination frequency provides valuable insights for developing more robust and reliable LLMs. The findings on "attention locking" and the balance between assimilation and diffusion offer promising avenues for targeted mitigation strategies. The cross-model validation strengthens the generalizability of the results.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Approach:** The controlled context manipulation paradigm is a major strength, allowing for precise control and measurement of hallucination dynamics.
    *   **Multi-faceted Analysis:** Combining overt hallucination detection with covert internal-state tracking provides a rich and nuanced understanding of the processes involved.
    *   **Cross-Model Validation:** Evaluating the findings across multiple LLMs enhances the generalizability and robustness of the conclusions.
    *   **Clear Identification of Key Phenomena:** The "attention-locking" threshold and the balance between assimilation and diffusion are important, actionable findings.
    *   **Well-organized writing:** The paper is organized in a very detailed manner, enhancing readability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on TruthfulQA:** The study relies heavily on TruthfulQA, which might have its own inherent biases. While designed to elicit hallucinations, its particular question types might not generalize to all scenarios.
    *   **Limited Model Selection:** While covering several open-source LLMs, the diversity could be improved by including a broader range of architectures and training data.
    *   **Causality claims may be overstated.** It is hard to verify if context directly leads to hallucinations, or if these patterns emerge together.
    *   **Limited Actionability:** While the findings provide insights, their direct applicability to practical mitigation strategies is not fully demonstrated in the paper. Further research is needed to translate these insights into effective interventions.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research on hallucination mitigation. The identified mechanisms of representation drift, attention re-routing, and attention locking can inform the development of novel detection and correction techniques. The findings may also motivate new architectural designs that promote more stable and reliable internal representations.

*   **Justification for Score:** Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field, but is far from providing a solution to the problem. The systematic methodology and the identification of key phenomena like attention locking are significant advances. However, the limitations in dataset reliance, model diversity, and actionability prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking study.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Code Graph Model (CGM): A Graph-Integrated Large Language Model for Repository-Level Software Engineering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16901v1)
- **Authors**: Hongyuan Tao, Ying Zhang, Zhenhao Tang, Hongen Peng, Xukun Zhu, Bingchang Liu, Yingguang Yang, Ziyin Zhang, Zhaogui Xu, Haipeng Zhang, Linchao Zhu, Rui Wang, Hang Yu, Jianguo Li, Peng Di
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise in function-level code generation, yet repository-level software engineering tasks remain challenging. Current solutions predominantly rely on proprietary LLM agents, which introduce unpredictability and limit accessibility, raising concerns about data privacy and model customization. This paper investigates whether open-source LLMs can effectively address repository-level tasks without requiring agent-based approaches. We demonstrate this is possible by enabling LLMs to comprehend functions and files within codebases through their semantic information and structural dependencies. To this end, we introduce Code Graph Models (CGMs), which integrate repository code graph structures into the LLM's attention mechanism and map node attributes to the LLM's input space using a specialized adapter. When combined with an agentless graph RAG framework, our approach achieves a 43.00% resolution rate on the SWE-bench Lite benchmark using the open-source Qwen2.5-72B model. This performance ranks first among open weight models, second among methods with open-source systems, and eighth overall, surpassing the previous best open-source model-based method by 12.33%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Code Graph Model (CGM): A Graph-Integrated Large Language Model for Repository-Level Software Engineering Tasks":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Code Graph Models (CGMs), a novel approach to improving the performance of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) on repository-level software engineering tasks, such as issue fixing and code completion.  The core idea is to integrate the structure and semantic information of code repositories into the LLM's attention mechanism. This is achieved by:

1.  **Constructing a Code Graph:** Representing the repository as a graph with nodes representing code entities (files, functions, classes) and edges representing dependencies (calls, imports, inheritance).
2.  **Semantic Integration:** Encoding node attributes (code, comments) using a pre-trained text encoder and mapping them to the LLM's input space via an adapter.
3.  **Structural Integration:** Modifying the LLM's attention mask to allow direct message passing only between neighboring nodes in the code graph.
4.  **Graph RAG Framework:** The CGM leverages a Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework, consisting of Rewriter, Retriever, Reranker, and Reader (CGM) modules, for focusing on most relevant subgraphs of the code graph.
5.  **Open-source LLM framework:** CGM utilizes an open-source LLM and provides a framework for developers to customize and extend the system.

The authors demonstrate that their approach, using the open-source Qwen2.5-72B model, achieves a 43.00% resolution rate on the SWE-bench Lite benchmark, outperforming previous open-source methods and approaching the performance of closed-source agent-based systems. They also demonstrate its effectiveness on other repository-level tasks, such as code completion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the effective integration of code graph structure into an LLM's attention mechanism, moving beyond simple text-based representations of code repositories. The adapter mechanism for semantic integration and the graph-aware attention mask are also novel contributions. The agentless RAG framework, while leveraging existing RAG concepts, is tailored effectively for the code domain and open-source LLMs.
*   **Significance:**  The work is significant for several reasons:
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Agent-based Systems:** It offers a viable alternative to LLM agents, which often suffer from unpredictability, lack of transparency, and dependence on closed-source models.
    *   **Improving Open-Source LLMs for Code:** The CGM approach significantly boosts the performance of open-source LLMs on complex software engineering tasks, making them more competitive with closed-source counterparts.
    *   **Enhancing Code Understanding:** The integration of code graph structure allows the LLM to better understand the relationships between code entities, leading to improved performance.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The CGM approach is demonstrated to be effective on real-world benchmarks and tasks, suggesting its potential for practical application in software development workflows.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Methodology:** The paper presents a clear and well-defined methodology for integrating code graphs into LLMs.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on SWE-bench Lite and other benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the CGM approach.
    *   **Open-Source Implementation:** The authors provide an open-source implementation of their method, increasing its accessibility and enabling further research and development.
    *   **Comprehensive Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies and analysis show the importance of the model's components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Support:** The experiments primarily focus on Python (and some Java), so the generalizability to other programming languages is not fully established. While the authors discuss code graphs supporting multiple languages, the results are mostly Python-centric.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper presents computational cost analysis, there is room for improvements, like optimizing for specific scenarios.
    *   **Generalization Beyond Benchmarks:** While SWE-bench is a valuable benchmark, further evaluation in diverse real-world projects would strengthen the claims. The limitations section acknowledges this issue, which is important.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The related works section includes Agentless [2] which has been retracted. It seems reasonable to compare it to Agentless-v1.5.

**Score: 8.5/10**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a crucial limitation of open-source LLMs in software engineering – the lack of effective understanding of code repository structure. The CGM approach offers a novel and effective solution, achieving state-of-the-art results among open-source methods and approaching closed-source performance.

The work is well-motivated, clearly presented, and supported by strong empirical results. The open-source implementation and detailed ablation studies further enhance its value and impact.

The major limitation is a focus on Python with more Java result analysis, with a limited scope on generalizability for different coding paradigms; however, the core ideas are relatively solid, and this could inspire a useful line of future research. The overall significance and novelty justify a high score, and the work represents a valuable step towards more powerful and transparent automated software engineering tools.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Unsupervised Prompting for Graph Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16903v1)
- **Authors**: Peyman Baghershahi, Sourav Medya
- **Abstract**: Prompt tuning methods for Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have become popular to address the semantic gap between pre-training and fine-tuning steps. However, existing GNN prompting methods rely on labeled data and involve lightweight fine-tuning for downstream tasks. Meanwhile, in-context learning methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promising performance with no parameter updating and no or minimal labeled data. Inspired by these approaches, in this work, we first introduce a challenging problem setup to evaluate GNN prompting methods. This setup encourages a prompting function to enhance a pre-trained GNN's generalization to a target dataset under covariate shift without updating the GNN's parameters and with no labeled data. Next, we propose a fully unsupervised prompting method based on consistency regularization through pseudo-labeling. We use two regularization techniques to align the prompted graphs' distribution with the original data and reduce biased predictions. Through extensive experiments under our problem setting, we demonstrate that our unsupervised approach outperforms the state-of-the-art prompting methods that have access to labels.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unsupervised Prompting for Graph Neural Networks."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, UGPROMPT, for prompting Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) in an unsupervised manner. The core idea is to address the "semantic gap" between pre-training and fine-tuning GNNs without relying on labeled data or fine-tuning the GNN parameters themselves. The authors propose a new problem setup, Unsupervised Graph Prompting Problem (UGPP), to benchmark GNN prompting methods under a covariate shift between the pre-training and target datasets. UGPROMPT leverages consistency regularization and pseudo-labeling, training a prompting function to align the pre-trained GNN's knowledge with the target distribution using confident pseudo-labels derived from augmented graphs. They also introduce regularization techniques to prevent biased predictions due to class imbalance and out-of-distribution issues. Through experiments on various graph tasks, the authors demonstrate that UGPROMPT outperforms state-of-the-art prompting methods that *do* use labeled data, even giving competitors access to labels.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its unsupervised approach to GNN prompting. Existing GNN prompting methods typically require labeled data for fine-tuning or training prompting parameters. The UGPROMPT framework addresses this limitation by leveraging consistency regularization and pseudo-labeling, drawing inspiration from unsupervised learning techniques successfully applied in other domains (like LLMs) but adapting them specifically for the graph domain.  The formulation of the UGPP benchmark is also a valuable contribution, explicitly isolating the prompting function's effectiveness in a realistic covariate shift setting. The idea of interpreting a prompted graph as a strongly augmented instance and utilizing pseudo-labels for training the prompting function is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-faceted:

    *   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** Unsupervised GNN prompting tackles the practical problem of limited labeled data in many graph-related applications. By eliminating the need for labels during the prompting stage, the framework enables broader applicability across diverse, unlabeled datasets.
    *   **Improved Generalization:** By freezing the pre-trained GNN parameters and focusing on learning an effective prompting function, the approach promotes better generalization to new datasets with distributional shifts. The experiments demonstrate this benefit compared to methods that fine-tune the base GNN model.
    *   **Benchmarking and Methodological Advancement:** The UGPP benchmark encourages future research into unsupervised GNN prompting techniques. UGPROMPT provides a solid foundation and a benchmark for evaluating new methods. The use of consistency regularization and pseudo-labeling in the GNN prompting context could inspire further research into other unsupervised learning techniques tailored for graphs.
    *   **Practical Application:** The proposed approach has the potential to be integrated into real-world graph-based applications, especially where labeled data is expensive or unavailable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The UGPP provides a clear and challenging problem formulation for evaluating GNN prompting methods.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The UGPROMPT framework is well-designed, leveraging established unsupervised learning techniques in a novel and effective manner.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are thorough, covering a range of graph tasks, datasets with different distribution shifts, and comparisons against strong baselines. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components of the framework. The design choice for the specific augmentation and prompting methods are well justified by analysis and results.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The availability of the code promotes reproducibility and further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While effective, UGPROMPT introduces some complexity, requiring careful tuning of hyperparameters related to augmentation, thresholding, and regularization.
    *   **Limited Scope of Unification:** The unification only looks at graph classification, the effectiveness of generalization is not well evaluated against the variety of tasks downstream.
    *   **Augmentation Dependence**: Some results depend heavily on the alignment of augmentation to the task and data (continuous vs discrete values) indicating it is easy to get the method wrong without good justification.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of GNNs by promoting the development of unsupervised prompting techniques. It could lead to the creation of more robust and generalizable GNN models that can be applied to a wider range of real-world problems. Also, the framework can be easily adapted to other areas of machine learning in general.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    *   The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of GNNs by introducing an unsupervised prompting framework. The technical approach is sound, the experiments are comprehensive, and the results are convincing. While there are some complexities and limitations, the overall impact of the paper is substantial.
    *   It tackles a practical problem (data scarcity) and demonstrates significant improvements over existing methods. The UGPP benchmark provides a valuable tool for future research.
    *   Although the core components (consistency regularization, pseudo-labeling) are not entirely new, the application and adaptation of these techniques to the GNN prompting context is innovative and effective. Also the choice of data domain and the constraints are novel.

### Backdoor Cleaning without External Guidance in MLLM Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16916v1)
- **Authors**: Xuankun Rong, Wenke Huang, Jian Liang, Jinhe Bi, Xun Xiao, Yiming Li, Bo Du, Mang Ye
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in fine-tuning-as-a-service (FTaaS) settings, where user-submitted datasets adapt general-purpose models to downstream tasks. This flexibility, however, introduces serious security risks, as malicious fine-tuning can implant backdoors into MLLMs with minimal effort. In this paper, we observe that backdoor triggers systematically disrupt cross-modal processing by causing abnormal attention concentration on non-semantic regions--a phenomenon we term attention collapse. Based on this insight, we propose Believe Your Eyes (BYE), a data filtering framework that leverages attention entropy patterns as self-supervised signals to identify and filter backdoor samples. BYE operates via a three-stage pipeline: (1) extracting attention maps using the fine-tuned model, (2) computing entropy scores and profiling sensitive layers via bimodal separation, and (3) performing unsupervised clustering to remove suspicious samples. Unlike prior defenses, BYE equires no clean supervision, auxiliary labels, or model modifications. Extensive experiments across various datasets, models, and diverse trigger types validate BYE's effectiveness: it achieves near-zero attack success rates while maintaining clean-task performance, offering a robust and generalizable solution against backdoor threats in MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the security risks introduced by fine-tuning-as-a-service (FTaaS) settings in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It identifies a vulnerability where malicious fine-tuning can implant backdoors into MLLMs through poisoned datasets. The core observation is that backdoor triggers cause "attention collapse," where the model abnormally concentrates attention on non-semantic regions. Based on this, the authors propose "Believe Your Eyes" (BYE), an unsupervised data filtering framework. BYE extracts attention maps, computes entropy scores to profile sensitive layers, and uses unsupervised clustering to remove suspicious samples. The method doesn't require clean supervision, auxiliary labels, or model modifications. The experiments demonstrate that BYE effectively reduces attack success rates while maintaining clean-task performance across various datasets and models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in two areas: 1) Identifying and characterizing the "attention collapse" phenomenon in MLLMs under backdoor attacks, and 2) Using attention entropy as a self-supervised signal for backdoor detection in MLLMs. While attention mechanisms have been studied extensively, the specific observation regarding attention collapse due to backdoors in MLLMs is a valuable contribution. Further, the unsupervised approach of using attention entropy is novel compared to prior backdoor defense techniques that require clean data or labeled supervision.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. MLLMs are vulnerable to backdoor attacks, especially in FTaaS scenarios. A practical, unsupervised defense method like BYE is needed. The experimental results seem convincing and show the efficacy of the method across different datasets and models. The limitations, such as the fact that the method operates offline, and single stage fine-tuning, are valid concerns. Additionally, the paper assumes that an attacker is unaware of the BYE defense. Adaptive backdoor attacks might be crafted to circumvent the attention entropy filter. The method also concentrates on visual triggers but other modality types are feasible.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unsupervised Approach:** Requires no clean data or auxiliary labels, making it practical for real-world FTaaS settings.
    *   **Model-Intrinsic Signal:** Leverages the model's internal attention mechanisms, potentially making it more robust against model variations.
    *   **Attention Collapse Identification:** The observed attention collapse effect provides a good intuition for developing backdoor defense strategies.
    *   **Generalizability:** Demonstrated effectiveness across diverse datasets and MLLM architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Offline Processing:** BYE operates as a pre-processing step, not an online or training-time defense.
    *   **Limited Attack Scenarios:** The method mainly focuses on patch-based visual triggers. Its effectiveness against other attack vectors (e.g., instruction-based triggers, more sophisticated adversarial triggers) is less clear. While multi-triggers are examined in the ablation study, it is still in controlled cases.
    *   **Potential for Adaptive Attacks:** If the attacker is aware of BYE, they might devise strategies to circumvent the attention entropy filter (e.g., using very subtle triggers that don't cause drastic attention shifts or those that more efficiently collude with attention patterns).
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study's focus is mainly on single-stage fine-tuning; extension to continual or task-transfer learning is not explored.
    *   The paper may overestimate the "generality" of the proposed method and it would be prudent to include specific future work to address these limitations.

*   **Impact:** BYE could influence the development of more robust and secure MLLM deployment strategies, especially in FTaaS settings. The work could encourage the use of model-internal signals for backdoor defense. Other researchers may explore different model signals to augment BYE, or to identify other modalities of backdoor attacks.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practical approach to backdoor defense in MLLMs by exploiting the attention collapse phenomenon. The unsupervised nature of the method and its demonstrated effectiveness across various datasets are valuable contributions. However, the limitations regarding offline processing, potential susceptibility to adaptive attacks, and the relatively narrow focus on patch-based visual triggers prevent it from achieving a higher score. The observed effects in different models makes this a worthy contribution, even if the approach has limitations. However, this is a good solution which merits further exploration in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### UNCLE: Uncertainty Expressions in Long-Form Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16922v1)
- **Authors**: Ruihan Yang, Caiqi Zhang, Zhisong Zhang, Xinting Huang, Dong Yu, Nigel Collier, Deqing Yang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are prone to hallucination, particularly in long-form generations. A promising direction to mitigate hallucination is to teach LLMs to express uncertainty explicitly when they lack sufficient knowledge. However, existing work lacks direct and fair evaluation of LLMs' ability to express uncertainty effectively in long-form generation. To address this gap, we first introduce UNCLE, a benchmark designed to evaluate uncertainty expression in both long- and short-form question answering (QA). UNCLE spans five domains and comprises 4k long-form QA instances and over 20k short-form QA pairs. Our dataset is the first to directly bridge short- and long-form QA with paired questions and gold-standard answers. Along with the benchmark, we propose a suite of new metrics to assess the models' capabilities to selectively express uncertainty. Using UNCLE, we then demonstrate that current models fail to convey uncertainty appropriately in long-form generation. We further explore both prompt-based and training-based methods to improve models' performance, with the training-based methods yielding greater gains. Further analysis of alignment gaps between short- and long-form uncertainty expression highlights promising directions for future research using UNCLE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNCLE, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to express uncertainty in both long-form and short-form question answering. The dataset spans five domains, pairing long-form questions with related short-form questions and corresponding ground truth answers for key aspects of the long-form question. The authors propose a suite of new metrics (Factual Accuracy, Uncertain Accuracy, Known to Correct Rate, Unknown to Uncertain Rate, and Expression Accuracy) to evaluate models' uncertainty expression capabilities. They then evaluate several popular LLMs on UNCLE, finding that current models struggle to express uncertainty effectively in long-form generation. Finally, they explore prompt-based and training-based methods to improve performance, showing that training-based methods yield greater gains, although a trade-off exists between performance on short and long forms, and that better results can be achieved with mixed data training. The authors also analyze alignment between short and long-form uncertainty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a contribution through its new benchmark dataset UNCLE. The pairing of short and long-form questions and answers, focusing on key aspects within the long-form context, is a novel approach. This setup enables a more direct and controlled evaluation of uncertainty expression compared to previous open-ended generation tasks. The proposed metrics offer a more nuanced perspective on assessing models' ability to selectively express uncertainty, going beyond simple binary correctness scores. The evaluation of different LLMs on the dataset and the exploration of training methods all adds incrementally to the overall novelty of the work.

*   **Significance:** Hallucination in LLMs is a major problem, and this paper addresses the problem of uncertainty expression, a promising mitigation strategy. The construction of UNCLE directly targets a significant gap in current evaluation practices by providing a structured benchmark designed to assess how well models can express uncertainty *selectively*. The paper's findings, highlighting the deficiencies of current models in long-form uncertainty expression, emphasize the importance of this research direction. This benchmark may enable new research into methods that allow LLMs to better convey what they don't know, improving their trustworthiness and reliability. Furthermore, their exploration of training strategies reveals a promising path toward improving models' ability to express uncertainty, with potential implications for diverse applications reliant on accurate and reliable information generation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Design:** UNCLE is well-motivated and thoughtfully designed, offering a valuable resource for the community. The focus on key aspects improves comparability across models.
    *   **Metrics:** The proposed metrics provide a comprehensive evaluation of uncertainty expression, addressing the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Empirical Analysis:** The experimental results provide valuable insights into the performance of existing LLMs and the effectiveness of different training strategies.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly identifies the challenges in evaluating and improving uncertainty expression in long-form generation.
    *   **Good Analysis:** The paper does a good job of examining factors that affect expression of uncertainty, such as prompting strategies and data balance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** While valuable, the dataset is still relatively modest in size (4k long-form questions). This could potentially limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Model Types:** The evaluation could benefit from including a wider range of LLMs, particularly those with architectures explicitly designed for factual accuracy or uncertainty handling.
    *   **Fact-checking automation:** Reliance on GPT-4 for evaluating the performance of the LLMs has its own limitations in accuracy and bias.

*   **Potential Influence:** UNCLE has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the field, driving progress in uncertainty-aware LLMs. The proposed metrics could be adopted by other researchers for evaluating their models. The empirical findings and training strategies explored in the paper will likely stimulate further research in this area. The benchmark also opens possibilities to more comprehensively analyse uncertainty expression in long-form generation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper delivers solid novelty and significant value through the creation of the UNCLE dataset and corresponding evaluation metrics. While the weaknesses are valid (small dataset, limited models evaluated, GPT-4 reliance), the strengths outweigh these limitations. The impact is likely to be felt by researchers working on improving reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs, a current concern within the community.

### LLaDA-V: Large Language Diffusion Models with Visual Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16933v1)
- **Authors**: Zebin You, Shen Nie, Xiaolu Zhang, Jun Hu, Jun Zhou, Zhiwu Lu, Ji-Rong Wen, Chongxuan Li
- **Abstract**: In this work, we introduce LLaDA-V, a purely diffusion-based Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) that integrates visual instruction tuning with masked diffusion models, representing a departure from the autoregressive paradigms dominant in current multimodal approaches. Built upon LLaDA, a representative large language diffusion model, LLaDA-V incorporates a vision encoder and MLP connector that projects visual features into the language embedding space, enabling effective multimodal alignment. Our empirical investigation reveals several intriguing results: First, LLaDA-V demonstrates promising multimodal performance despite its language model being weaker on purely textual tasks than counterparts like LLaMA3-8B and Qwen2-7B. When trained on the same instruction data, LLaDA-V is highly competitive to LLaMA3-V across multimodal tasks with better data scalability. It also narrows the performance gap to Qwen2-VL, suggesting the effectiveness of its architecture for multimodal tasks. Second, LLaDA-V achieves state-of-the-art performance in multimodal understanding compared to existing hybrid autoregressive-diffusion and purely diffusion-based MLLMs. Our findings suggest that large language diffusion models show promise in multimodal contexts and warrant further investigation in future research. Project page and codes: https://ml-gsai.github.io/LLaDA-V-demo/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LLaDA-V paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLaDA-V, a novel multimodal large language model (MLLM) built using a purely diffusion-based approach. This contrasts with many existing MLLMs that rely on autoregressive architectures. LLaDA-V extends the existing LLaDA language diffusion model by incorporating a vision encoder and MLP connector to process and align visual features with the language embedding space. The authors conduct experiments to assess LLaDA-V's multimodal performance, data scalability, and compare it to autoregressive and hybrid autoregressive-diffusion models. Results indicate promising multimodal capabilities, especially in multidisciplinary knowledge and mathematical reasoning tasks, and good data scalability. LLaDA-V achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to other hybrid and purely diffusion-based MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in demonstrating the feasibility and potential of purely diffusion-based models for multimodal understanding at a scale competitive with autoregressive models, even when the underlying language model is less powerful than its autoregressive counterparts. While other attempts have been made at incorporating diffusion models into MLLMs, most have relied on autoregressive components for strong language modeling or have used discrete diffusion approaches with limited language capabilities. LLaDA-V presents a more complete diffusion-based MLLM architecture, using a vision encoder and instruction tuning approach. This specific combination is not entirely new since other MLLMs have employed similar visual instruction tuning, but the application of this technique within a *purely* diffusion-based architecture is where the innovation lies.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is multifaceted.

*   **Exploration of Alternative Paradigms:** The paper challenges the dominance of autoregressive models in MLLMs, suggesting that diffusion models can be a viable alternative. This opens avenues for exploring different probabilistic modeling techniques for multimodal understanding.
*   **Data Scalability Insights:** The paper provides valuable insights into the data scalability of diffusion-based MLLMs, showing that LLaDA-V's performance continues to improve with more training data, particularly in knowledge-intensive tasks. This has implications for future MLLM development.
*   **Architecture Choices:** The investigation of different attention mechanisms and the choice of a bidirectional attention is noteworthy and well-motivated.
*   **State-of-the-art Results:** The results showing LLaDA-V achieving state-of-the-art performance among *both hybrid and purely diffusion-based* MLLMs validates the approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing MLLMs and motivates the exploration of diffusion models.
*   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The architecture of LLaDA-V is well-defined, and the rationale behind each component is explained.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments to evaluate LLaDA-V's performance across various benchmarks and compared to different baselines.
*   **Analysis and Insights:** The paper provides valuable insights into the data scalability and architectural choices of diffusion-based MLLMs.
*   The project page with code and demo is a great addition making the work more reproducible.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Language Model Limitations:** The paper acknowledges that LLaDA-V uses a weaker language model than some autoregressive counterparts (Qwen2-7B). While the paper argues that this highlights the effectiveness of the diffusion architecture for multimodal understanding, it also presents a potential limitation. It's important to establish that the architecture's strengths are not compensating for deficiencies in the diffusion language model itself. A stronger diffusion language model (if one existed) would have provided a clearer comparison to state of the art autoregressive models.
*   **Image resolution limitations:** As detailed in the limitation paragraph, there is no discussion about the effects the image resolution and splitting up the image has on results.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper includes an ablation study on attention mask, but further ablation studies exploring other design choices (e.g., different vision encoders, MLP connector architectures, training strategies) could have provided deeper insights.

**Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in MLLMs by highlighting the promise of diffusion models and by providing a framework for building purely diffusion-based MLLMs. The paper's insights into data scalability and architectural choices can guide future development efforts. However, the paper's influence may be limited by the fact that autoregressive models continue to dominate the MLLM landscape and that further progress in diffusion language models is needed.

**Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of multimodal learning.** It demonstrates that a purely diffusion-based MLLM can achieve competitive performance, especially in tasks requiring complex reasoning, and offers a new perspective on how to build such models.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant engineering effort resulting in a competitive MLLM built on diffusion techniques, but doesn't constitute a groundbreaking conceptual shift. The limitations of the underlying language model and further ablation studies prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### In-Context Watermarks for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16934v1)
- **Authors**: Yepeng Liu, Xuandong Zhao, Christopher Kruegel, Dawn Song, Yuheng Bu
- **Abstract**: The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for sensitive applications has highlighted the need for effective watermarking techniques to ensure the provenance and accountability of AI-generated text. However, most existing watermarking methods require access to the decoding process, limiting their applicability in real-world settings. One illustrative example is the use of LLMs by dishonest reviewers in the context of academic peer review, where conference organizers have no access to the model used but still need to detect AI-generated reviews. Motivated by this gap, we introduce In-Context Watermarking (ICW), which embeds watermarks into generated text solely through prompt engineering, leveraging LLMs' in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. We investigate four ICW strategies at different levels of granularity, each paired with a tailored detection method. We further examine the Indirect Prompt Injection (IPI) setting as a specific case study, in which watermarking is covertly triggered by modifying input documents such as academic manuscripts. Our experiments validate the feasibility of ICW as a model-agnostic, practical watermarking approach. Moreover, our findings suggest that as LLMs become more capable, ICW offers a promising direction for scalable and accessible content attribution.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can help with that. Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "In-Context Watermarks for Large Language Models" paper, including a novelty/significance score and rigorous justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces In-Context Watermarking (ICW), a novel approach to watermarking text generated by large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing methods that require access to the LLM's decoding process, ICW embeds watermarks solely through prompt engineering, leveraging the in-context learning and instruction-following capabilities of LLMs.  The paper explores four distinct ICW strategies at varying levels of granularity (Unicode, Initials, Lexical, and Acrostics), each paired with a custom detection method.  It also investigates the application of ICW in the Indirect Prompt Injection (IPI) setting, specifically focusing on detecting AI-generated peer reviews in academic conferences.  The authors present experimental results demonstrating the feasibility of ICW as a model-agnostic and practical watermarking approach, with the effectiveness dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:**  The primary strength lies in the innovative approach of embedding watermarks *without* direct access to the LLM's internal mechanisms. This addresses a significant limitation of existing watermarking techniques and broadens the applicability of content attribution to scenarios where the model is a black box (e.g., using a third-party LLM API). The four proposed ICW methods offer a good range of granularity levels and provide flexibility in terms of LLM requirements and detectability.
    *   **Practical Significance:**  The IPI use case, specifically the detection of AI-generated peer reviews, is highly relevant and addresses a real concern within the academic community. It demonstrates the practical application of ICW to a challenging and important problem.
    *   **Model Agnostic and Accessible:** The core concept of ICW relies on leveraging capabilities readily available in state-of-the-art LLMs, such as prompt engineering and instruction following, and does not require retraining or modifications.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a well-structured evaluation, exploring detectability, robustness (against editing and paraphrasing), and text quality. It compares the proposed ICW methods with existing baselines (where applicable).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:**  The paper acknowledges that the effectiveness of ICW is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. This is a significant limitation, as the proposed methods might not be effective on less powerful models or models with weaker instruction-following abilities.
    *   **Vulnerability to Sophisticated Adversaries:** The paper discusses one potential attack, but it is relatively limited in scope. More sophisticated adversarial techniques, such as fine-tuning a model to resist ICW or using more complex prompt manipulation, might be able to bypass the proposed watermarking schemes.
    *   **Limited Robustness Information in IPI:** The IPI robustness results are only provided through table 6 in the appendix. As one of the most important demonstrations of ICW's potential, the IPI should have received a more prominent presence in the robustness section.
    *   **Limited Generality of Green Words:** Lexical ICW relies heavily on the selection of green words. The paper only evaluates one example of green words, and it does not sufficiently evaluate this approach's validity and application in other fields.
    *   **Some Methods are Less Practical:** The Unicode ICW method, while simple and placing minimal requirements on LLMs, is easily defeated by most text processing pipelines and lacks robustness. Its practical utility is limited.
    *   **Lack of a theoretical framework:** The authors don't provide a sound mathematical framework for justifying the effectiveness of ICW. Instead, the authors rely almost exclusively on empirically-based experiments to prove the effectiveness of ICW.

*   **Overall Significance and Potential Influence:**

    The paper makes a valuable contribution by exploring a new and accessible direction for LLM watermarking. The concept of embedding watermarks through prompt engineering has the potential to significantly broaden the scope of content attribution and provenance tracking. While the current methods have limitations, they represent a promising starting point for future research. As LLMs continue to evolve and become more capable, ICW could become a more practical and effective approach.

    The IPI demonstration is also significant because it shows the potential for covertly embedding instructions into a manuscript. This demonstrates the versatility of ICW, proving its potential for AI misuse detection.
    The impact of this work will likely depend on the further development of more robust and adaptable ICW techniques, as well as the adoption of these techniques by organizations that need to trace the origin of AI-generated content.

**Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 7.5 reflects the significant novelty of the in-context watermarking approach and its potential impact on content attribution. However, the limitations related to LLM dependency, adversarial robustness, and the early stage of development prevent a higher score. The demonstration of IPI's usefulness is also a major aspect of the score. Future research addressing these weaknesses could significantly increase the impact and practical applicability of ICW.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AGENTIF: Benchmarking Instruction Following of Large Language Models in Agentic Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16944v1)
- **Authors**: Yunjia Qi, Hao Peng, Xiaozhi Wang, Amy Xin, Youfeng Liu, Bin Xu, Lei Hou, Juanzi Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated advanced capabilities in real-world agentic applications. Growing research efforts aim to develop LLM-based agents to address practical demands, introducing a new challenge: agentic scenarios often involve lengthy instructions with complex constraints, such as extended system prompts and detailed tool specifications. While adherence to such instructions is crucial for agentic applications, whether LLMs can reliably follow them remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce AgentIF, the first benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM instruction following ability in agentic scenarios. AgentIF features three key characteristics: (1) Realistic, constructed from 50 real-world agentic applications. (2) Long, averaging 1,723 words with a maximum of 15,630 words. (3) Complex, averaging 11.9 constraints per instruction, covering diverse constraint types, such as tool specifications and condition constraints. To construct AgentIF, we collect 707 human-annotated instructions across 50 agentic tasks from industrial application agents and open-source agentic systems. For each instruction, we annotate the associated constraints and corresponding evaluation metrics, including code-based evaluation, LLM-based evaluation, and hybrid code-LLM evaluation. We use AgentIF to systematically evaluate existing advanced LLMs. We observe that current models generally perform poorly, especially in handling complex constraint structures and tool specifications. We further conduct error analysis and analytical experiments on instruction length and meta constraints, providing some findings about the failure modes of existing LLMs. We have released the code and data to facilitate future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AGENTIF: Benchmarking Instruction Following of Large Language Models in Agentic Scenarios":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AGENTIF, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the instruction-following capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) specifically in agentic scenarios. These scenarios often involve long, complex instructions with various constraints like tool specifications, condition-based requirements, and formatting guidelines. AGENTIF is constructed from real-world agentic applications, featuring long instructions (average of 1723 words) and a high number of diverse constraints (average of 11.9 per instruction). The authors collect 707 instructions and annotate them with associated constraints and appropriate evaluation metrics, including code-based, LLM-based, and hybrid approaches. The paper then evaluates existing LLMs on AGENTIF, revealing that current models perform poorly, especially when dealing with complex constraints and tool specifications. Error analysis and analytical experiments related to instruction length and meta-constraints are also conducted, providing insights into failure modes.  The dataset and code are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper tackles a crucial and under-explored problem: the ability of LLMs to reliably follow complex instructions in agentic contexts. Existing instruction-following benchmarks often focus on simpler, shorter instructions, failing to capture the complexities of real-world agentic applications.
*   **Realistic and Complex Benchmark:** The use of real-world agentic applications ensures the benchmark's relevance and ecological validity. The long instruction lengths and the diverse array of constraints accurately reflect the challenges encountered in practical agentic setups. The high quality of annotations adds significant value.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Methodology:** The multi-faceted evaluation approach, incorporating code-based, LLM-based, and hybrid methods, is well-designed and allows for a thorough assessment of LLM performance. The inclusion of meta-constraints is also novel and valuable.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The error analysis and experiments on instruction length and constraint quantity provide valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs and suggest areas for future improvement. The observation that thinking models perform better is also noteworthy.
*   **Public Availability:** Releasing the benchmark data and code promotes reproducibility and allows for community-driven advancements in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Evaluation:** While the paper evaluates several prominent LLMs, the scope could be expanded to include a wider range of models, particularly those specifically designed for instruction following and agentic tasks.
*   **Dependence on GPT-4 for Evaluation:** Reliance on GPT-4 for evaluation introduces a potential bias. While LLM-based evaluation is necessary for some constraint types, it would be beneficial to explore alternative evaluation strategies or utilize an ensemble of evaluators to mitigate this bias. The limited annotation scale in the paper also poses potential risks of model bias to gpt-4.
*   **Limited exploration of fine-tuning:** The paper mainly focuses on evaluating the performance of existing LLMs without exploring fine-tuning or adaptation methods to improve their performance on AGENTIF. Adding such experiments would further strengthen the paper's contributions.
*   **English and Chinese focus:** The limitation to English and Chinese instructions limits the global accessibility and applicability of the benchmark.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper is highly novel. AGENTIF addresses a critical gap in existing benchmarks and has the potential to significantly advance research on instruction-following in agentic scenarios. The insights gained from the evaluation and analysis are valuable for guiding future LLM development and for designing effective agentic systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a highly relevant, well-constructed benchmark that tackles an important problem in the field of LLMs and agentic systems. The detailed analysis provides valuable insights, and the public release of the data and code will undoubtedly facilitate further research. The weaknesses are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. The dependence on GPT-4 for evaluation creates potential bias.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### MixAT: Combining Continuous and Discrete Adversarial Training for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16947v1)
- **Authors**: Csaba Dékány, Stefan Balauca, Robin Staab, Dimitar I. Dimitrov, Martin Vechev
- **Abstract**: Despite recent efforts in Large Language Models (LLMs) safety and alignment, current adversarial attacks on frontier LLMs are still able to force harmful generations consistently. Although adversarial training has been widely studied and shown to significantly improve the robustness of traditional machine learning models, its strengths and weaknesses in the context of LLMs are less understood. Specifically, while existing discrete adversarial attacks are effective at producing harmful content, training LLMs with concrete adversarial prompts is often computationally expensive, leading to reliance on continuous relaxations. As these relaxations do not correspond to discrete input tokens, such latent training methods often leave models vulnerable to a diverse set of discrete attacks. In this work, we aim to bridge this gap by introducing MixAT, a novel method that combines stronger discrete and faster continuous attacks during training. We rigorously evaluate MixAT across a wide spectrum of state-of-the-art attacks, proposing the At Least One Attack Success Rate (ALO-ASR) metric to capture the worst-case vulnerability of models. We show MixAT achieves substantially better robustness (ALO-ASR < 20%) compared to prior defenses (ALO-ASR > 50%), while maintaining a runtime comparable to methods based on continuous relaxations. We further analyze MixAT in realistic deployment settings, exploring how chat templates, quantization, low-rank adapters, and temperature affect both adversarial training and evaluation, revealing additional blind spots in current methodologies. Our results demonstrate that MixAT's discrete-continuous defense offers a principled and superior robustness-accuracy tradeoff with minimal computational overhead, highlighting its promise for building safer LLMs. We provide our code and models at https://github.com/insait-institute/MixAT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MIXAT, a novel adversarial training method for Large Language Models (LLMs). MIXAT combines continuous and discrete adversarial attacks during training to improve model robustness against a wider range of attacks.  The core idea is to perturb discrete adversarial prompts (like paraphrased harmful requests) using continuous adversarial perturbations in the embedding space. This aims to bridge the gap between computationally expensive discrete adversarial training and faster but less robust continuous training methods. The paper evaluates MIXAT extensively against state-of-the-art attacks and other adversarial training baselines, introducing the "At Least One Attack Success Rate" (ALO-ASR) metric to capture worst-case vulnerability.  The results suggest that MIXAT achieves a better robustness-utility trade-off compared to existing methods with minimal computational overhead and a better ability to generalize. The paper further investigates the effects of chat templates, quantization, low-rank adapters, and temperature scaling on adversarial training and evaluation, highlighting potential blind spots in current methodologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining discrete and continuous adversarial training is novel and provides a principled way to improve adversarial robustness.  While both discrete and continuous adversarial training exist, the synergistic combination and its exploration within the LLM context represent a significant contribution. The MIXAT approach seems like an intuitive extension that balances efficiency and effectiveness.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in LLM safety: vulnerability to adversarial attacks. LLMs are increasingly used in real-world applications, making robustness a crucial requirement. The superior performance of MIXAT over existing defenses, as demonstrated by the comprehensive evaluation and the ALO-ASR metric, is significant. The exploration of practical deployment considerations like chat templates and quantization adds further value. This could pave the way to more secure and robust deployed models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a rigorous evaluation of MIXAT against a wide range of attacks and baselines. The introduction of the ALO-ASR metric is a good addition as a worst-case indicator. The inclusion of realistic deployment settings in the experiments is commendable.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The figures and tables are clear and informative.
    *   **Practicality:** The method has a reasonable computational overhead.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **GCG Vulnerability:** Despite its strong overall performance, MIXAT still exhibits some vulnerability to GCG attacks.  Further improvements in this area are needed. Although including GCG samples in training offers marginally better robustness, its computational cost is excessively high.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation focuses mainly on Zephyr-7B, with some experiments on Llama3 and Qwen. While demonstrating scalability is important, more diverse architectural evaluations would have strengthened the findings.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** Although the empirical results are promising, a stronger theoretical justification for why this specific combination of discrete and continuous attacks works so well could improve the paper. More analysis about the distribution of harmful prompt embeddings is needed.
    *   **Incomplete Reproduction:** The paper fails to reproduce the robustness level of the Llama3 LAT model. Although this doesn't invalidate the claims of the paper, it does give raise to doubts about the quality of the experimental results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more effective and practical approach to adversarial training for LLMs.  The ALO-ASR metric could become a standard for evaluating robustness. The practical insights into deployment considerations are also valuable for practitioners.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper introduces a novel and practically relevant method, MIXAT, for enhancing the adversarial robustness of LLMs. The empirical evidence, combined with the analysis of deployment settings and practical trade-offs, significantly improves upon the existing state of the art. The identified vulnerabilities and improvements, especially to the evaluation procedures, are important to address for building safer LLMs. However, there remain concerns regarding the still non-negligible vulnerability to GCG attacks, the limited variety of models, and the theoretical foundation of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bottlenecked Transformers: Periodic KV Cache Abstraction for Generalised Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16950v1)
- **Authors**: Adnan Oomerjee, Zafeirios Fountas, Zhongwei Yu, Haitham Bou-Ammar, Jun Wang
- **Abstract**: Despite their impressive capabilities, Large Language Models struggle with generalisation beyond their training distribution, often exhibiting sophisticated pattern interpolation rather than true abstract reasoning (extrapolation). In this work, we approach this limitation through the lens of Information Bottleneck (IB) theory, which posits that model generalisation emerges from an optimal balance between input compression and retention of predictive information in latent representations. We prove using IB theory that decoder-only Transformers are inherently constrained in their ability to form task-optimal sequence representations. We then use this result to demonstrate that periodic global transformation of the internal sequence-level representations (KV cache) is a necessary computational step for improving Transformer generalisation in reasoning tasks. Based on these theoretical insights, we propose a modification to the Transformer architecture, in the form of an additional module that globally rewrites the KV cache at periodic intervals, shifting its capacity away from memorising input prefixes and toward encoding features most useful for predicting future tokens. Our model delivers substantial gains on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, outperforming both vanilla Transformers with up to 3.5x more parameters, as well as heuristic-driven pruning mechanisms for cache compression. Our approach can be seen as a principled generalisation of existing KV-cache compression methods; whereas such methods focus solely on compressing input representations, they often do so at the expense of retaining predictive information, and thus their capabilities are inherently bounded by those of an unconstrained model. This establishes a principled framework to manipulate Transformer memory using information theory, addressing fundamental reasoning limitations that scaling alone cannot overcome.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the generalization limitations of large language models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks. It leverages Information Bottleneck (IB) theory to argue that decoder-only Transformers are inherently limited in their ability to form task-optimal sequence representations. The authors propose a Bottlenecked Transformer architecture, which includes a periodic Cache Processor that globally rewrites the KV cache. This modification aims to shift the model's capacity away from memorizing input prefixes and towards encoding features useful for predicting future tokens. The paper presents theoretical justification, architectural modifications, and empirical results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating substantial gains over vanilla Transformers and heuristic-driven cache compression methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of periodically rewriting the KV cache via a separate module, motivated by IB theory to improve generalization, represents a notable contribution.  The theoretical grounding linking decoder-only Transformers' limitations to the standard training objective's pressure to retain input information is also novel.  While KV cache compression methods exist, this paper distinguishes itself by providing a principled, information-theoretic rationale and a dedicated module for filtering predictive information. This contrasts with purely heuristic pruning methods. The idea of using a second Transformer to rewrite the cache is interesting as it enables a non-linear transformation.

*   **Significance:** If the results generalize to more complex and real-world reasoning tasks, this work could be very significant. The paper establishes a concrete connection between the limitations of scaling LLMs and the need for more targeted information processing.  It offers a practical architectural solution to address a fundamental problem (generalization) in LLMs. The performance improvements on mathematical reasoning tasks are promising, outperforming vanilla Transformers and other cache compression techniques. It has the potential to guide future research toward more effective ways to manipulate Transformer memory, moving beyond simply increasing model size.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a strong theoretical foundation based on Information Bottleneck theory, justifying the architectural modification.
    *   **Principled Approach:** Unlike heuristic methods, the proposed Bottlenecked Transformer is motivated by information-theoretic principles.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements in mathematical reasoning tasks, including out-of-distribution scenarios.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental setup.
    *   **Architecture design is clear** It is simple to understand how the modules are connected and how the rewriting of the cache occurs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Domain:** The experiments are limited to mathematical reasoning benchmarks. While these are relevant, they represent a specific type of reasoning and may not fully capture the complexities of general reasoning.
    *   **Small Transformer Size:** The backbone Transformer size (4M parameters) is relatively small compared to state-of-the-art LLMs. It's unclear how well this approach would scale to much larger models.
    *   **Architecture is specific to decoder-only models** It is not clear how this method could be adapted to encoder-decoder architectures.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The paper lacks thorough ablation studies, particularly concerning different architectural choices for the Cache Processor (e.g., different numbers of layers, types of attention, different cache refresh rates).
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the modified architecture improves generalization, the additional Cache Processor adds computational overhead. The trade-off between performance and computational cost needs further analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could significantly influence research on improving the reasoning abilities of LLMs. The IB perspective provides a valuable framework for understanding generalization limitations. The proposed architectural solution provides a potential path forward, encouraging future work on more sophisticated methods for manipulating Transformer memory.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a novel contribution by grounding a practical architectural modification in Information Bottleneck theory to tackle the long-standing generalization issue of LLMs. It is well-justified with a combination of theoretical analysis and empirical validation. While the scope of the experiments is limited to mathematical reasoning and relatively small models, the approach has the potential to influence research towards principled architectural designs for improving reasoning capabilities in LLMs. Furthermore, the architecture is clean and it is clear to see the intuition and the functionality of the method.

### Invisible Prompts, Visible Threats: Malicious Font Injection in External Resources for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16957v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Xiong, Changjia Zhu, Shuhang Lin, Chong Zhang, Yongfeng Zhang, Yao Liu, Lingyao Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly equipped with capabilities of real-time web search and integrated with protocols like Model Context Protocol (MCP). This extension could introduce new security vulnerabilities. We present a systematic investigation of LLM vulnerabilities to hidden adversarial prompts through malicious font injection in external resources like webpages, where attackers manipulate code-to-glyph mapping to inject deceptive content which are invisible to users. We evaluate two critical attack scenarios: (1) "malicious content relay" and (2) "sensitive data leakage" through MCP-enabled tools. Our experiments reveal that indirect prompts with injected malicious font can bypass LLM safety mechanisms through external resources, achieving varying success rates based on data sensitivity and prompt design. Our research underscores the urgent need for enhanced security measures in LLM deployments when processing external content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Invisible Prompts, Visible Threats: Malicious Font Injection in External Resources for Large Language Models" investigates a novel attack vector against Large Language Models (LLMs).  The attack leverages malicious font injection in external resources (webpages, documents) to introduce hidden adversarial prompts, invisible to human observers but interpretable by LLMs.  The authors evaluate two scenarios: (1) malicious content relay, where the LLM unwittingly disseminates harmful content, and (2) sensitive data leakage, where the LLM, equipped with Model Context Protocol (MCP), exfiltrates user data to an attacker. The experiments demonstrate the feasibility of this attack, highlight factors influencing its success (prompt design, document format, injection frequency, placement), and reveal vulnerabilities in current LLM security measures. The study also highlights the impact of using MCP with LLMs, pointing out the increased risks when interacting with real-world data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using font injection to create invisible adversarial prompts is relatively novel. While prior work explored indirect prompt injection, using fonts as a mechanism for concealing and injecting the prompts offers a unique and potentially more stealthy approach. It leverages the often-overlooked aspect of how LLMs process visual data from external sources, particularly fonts.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   It exposes a new attack surface for LLMs, particularly those integrated with web browsing and MCP-like functionalities.
    *   It demonstrates how existing safety mechanisms can be bypassed through visual deception, which current LLMs are not designed to detect.
    *   The results are relevant to the growing use of LLMs in real-world applications where they interact with untrusted external data sources.
    *   It underscores the urgency of developing more robust security measures for LLMs that account for the semantic content of the external resources, not only to detect visual deceptive content but also to improve content integrity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the attack mechanism.
    *   The experimental design is comprehensive, exploring various factors influencing attack success and the comparison across LLM models.
    *   The two scenarios (content relay and data leakage) are well-motivated and demonstrate different aspects of the threat.
    *   The analysis is thorough, providing insights into the interplay between prompt design, document format, data sensitivity, and LLM behavior.
    *   The paper discusses limitations and ethical considerations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study primarily focuses on English language content, and it is unclear how the attack would perform with other languages.
    *   While the paper tested multiple LLMs, the conclusions might not generalize to future versions of these models or entirely different architectures.
    *   The evaluation of "stealthiness" is limited. While the prompts are visually hidden, the paper doesn't deeply explore whether the attack leaves other detectable traces (e.g., in network traffic or system logs).
    *   The countermeasures were not explored or discussed.
    *   The evaluation is done using synthetic data, which means that the results and sensitivity might not be easily transferred to real-world scenarios.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM security, particularly on adversarial attacks and data privacy. It highlights a need for new security frameworks that address content semantics and visual representation integrity, that may significantly increase the security of LLMs that operate in real-world scenarios.

*   **Score:** 7.  The paper presents a novel attack vector with significant implications for LLM security.  The experimental evaluation is thorough, and the findings are relevant to the growing use of LLMs in real-world applications. However, the limitations regarding language, model generalization, detection, real-world applicability, and lack of explored countermeasures slightly temper the overall impact. The paper provides a good starting point for exploring the attack, and raises important questions about LLM security.
