### Bigger Isn't Always Memorizing: Early Stopping Overparameterized Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16959v1)
- **Authors**: Alessandro Favero, Antonio Sclocchi, Matthieu Wyart
- **Abstract**: Diffusion probabilistic models have become a cornerstone of modern generative AI, yet the mechanisms underlying their generalization remain poorly understood. In fact, if these models were perfectly minimizing their training loss, they would just generate data belonging to their training set, i.e., memorize, as empirically found in the overparameterized regime. We revisit this view by showing that, in highly overparameterized diffusion models, generalization in natural data domains is progressively achieved during training before the onset of memorization. Our results, ranging from image to language diffusion models, systematically support the empirical law that memorization time is proportional to the dataset size. Generalization vs. memorization is then best understood as a competition between time scales. We show that this phenomenology is recovered in diffusion models learning a simple probabilistic context-free grammar with random rules, where generalization corresponds to the hierarchical acquisition of deeper grammar rules as training time grows, and the generalization cost of early stopping can be characterized. We summarize these results in a phase diagram. Overall, our results support that a principled early-stopping criterion - scaling with dataset size - can effectively optimize generalization while avoiding memorization, with direct implications for hyperparameter transfer and privacy-sensitive applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Bigger Isn't Always Memorizing: Early Stopping Overparameterized Diffusion Models" challenges the traditional view that generalization in generative models, particularly diffusion models, is solely dependent on underparameterization.  The authors demonstrate empirically that even highly overparameterized diffusion models exhibit a phase of generalization *before* memorization occurs during training on natural data. They observe that the onset of memorization scales linearly with the size of the training dataset. They further investigate this phenomenon using a discrete diffusion model trained on a simple probabilistic context-free grammar (PCFG), showing that the model learns hierarchical grammar rules progressively with training.  The paper proposes that early stopping, informed by dataset size, can effectively optimize generalization and avoid memorization, with implications for hyperparameter transfer and privacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central claim – that overparameterized diffusion models generalize *before* memorizing – is a significant and novel finding.  Previous work has largely focused on model capacity as the primary determinant of generalization, with memorization seen as a consequence of excessive capacity.  Demonstrating that generalization is a dynamic process occurring *prior* to memorization in these models is a valuable contribution. The connection to early stopping is well-motivated and leads to immediate practical impact. The analysis of generalization via hierarchical grammar learning in PCFGs, though simplified, provides useful insights and strengthens the argument.

*   **Significance:** The findings have substantial implications for training and deploying diffusion models, particularly in contexts where privacy and copyright are concerns. By showing that early stopping (informed by data set size) can optimize generalization, the paper offers a practical strategy for mitigating memorization. The implications for hyperparameter transfer are also important, as they suggest that optimal training strategies can be adapted to different dataset sizes. The link between data size and memorization can also lead to a better understanding of the theoretical properties of diffusion models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper presents convincing empirical results across diverse datasets (images and text) and diffusion model architectures (iDDPM, Stable Diffusion, MD4, D3PM), strengthening the generality of the claim.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The PCFG experiments, while simplified, provide a theoretical grounding for the observed phenomena, linking generalization to the hierarchical learning of grammar rules.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers a concrete and actionable strategy (early stopping) for improving generalization and mitigating memorization in diffusion models.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it accessible to a broad audience. The figures are informative and effectively communicate the key findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **PCFG Simplification:** The PCFG experiments, while insightful, are based on a highly simplified grammar. It would be valuable to see if the same principles hold for more complex and realistic grammars, more closely resembling natural language.
    *   **Linear Scaling Assumption:** The linear scaling relationship between memorization time and dataset size is largely empirically driven. A deeper theoretical understanding of why this occurs would further strengthen the work. It would also be interesting to investigate the theoretical underpinnings of the "early stopping scaling law".
    *   **Limited exploration of alternative memorization metrics**. While the replication detection is a sound approach, the field continues to develop novel ways to assess the memorization in generative models, and exploring the robustness of the findings across various metrics would be interesting.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research on diffusion models in several ways:
    *   Shift the focus from model capacity to training dynamics as a key factor in generalization.
    *   Encourage the development of early stopping strategies tailored to dataset size.
    *   Spur further theoretical investigation of the generalization-memorization trade-off in overparameterized models.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:** The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the understanding of generalization in diffusion models. The empirical evidence is compelling, the theoretical justification is insightful, and the practical implications are valuable. While some aspects of the analysis could be further developed (e.g., more complex PCFGs, a deeper theoretical explanation of the scaling relationship), the paper represents a substantial advance in the field and is likely to have a lasting impact. The paper is a strong contribution with potential for practical use and immediate theoretical impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SWE-Dev: Evaluating and Training Autonomous Feature-Driven Software Development
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16975v1)
- **Authors**: Yaxin Du, Yuzhu Cai, Yifan Zhou, Cheng Wang, Yu Qian, Xianghe Pang, Qian Liu, Yue Hu, Siheng Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capability in diverse software engineering tasks, e.g. code completion, bug fixing, and document generation. However, feature-driven development (FDD), a highly prevalent real-world task that involves developing new functionalities for large, existing codebases, remains underexplored. We therefore introduce SWE-Dev, the first large-scale dataset (with 14,000 training and 500 test samples) designed to evaluate and train autonomous coding systems on real-world feature development tasks. To ensure verifiable and diverse training, SWE-Dev uniquely provides all instances with a runnable environment and its developer-authored executable unit tests. This collection not only provides high-quality data for Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), but also enables Reinforcement Learning (RL) by delivering accurate reward signals from executable unit tests. Our extensive evaluations on SWE-Dev, covering 17 chatbot LLMs, 10 reasoning models, and 10 Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), reveal that FDD is a profoundly challenging frontier for current AI (e.g., Claude-3.7-Sonnet achieves only 22.45\% Pass@3 on the hard test split). Crucially, we demonstrate that SWE-Dev serves as an effective platform for model improvement: fine-tuning on training set enabled a 7B model comparable to GPT-4o on \textit{hard} split, underscoring the value of its high-quality training data. Code is available here \href{https://github.com/justLittleWhite/SWE-Dev}{https://github.com/justLittleWhite/SWE-Dev}.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the SWE-Dev paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SWE-Dev, a new large-scale dataset for evaluating and training autonomous coding systems on real-world feature-driven development (FDD) tasks.  Unlike existing benchmarks, SWE-Dev provides a runnable environment and executable unit tests for every instance, enabling verifiable training via SFT, RL, and MAS. The dataset comprises 14,000 training and 500 test samples derived from open-source projects. Extensive evaluations are presented, benchmarking chatbot LLMs, reasoning models, and multi-agent systems, highlighting the challenges of FDD and demonstrating the dataset's utility for model improvement through fine-tuning.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a gap in the evaluation and training of coding AI on realistic, end-to-end software development tasks.  SWE-Dev's novelty lies in:

*   **Focus on Feature-Driven Development (FDD):** Shifting the focus from isolated code snippets and bug fixes to the more prevalent and complex task of integrating new functionalities into existing codebases.
*   **Realistic Scale and Complexity:** Providing substantial code modification requirements and cross-file dependencies, reflecting real-world development scenarios.
*   **Robust and Grounded Evaluation:** Using executable test cases from real open-source repositories ensures the functional correctness and verifiability of generated code, unlike similarity metrics or static heuristics.
*   **Executable Training Set:** Providing runnable environments and executable unit tests for both training and testing is a unique and crucial feature, enabling more reliable supervision and reinforcement learning.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The thorough evaluation of diverse LLMs and multi-agent systems highlights the limitations of current AI in handling complex FDD tasks and identifies promising directions for future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined and Motivated Problem:**  Clearly identifies the limitations of current coding benchmarks and justifies the need for a FDD-focused dataset.
*   **Rigorous Data Collection and Construction:**  Describes a meticulous process for creating SWE-Dev, ensuring quality, verifiability, and control over task difficulty.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Evaluates a wide range of state-of-the-art models and training paradigms, providing valuable insights into their capabilities and limitations.
*   **Demonstrated Utility:**  Shows SWE-Dev's effectiveness in improving model performance through fine-tuning, validating its value as a training platform.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Language Scope:** The dataset is currently focused on Python, limiting its generality to other programming languages.
*   **Basic Training Strategies:**  While demonstrating the utility of SFT and RL, the paper could explore more advanced training techniques, such as curriculum learning or dynamic agent coordination.
*   **Complexity Control:** While call trees are used, it's unclear how granular the complexity control is. More detailed analysis on the relationship between call tree characteristics and model performance would be useful.
*   **The impact and influence of different components in PRD needs to be explored**

**Potential Influence:**

SWE-Dev has the potential to significantly influence the field of AI-driven software development by:

*   **Driving Innovation:** Encouraging researchers to develop models and algorithms specifically designed for FDD tasks.
*   **Facilitating Benchmarking:** Providing a standardized and rigorous benchmark for evaluating the progress of coding AI systems.
*   **Enabling Training:** Serving as a valuable resource for training models capable of handling complex, real-world software development scenarios.
*   **Spurring New Research Directions:**  Inspiring new research into long-context reasoning, agent orchestration, and execution-aware training.

The work is a significant and timely contribution, opening up new avenues for research and development in autonomous coding systems. While there are some areas for future improvement, the rigor, scale, and real-world relevance of SWE-Dev make it a valuable resource for the community.

### Creatively Upscaling Images with Global-Regional Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16976v1)
- **Authors**: Yurui Qian, Qi Cai, Yingwei Pan, Ting Yao, Tao Mei
- **Abstract**: Contemporary diffusion models show remarkable capability in text-to-image generation, while still being limited to restricted resolutions (e.g., 1,024 X 1,024). Recent advances enable tuning-free higher-resolution image generation by recycling pre-trained diffusion models and extending them via regional denoising or dilated sampling/convolutions. However, these models struggle to simultaneously preserve global semantic structure and produce creative regional details in higher-resolution images. To address this, we present C-Upscale, a new recipe of tuning-free image upscaling that pivots on global-regional priors derived from given global prompt and estimated regional prompts via Multimodal LLM. Technically, the low-frequency component of low-resolution image is recognized as global structure prior to encourage global semantic consistency in high-resolution generation. Next, we perform regional attention control to screen cross-attention between global prompt and each region during regional denoising, leading to regional attention prior that alleviates object repetition issue. The estimated regional prompts containing rich descriptive details further act as regional semantic prior to fuel the creativity of regional detail generation. Both quantitative and qualitative evaluations demonstrate that our C-Upscale manages to generate ultra-high-resolution images (e.g., 4,096 X 4,096 and 8,192 X 8,192) with higher visual fidelity and more creative regional details.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces C-Upscale, a tuning-free method for generating high-resolution images from either low-resolution images or text prompts.  C-Upscale leverages three key priors: a *global structure prior* (GSP) derived from the low-frequency component of the low-resolution image to maintain overall scene consistency, a *regional attention prior* (RAP) to filter out irrelevant cross-attention between the global prompt and each region, mitigating object repetition, and a *regional semantic prior* (RSP) generated by a Multimodal LLM to inject creative and detailed regional content. The method divides the image into overlapping regions, denoises each region individually using the priors, and then reassembles them to create the final high-resolution image. Extensive experiments demonstrate that C-Upscale generates ultra-high-resolution images with improved visual quality, better semantic alignment, and more creative regional details compared to existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines existing concepts (diffusion models, regional denoising, multimodal LLMs) in a novel way to address the specific problem of high-resolution image generation that balances global coherence with regional creativity. The explicit extraction and use of global and regional priors is a strong point. The integration of a multimodal LLM to generate regional semantic prompts is innovative and significantly contributes to the creative aspect of the upscaling.
*   **Significance:** The ability to generate high-resolution images with semantic coherence and detailed regional content has significant practical applications in fields like advertising, film production, and art.  Tuning-free methods are valuable because they offer flexibility and accessibility without requiring extensive retraining of models.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation with quantitative metrics (MUSIQ, ClipIQA, ManIQA, FID, LPIPS, CLIP score) and qualitative comparisons. It benchmarks C-Upscale against a wide range of existing methods, including super-resolution techniques and other tuning-free diffusion-based approaches.  The human preference study provides valuable insights into the subjective quality of the generated images.
*   **Generalization ability**: The approach is tested across multiple datasets, different prompts, and different network architectures, highlighting its potential.
*   **Efficiency:** The paper addresses the computational cost and provides details on its superior efficiency compared to some existing methods, like DemoFusion. It also explores acceleration techniques using distilled models and optimized implementations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:**  The system involves multiple components (diffusion model, VAE, multimodal LLM, wavelet transform, attention mechanisms), which adds to the overall complexity. While it's tuning-free, implementing it may still require significant engineering effort.
*   **Reliance on MLLM quality:** The quality of the regional semantic priors depends heavily on the performance of the Multimodal LLM. While LLaVA-1.6 is used, there may be cases where the generated prompts are not accurate or relevant, which could negatively affect the final image quality.
*   **Limited novelty in some aspects**: The high-level idea of combining global and local information is not entirely new in image processing. The paper's contribution lies in the specific way this is achieved with diffusion models and multimodal LLMs.
*   **Overclaims:** The term "Creative Upscaling" might be considered an overclaim. While the regional semantic prior adds details, the level of creativity is still constrained by the pre-trained diffusion model and the MLLM. The images are more detailed and plausible, but not necessarily exhibiting truly "creative" outputs in the sense of unexpected or artistic deviation from the prompt.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of high-resolution image generation. The key innovation lies in the combination of global and regional priors with a diffusion model and a multimodal LLM to achieve a compelling balance of semantic consistency and regional detail. While there are some weaknesses, the thorough evaluation and the demonstration of practical benefits justify a high score. The work builds upon existing methods but significantly improves the quality and flexibility of high-resolution image generation. However, there are existing diffusion models that address some of the limitations, the complexity of the pipeline warrants a reduction in score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV

### Incorporating Visual Correspondence into Diffusion Model for Virtual Try-On
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16977v1)
- **Authors**: Siqi Wan, Jingwen Chen, Yingwei Pan, Ting Yao, Tao Mei
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have shown preliminary success in virtual try-on (VTON) task. The typical dual-branch architecture comprises two UNets for implicit garment deformation and synthesized image generation respectively, and has emerged as the recipe for VTON task. Nevertheless, the problem remains challenging to preserve the shape and every detail of the given garment due to the intrinsic stochasticity of diffusion model. To alleviate this issue, we novelly propose to explicitly capitalize on visual correspondence as the prior to tame diffusion process instead of simply feeding the whole garment into UNet as the appearance reference. Specifically, we interpret the fine-grained appearance and texture details as a set of structured semantic points, and match the semantic points rooted in garment to the ones over target person through local flow warping. Such 2D points are then augmented into 3D-aware cues with depth/normal map of target person. The correspondence mimics the way of putting clothing on human body and the 3D-aware cues act as semantic point matching to supervise diffusion model training. A point-focused diffusion loss is further devised to fully take the advantage of semantic point matching. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong garment detail preservation of our approach, evidenced by state-of-the-art VTON performances on both VITON-HD and DressCode datasets. Code is publicly available at: https://github.com/HiDream-ai/SPM-Diff.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new diffusion model, SPM-Diff, for virtual try-on (VTON).  Unlike existing approaches that rely on implicitly warping garments or encoding them holistically, SPM-Diff explicitly incorporates visual correspondence between the in-shop garment and the target person.  It does this by sampling semantic points on the garment, matching them to corresponding points on the target person using local flow warping, and augmenting these 2D points with 3D-aware cues (depth/normal maps).  A dual-branch diffusion architecture is used, with the explicit correspondence serving as a prior to guide the Main-UNet for better detail preservation.  A point-focused diffusion loss is introduced to emphasize the reconstruction of semantic points.  Experiments on VITON-HD and DressCode datasets demonstrate that SPM-Diff achieves state-of-the-art performance, particularly in preserving garment details and shape.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit use of visual correspondence between the garment and the target person in a diffusion-based VTON model. Prior works often rely on implicit warping or encoding the entire garment image as a condition.  The idea of using semantic points as an explicit prior is a significant departure and addresses a key limitation of existing methods, which struggle to preserve garment details.  While Reference-Net approaches are mentioned, the explicit point-matching mechanism with 3D-aware cues is what distinguishes this paper. This idea alone provides added innovation compared to existing works.

*   **Significance:**  VTON is a commercially relevant task, and improving the realism and detail preservation of generated images is crucial.  The SPM-Diff model addresses a significant challenge in the field – the difficulty in maintaining garment detail and shape when using diffusion models, due to their inherent stochasticity. The superior results on standard benchmarks suggest that this approach has the potential to advance the state-of-the-art in VTON and provide a more realistic and satisfying virtual try-on experience. Another important point is how they compare with traditional methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The explicit visual correspondence mechanism is a novel and effective way to guide the diffusion process.
    *   The augmentation of 2D points with 3D-aware cues is a clever technique to improve the accuracy of point matching and preserve garment shape.
    *   The point-focused diffusion loss further reinforces the importance of semantic point reconstruction.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The approach relies on a pre-trained warping module and 3D human reconstruction model, which could introduce errors or limitations.  The accuracy of these pre-trained models directly affects the performance of SPM-Diff. However, the ablation on 3D reconstruction models reveals it is robust to different models used for 3D modelling.
    *   The paper mentions limitations regarding handling decorative items and overlapping hands, which indicates areas for future improvement.
    *   A deeper analysis of the trade-offs between computational cost and performance would be valuable. The section on performance and effect of various parameters are limited and should be improved.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the VTON field, as it introduces a new and effective approach to preserve garment details and shape.  The idea of using explicit visual correspondence as a prior could be adopted by other researchers working on diffusion-based image synthesis tasks. It opens the door to future work exploring different types of correspondence or improving the accuracy of point matching.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, the novelty of the explicit visual correspondence mechanism, the effectiveness of the proposed techniques, and the state-of-the-art results justify a high score. The method introduces a tangible benefit over implicit methods for VTON.

### HyGenar: An LLM-Driven Hybrid Genetic Algorithm for Few-Shot Grammar Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16978v1)
- **Authors**: Weizhi Tang, Yixuan Li, Chris Sypherd, Elizabeth Polgreen, Vaishak Belle
- **Abstract**: Grammar plays a critical role in natural language processing and text/code generation by enabling the definition of syntax, the creation of parsers, and guiding structured outputs. Although large language models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive capabilities across domains, their ability to infer and generate grammars has not yet been thoroughly explored. In this paper, we aim to study and improve the ability of LLMs for few-shot grammar generation, where grammars are inferred from sets of a small number of positive and negative examples and generated in Backus-Naur Form. To explore this, we introduced a novel dataset comprising 540 structured grammar generation challenges, devised 6 metrics, and evaluated 8 various LLMs against it. Our findings reveal that existing LLMs perform sub-optimally in grammar generation. To address this, we propose an LLM-driven hybrid genetic algorithm, namely HyGenar, to optimize grammar generation. HyGenar achieves substantial improvements in both the syntactic and semantic correctness of generated grammars across LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HyGenar: An LLM-Driven Hybrid Genetic Algorithm for Few-Shot Grammar Generation" addresses the problem of generating Backus-Naur Form (BNF) grammars from a small set of positive and negative examples using Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors observe that existing LLMs perform sub-optimally in this task. To improve grammar generation, they propose HyGenar, a hybrid genetic algorithm that leverages LLMs for population initialization and mutation within a genetic algorithm framework. The paper introduces a novel dataset of 540 grammar generation challenges, along with six metrics to evaluate the syntactic and semantic correctness, over-fitting, over-generalization, and utility of the generated grammars. The results demonstrate that HyGenar significantly enhances the grammar generation ability of various LLMs. The paper also incorporates two baselines, Direct Prompting (DP) and Optimized BNF Parser for LLM-Friendly Feedback (OPF).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of a hybrid genetic algorithm, driven by LLMs, to the specific problem of few-shot grammar generation. While genetic algorithms and LLMs have been used separately in various contexts, the combination is relatively novel, particularly within the grammar inference domain. The LLM-driven population initialization and mutation are interesting aspects. It extends prior work in code generation by adapting techniques like Reflexion and Self-Refine. The development of a dedicated dataset with specific metrics for the task is another strong contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to automate grammar inference, which has applications in natural language processing, software engineering, and code generation. Improving LLMs' ability to generate grammars from limited examples is a valuable step towards more automated and robust language processing systems. The performance improvements demonstrated by HyGenar, especially on challenging datasets with few examples, suggests a practical advancement. The detailed analysis of various LLMs and the proposed metrics contribute to a better understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in grammar generation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The HyGenar algorithm presents a well-defined method for improving LLM-based grammar generation.
    *   The dataset fills a gap in resources for evaluating grammar generation abilities, including a range of grammars and the corresponding challenges.
    *   The defined metrics enable a more comprehensive and nuanced evaluation than traditional metrics, by considering over-fitting and over-generalization.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, encompassing multiple LLMs and a comparison against relevant baselines.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components of the HyGenar algorithm.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the approach demonstrates improvement, the performance, especially for semantic correctness, is still not entirely satisfactory, indicating room for further research.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for generating the dataset introduces a potential bias. While the paper mentions manual correction, the initial bias could still influence the results.
    *   The algorithm may be computationally expensive, especially due to the inherent cost of LLM calls within the genetic algorithm loop.  The paper doesn't discuss the computational cost.
    *   The generality of the approach to different types of grammars and languages beyond the evaluated dataset is not thoroughly explored.
    *   The paper relies on prompting and may be vulnerable to prompt engineering or unintended behaviors from the LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It encourages the development of more sophisticated algorithms for grammar inference using LLMs.
    *   The dataset and metrics can serve as a benchmark for evaluating new grammar generation methods.
    *   The insights into LLMs' strengths and weaknesses in grammar generation can guide the design of more effective LLM-based tools.
    *   The hybrid approach can be applied to other language-related tasks, combining the strengths of symbolic methods with LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty of the hybrid approach, the practical significance of automated grammar inference, the thoroughness of the evaluation, and the limitations mentioned above, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper presents a solid contribution with clear potential for future research. However, there's still room for significant improvement in the absolute performance and robustness of the system, addressing the dataset bias, and further exploring the computational aspects of the approach.

### Know the Ropes: A Heuristic Strategy for LLM-based Multi-Agent System Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16979v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenkun Li, Lingyao Li, Shuhang Lin, Yongfeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Single-agent LLMs hit hard limits--finite context, role overload, and brittle domain transfer. Conventional multi-agent fixes soften those edges yet expose fresh pains: ill-posed decompositions, fuzzy contracts, and verification overhead that blunts the gains. We therefore present Know-The-Ropes (KtR), a framework that converts domain priors into an algorithmic blueprint hierarchy, in which tasks are recursively split into typed, controller-mediated subtasks, each solved zero-shot or with the lightest viable boost (e.g., chain-of-thought, micro-tune, self-check). Grounded in the No-Free-Lunch theorem, KtR trades the chase for a universal prompt for disciplined decomposition. On the Knapsack problem (3-8 items), three GPT-4o-mini agents raise accuracy from 3% zero-shot to 95% on size-5 instances after patching a single bottleneck agent. On the tougher Task-Assignment problem (6-15 jobs), a six-agent o3-mini blueprint hits 100% up to size 10 and 84% on sizes 13-15, versus 11% zero-shot. Algorithm-aware decomposition plus targeted augmentation thus turns modest models into reliable collaborators--no ever-larger monoliths required.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Know the Ropes" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Know-The-Ropes (KtR), a framework for designing multi-agent systems (MAS) based on LLMs.  KtR leverages domain priors to create algorithmic blueprint hierarchies.  It recursively splits complex tasks into typed, controller-mediated sub-tasks that are either solved zero-shot or with minimal augmentation (CoT, micro-tuning). The core idea is to replace generic prompts with a structured decomposition process tailored to the problem. The framework uses classical algorithms to promote understandability and optimization. Experiments on Knapsack and Task-Assignment problems show that KtR enables smaller LLMs to achieve high accuracy by optimizing system architecture rather than relying on large language models or extensive prompt engineering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of using algorithm-aware decomposition to build MAS is a significant contribution. While multi-agent systems and domain-specific optimizations exist, the specific combination of a heuristic decomposition approach, emphasis on lightweight augmentation of smaller models, and formalization into a blueprint hierarchy does appear novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM-based MAS: the difficulty in prompting, the fragility of zero/few-shot performance, and the challenges of scaling larger models. KtR proposes a pragmatic approach to building reliable MAS by leveraging domain knowledge and algorithmic structure. The empirical results are impressive, demonstrating substantial gains in accuracy for both Knapsack and Task-Assignment problems, especially by making better use of small language models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper identifies key limitations of existing MAS approaches.
    *   **Principled Design:** KtR offers a structured design methodology based on the No-Free-Lunch theorem.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The two case studies provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. The focus on using smaller models (mini-GPT and 03-mini) makes the results particularly interesting, suggesting a way to create powerful systems without simply relying on the largest possible models.
    *   **Focus on Tractability:** Prioritizes smaller augmentation, with a chain of thought.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The evaluation is restricted to two classic optimization problems, which have well-defined algorithms and clear domain structure.  The generalizability to more open-ended, real-world tasks is an open question.
    *   **Synthetic Data:** The use of synthetic data, while practical for controlled experiments, may not fully capture the complexity and nuances of real-world data. The reliance on noiseless and completely specified data is a limitation.
    *   **Heuristic Bottleneck Detection:** The approach to identifying bottlenecks relying on the cheap availability of ground-truth labels is unrealistic in many real world settings.
    *   **Limited Benchmarking of Multi-Agents:** While performance increases substantially, the evaluation section only compares the KtR MAS to GPT family members, but not other multi-agent system frameworks for similar benchmarks.

*   **Impact:**  KtR could potentially influence the design of LLM-based MAS by shifting the focus from complex prompts and large models to structured decomposition and domain-specific knowledge. The work is likely to encourage further research into algorithm-aware MAS design.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and promising framework, the limitations in task scope and data realism prevent it from receiving a higher score. The concentration on a small number of well-defined optimization problems, while useful for proof-of-concept, raises questions about its broader applicability. A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field but also acknowledges the need for further research to address its limitations.

### Pursuing Temporal-Consistent Video Virtual Try-On via Dynamic Pose Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16980v1)
- **Authors**: Dong Li, Wenqi Zhong, Wei Yu, Yingwei Pan, Dingwen Zhang, Ting Yao, Junwei Han, Tao Mei
- **Abstract**: Video virtual try-on aims to seamlessly dress a subject in a video with a specific garment. The primary challenge involves preserving the visual authenticity of the garment while dynamically adapting to the pose and physique of the subject. While existing methods have predominantly focused on image-based virtual try-on, extending these techniques directly to videos often results in temporal inconsistencies. Most current video virtual try-on approaches alleviate this challenge by incorporating temporal modules, yet still overlook the critical spatiotemporal pose interactions between human and garment. Effective pose interactions in videos should not only consider spatial alignment between human and garment poses in each frame but also account for the temporal dynamics of human poses throughout the entire video. With such motivation, we propose a new framework, namely Dynamic Pose Interaction Diffusion Models (DPIDM), to leverage diffusion models to delve into dynamic pose interactions for video virtual try-on. Technically, DPIDM introduces a skeleton-based pose adapter to integrate synchronized human and garment poses into the denoising network. A hierarchical attention module is then exquisitely designed to model intra-frame human-garment pose interactions and long-term human pose dynamics across frames through pose-aware spatial and temporal attention mechanisms. Moreover, DPIDM capitalizes on a temporal regularized attention loss between consecutive frames to enhance temporal consistency. Extensive experiments conducted on VITON-HD, VVT and ViViD datasets demonstrate the superiority of our DPIDM against the baseline methods. Notably, DPIDM achieves VFID score of 0.506 on VVT dataset, leading to 60.5% improvement over the state-of-the-art GPD-VVTO approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new framework called Dynamic Pose Interaction Diffusion Models (DPIDM) for temporal-consistent video virtual try-on.  DPIDM focuses on modeling the dynamic pose interactions between the human body and the garment in a video. It uses a skeleton-based pose adapter to integrate human and garment poses into a denoising network. The core of DPIDM is a hierarchical attention module comprising pose-aware spatial, temporal-shift, cross, and pose-aware temporal attention blocks, designed to capture intra-frame pose alignment and long-term human pose dynamics. A temporal regularized attention loss is also introduced to improve temporal consistency. The authors demonstrate the superiority of DPIDM over existing methods on VITON-HD, VVT, and ViViD datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the explicit modeling of dynamic pose interactions for video virtual try-on.  While previous works have addressed temporal consistency and pose alignment to some extent, DPIDM uniquely integrates both spatial and temporal pose information directly into the attention mechanisms of a diffusion model. The hierarchical attention module and the temporal regularized attention loss are also novel contributions. The use of a skeleton-based pose adapter, while not entirely new in itself, is effectively integrated into the diffusion process in DPIDM.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in existing video virtual try-on methods: the lack of explicit modeling of pose dynamics and interactions between the human and garment. By addressing this limitation, DPIDM achieves significant improvements in temporal consistency and visual authenticity, as demonstrated by the experimental results. The significant performance boost on VVT, especially the VFID score improvements, is quite compelling. If this holds up under further scrutiny, it's a sign that the approach is not just incrementally better, but genuinely better at capturing the dynamic relationship. The paper offers a well-designed and implementable approach that can potentially influence future research in this area. The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing methods and motivates the need for modeling dynamic pose interactions.
    * **Well-designed framework:** DPIDM is a well-structured and technically sound framework, with a clear explanation of each component.
    * **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on multiple datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of DPIDM.
    * **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies effectively validate the contribution of each component of DPIDM.
    * **Strong empirical performance:**  The results indicate a substantial improvement in VFID score on VVT compared to previous approaches.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on pose estimation:** The framework relies on accurate pose estimation, and performance may degrade with inaccurate pose information, although the authors address this with a condition dropping strategy.
    * **Computational complexity:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive, and DPIDM's explicit modeling of pose interactions may further increase the computational burden. While they mention reducing GPU memory, concrete details on inference time and resource use are needed.
    * **Limited discussion on failure cases:**  The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of failure cases and limitations of DPIDM.  For instance, how does it handle extreme pose variations or occlusions?  What are the limitations of the temporal consistency achieved?
    * **Incremental, not Revolutionary:** While technically sound, the approach builds upon existing diffusion models and attention mechanisms. It's an important refinement, but not a completely groundbreaking paradigm shift.

* **Potential Influence:** DPIDM has the potential to influence future research in video virtual try-on by highlighting the importance of modeling dynamic pose interactions. The hierarchical attention module and the temporal regularized attention loss can serve as valuable building blocks for future frameworks. However, more practical improvements are needed to make this a tool that can be applied to many domains.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a technically sound and empirically strong contribution to the field of video virtual try-on. The novelty lies in the specific way dynamic pose interactions are modeled within a diffusion framework. The significant performance improvements, particularly on VVT, demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. However, the paper is primarily an incremental improvement upon existing diffusion-based methods. The potential for wider adoption is somewhat hampered by reliance on pose estimation and potential computational overhead. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the significant but incremental nature of the contribution, the strong empirical results, and the potential for future research based on the ideas presented.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Correlation: Towards Causal Large Language Model Agents in Biomedicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16982v1)
- **Authors**: Adib Bazgir, Amir Habibdoust Lafmajani, Yuwen Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) show promise in biomedicine but lack true causal understanding, relying instead on correlations. This paper envisions causal LLM agents that integrate multimodal data (text, images, genomics, etc.) and perform intervention-based reasoning to infer cause-and-effect. Addressing this requires overcoming key challenges: designing safe, controllable agentic frameworks; developing rigorous benchmarks for causal evaluation; integrating heterogeneous data sources; and synergistically combining LLMs with structured knowledge (KGs) and formal causal inference tools. Such agents could unlock transformative opportunities, including accelerating drug discovery through automated hypothesis generation and simulation, enabling personalized medicine through patient-specific causal models. This research agenda aims to foster interdisciplinary efforts, bridging causal concepts and foundation models to develop reliable AI partners for biomedical progress.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a vision for "causal LLM agents" in biomedicine. It argues that current LLMs are limited by their reliance on correlations and lack of true causal understanding. The authors envision agents that integrate multimodal biomedical data (text, images, genomics), perform intervention-based reasoning, and utilize formal causal inference methods to infer cause-and-effect relationships. The paper outlines key challenges in building such agents, including designing safe and controllable frameworks, developing benchmarks for causal evaluation, integrating heterogeneous data sources, and synergistically combining LLMs with knowledge graphs (KGs) and causal inference tools. The authors highlight potential applications in drug discovery, personalized medicine, and public health.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of moving beyond correlation to causal reasoning in LLMs is not entirely novel. Several recent papers have explored similar themes, as cited by the authors. However, the paper makes a valuable contribution by specifically focusing on the biomedical domain and providing a comprehensive vision for how causal LLM agents could transform various aspects of biomedical research and healthcare. The paper offers a cohesive articulation of challenges and potential solutions, which sets it apart from more narrowly focused contributions. The emphasis on the synergistic integration of LLMs with KGs and formal causal methods is a key strength.
*   **Significance:** If realized, the vision presented in this paper could have a significant impact on biomedicine. Automated causal knowledge discovery, personalized medicine based on individual causal drivers, and improved public health policies grounded in causal mechanisms are all potentially transformative applications. The paper effectively articulates the limitations of current LLMs in biomedicine and proposes a concrete path forward.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Vision:** The paper provides a well-structured and comprehensive overview of the challenges and opportunities associated with causal LLM agents in biomedicine.
    *   **Clear Articulation of Challenges:** The paper clearly identifies key technical and ethical challenges that need to be addressed.
    *   **Focus on Integration:** The emphasis on synergistically integrating LLMs with KGs, causal inference tools, and multimodal data is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Relevant Applications:** The paper highlights concrete applications that could benefit from causal LLM agents.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Conceptual rather than Empirical:** The paper is primarily a vision paper and does not present any new empirical results or demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed approach. The claims regarding the benefits of these agents are largely speculative.
    *   **Overly Optimistic:** Some of the applications described may be oversimplified. For example, personalized medicine is a complex field with many factors beyond simple causal relationships.
    *   **Lack of Detailed Implementation Plans:** While the paper outlines a vision, it does not provide detailed implementation plans or specific architectures for the proposed causal LLM agents. The technical details remain high-level.
    *   **Ethics and Safety Concerns:** While the paper touches upon safety concerns, the ethical implications of using causal LLM agents in biomedicine deserve more detailed discussion.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by providing a roadmap for future research on causal LLM agents in biomedicine. It could stimulate interdisciplinary collaborations between researchers in AI, biomedicine, and causal inference. The paper could also inform the development of new benchmarks and evaluation metrics for causal reasoning in LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable vision for causal LLM agents in biomedicine, outlining key challenges and potential applications. However, it is primarily a conceptual paper without any new empirical results. While the paper is well-written and provides a comprehensive overview of the field, its impact will depend on whether researchers can successfully address the challenges outlined and demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed approach. The score reflects the paper's significant potential, balanced against its lack of concrete implementation and the existence of related prior work. The vision presented has merit, and could galvanize research within the burgeoning field, but the lack of results and feasibility demonstrations prevents a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### T1: A Tool-Oriented Conversational Dataset for Multi-Turn Agentic Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16986v1)
- **Authors**: Amartya Chakraborty, Paresh Dashore, Nadia Bathaee, Anmol Jain, Anirban Das, Shi-Xiong Zhang, Sambit Sahu, Milind Naphade, Genta Indra Winata
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities as intelligent agents capable of solving complex problems. However, effective planning in scenarios involving dependencies between API or tool calls-particularly in multi-turn conversations-remains a significant challenge. To address this, we introduce T1, a tool-augmented, multi-domain, multi-turn conversational dataset specifically designed to capture and manage inter-tool dependencies across diverse domains. T1 enables rigorous evaluation of agents' ability to coordinate tool use across nine distinct domains (4 single domain and 5 multi-domain) with the help of an integrated caching mechanism for both short- and long-term memory, while supporting dynamic replanning-such as deciding whether to recompute or reuse cached results. Beyond facilitating research on tool use and planning, T1 also serves as a benchmark for evaluating the performance of open-source language models. We present results powered by T1-Agent, highlighting their ability to plan and reason in complex, tool-dependent scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, aiming for a rigorous assessment of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "T1," a new tool-oriented conversational dataset designed for multi-turn agentic planning. T1 focuses on evaluating how well Large Language Models (LLMs) can effectively plan and execute tool use, especially when there are dependencies between tool calls within a conversation. The dataset covers nine domains (four single, five multi-domain) and incorporates a caching mechanism to simulate short- and long-term memory and support dynamic replanning. The authors also present T1-AGENT, an agent designed to use the dataset, and evaluate its performance, highlighting the challenges of planning and reasoning in complex tool-dependent scenarios. The paper intends to address the current lack of comprehensive resources for training and evaluating multi-turn, multi-domain conversational agents and tool use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper tackles a relevant and important challenge in the field of conversational AI – effective tool use and planning in multi-turn dialogues. Existing datasets tend to focus on single-turn interactions, and T1's emphasis on inter-tool dependencies fills a significant gap.
*   **Comprehensive Dataset Design:** The multi-domain nature of the dataset, along with the inclusion of caching and dynamic replanning capabilities, adds complexity and realism, making it a valuable resource for training and evaluating agents.
*   **Evaluation Framework:** The inclusion of T1-AGENT provides a baseline and framework for evaluating other LLMs, which is beneficial for further research.
*   **Potential for Community Impact:** The authors intend to release the dataset and code publicly, which could foster further research and development in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** While the authors collect some data from Wikipedia, a significant portion of the dataset relies on synthetic data generation for entities and attributes. The use of LLMs to generate dialogues can introduce biases and limit the realism of conversations, especially the extent to which it resembles human-to-AI interaction. The paper provides limited discussion on the potential biases introduced by LLM-generated dialogues.
*   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The paper's evaluation focuses primarily on Llama models, which does not establish a comprehensive performance landscape across various LLM architectures. Evaluating a broader range of models would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Lack of In-Depth Analysis:** While the paper presents quantitative results, the analysis lacks a detailed qualitative examination of the types of errors made by the models. Deeper error analysis would give more insight into the specific challenges and limitations of LLMs in this context.
*   **Limited novelty:** The dataset generation and tool use is not new. The dataset mainly helps evaluate the performance of open source models in the same settings.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty lies in the creation of a multi-turn, multi-domain dataset *specifically designed* to test inter-tool dependency planning and caching abilities of LLM-based agents. While datasets exist for tool use and multi-turn dialogues separately, their combination and focused design are a notable contribution.

The significance is moderate.  It offers a practical resource for evaluating and improving LLMs in a specific area of conversational AI. However, the dependence on synthetic data and lack of more robust model evaluations somewhat limit its impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, a score of **6** is justified.

*   The paper makes a definite contribution by addressing a clear gap in existing resources.  It provides a targeted dataset and evaluation framework for multi-turn tool use in conversational AI.
*   However, the significant reliance on synthetic data, limitations in the scope of model evaluations, and the lack of qualitative error analysis hold back the paper's overall impact. While the T1 dataset could be a beneficial tool for researchers working on tool-augmented conversational agents, it still presents room for improvement for future research.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Dimple: Discrete Diffusion Multimodal Large Language Model with Parallel Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16990v1)
- **Authors**: Runpeng Yu, Xinyin Ma, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: In this work, we propose Dimple, the first Discrete Diffusion Multimodal Large Language Model (DMLLM). We observe that training with a purely discrete diffusion approach leads to significant training instability, suboptimal performance, and severe length bias issues. To address these challenges, we design a novel training paradigm that combines an initial autoregressive phase with a subsequent diffusion phase. This approach yields the Dimple-7B model, trained on the same dataset and using a similar training pipeline as LLaVA-NEXT. Dimple-7B ultimately surpasses LLaVA-NEXT in performance by 3.9%, demonstrating that DMLLM can achieve performance comparable to that of autoregressive models. To improve inference efficiency, we propose a decoding strategy termed confident decoding, which dynamically adjusts the number of tokens generated at each step, significantly reducing the number of generation iterations. In autoregressive models, the number of forward iterations during generation equals the response length. With confident decoding, however, the number of iterations needed by Dimple is even only $\frac{\text{response length}}{3}$. We also re-implement the prefilling technique in autoregressive models and demonstrate that it does not significantly impact performance on most benchmark evaluations, while offering a speedup of 1.5x to 7x. Additionally, we explore Dimple's capability to precisely control its response using structure priors. These priors enable structured responses in a manner distinct from instruction-based or chain-of-thought prompting, and allow fine-grained control over response format and length, which is difficult to achieve in autoregressive models. Overall, this work validates the feasibility and advantages of DMLLM and enhances its inference efficiency and controllability. Code and models are available at https://github.com/yu-rp/Dimple.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Dimple: Discrete Diffusion Multimodal Large Language Model with Parallel Decoding" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dimple, the first Discrete Diffusion Multimodal Large Language Model (DMLLM).  It addresses challenges encountered when training a pure discrete diffusion model for multimodal tasks, such as training instability, suboptimal performance, and length bias. The key innovation is a two-phase training approach: first, an autoregressive phase for vision-language alignment and instruction tuning, followed by a diffusion phase to enable parallel decoding. The paper also proposes "Confident Decoding" to improve inference efficiency by dynamically adjusting the number of decoded tokens per iteration and explores Structure Priors for controllability of response format and length. Experiments on MLLM benchmarks show that Dimple achieves performance comparable to autoregressive MLLMs like LLaVA-NEXT with similar training budgets and exhibiting unique response control behaviors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in combining discrete diffusion models with multimodal tasks, a previously unexplored area. The proposed two-phase training strategy (Autoregressive-then-Diffusion) is a practical workaround for the known instability and limitations of pure discrete diffusion training. Confident Decoding and Structure Priors further add to the practical contribution, but their individual novelty is more incremental. The reimplementation of prefilling for autoregressive models, while useful, does not represent a major contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating the feasibility of DMLLMs and opening up a new avenue for multimodal language modeling research. The performance comparison with LLaVA-NEXT is crucial as it shows that DMLLMs can be competitive with well-established autoregressive architectures. The advantages of DMLLMs, such as precise control over response structure, open new possibilities for applications needing specific output formats or reasoning trajectories.  The paper also presents improvements for inference, a major practical concern in diffusion models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a crucial gap in the field by exploring discrete diffusion in multimodal settings.
    *   Presents a practical two-phase training paradigm that tackles key limitations of pure diffusion approaches.
    *   Proposes a new decoding strategy, namely confident decoding to improve inference.
    *   Achieves performance comparable to existing autoregressive MLLMs.
    *   Demonstrates unique capabilities in response control using structure priors.
    *   Provides detailed analysis and ablation studies for various components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dimple's performance lags behind the state-of-the-art (SOTA) models with much larger training data due to limited data budget, even when using a stronger language model foundation (Qwen2.5). The performance comparison is more about proving feasibility rather than exceeding SOTA.
    *   The paper does not thoroughly explore the theoretical benefits of using diffusion models (e.g., parallel decoding). While mentioned, the speedups achieved with prefilling and confident decoding do not fully leverage the potential of parallel generation, and further optimization is needed.
    *   The structure prior work, although promising, requires more exploration in different tasks and applications.
    *   It's unclear how well the autoregressive phase in the training is optimizing for instruction following compared to the diffusion phase.
    *   The paper presents the use of prefilling without clearly stating the limitations such as loss in performance with DMLLM models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence the field by encouraging further research into DMLLMs, focusing on: 1) scaling up DMLLMs with larger datasets and model sizes, 2) developing more efficient and parallel inference techniques specifically designed for DMLLMs, and 3) exploring novel applications that leverage the controllability and flexibility of DMLLMs.  The training approach and techniques developed here may serve as a foundation for future DMLLM architectures.

**Rationale for Score:**

While the paper demonstrates a novel architecture and useful techniques, its limitations (specifically performance relative to larger models and unrealized potential for true parallel speed-up) prevent it from being considered truly exceptional. The work lays a good foundation and opens a promising research direction.  The novel Autoregressive-then-Diffusion training approach, alongside confident decoding, is significant.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
